# Somewhere Over the Horizon: where the two halves of a folded universe touch, and where the classical world comes from

**B. H. Wiseman**

*Linnet Labs, Sydney, Australia (independent researcher; no external funding)*

*gr-qc (primary); astro-ph.CO, hep-th (cross-list).*

*Companion to* Separate Ways and the Upside Down: the classical world and the field equations
from a CPT fold, and the neutrino line that would break it.

![](fig_companion_graphical_abstract.pdf){width=100%}

*Graphical abstract. Left: outside any horizon a point and its fold image are spacelike separated,
because reaching the antipode takes a conformal time of $\pi$ and the spacetime lasts $\pi$, so
both light cones fall short and the two sheets are silent. Centre: inside a hole possessing a past
the separation turns timelike, and the angular budget available to a causal curve,
$4\arcsin\sqrt{x}$ across the interior, first meets the half-turn the antipodal map bills at the
midpoint, so contact fills the inner half at any charge. Right: the same budget in $D$ dimensions
clears the bill with a factor of two to spare in four, equals it in five where no curve attains
it, and falls short above. No parameter enters either side of the comparison.*

## Abstract

A horizon surrounds every observer, and the mathematics sealing its far side seals a black hole.
The cosmology paper this accompanies takes CPT to hold of the universe, so the Big Bang has a far
side and Einstein's equations follow with nothing quantised. That fixes everything at our horizon
and one thing at a hole's: where the halves can touch.

Nothing is chosen at our horizon and a reciprocal law follows: the correlation across a reflected
separation over the direct one, times the same with the reflection applied again, is
one for every state, metric and involution. Its logarithm is odd, so all even multipoles vanish
exactly. The fold's map is the square root of the thermal transformation at the primitive period.
Its parity is the classical-quantum split, and its squeeze at a horizon is fixed by the
temperature. Three readings of it as one copy of our own fail: positivity, the first law,
the Hulse-Taylor pulsar.

They cannot touch outside a horizon, where the commutator vanishes, and can inside a hole with a
past. Reaching it needs half a turn of the sphere as well as a fall, and causality caps the
turning at $\pi/(D-3)$ per leg whatever the mass. Two legs give $2\pi$ against a bill of $\pi$,
so contact fills the inner half at any charge, and the quotient there carries closed causal
curves. Five dimensions give exactly $\pi$, never attained: only four lets the sheets touch.

A hole with a past takes matter from our side and antimatter from the mirror, and must be one the
fold maps to itself, forcing the map and a second exterior. In Schwarzschild-de Sitter only
the maximal hole qualifies, its horizon ten billion light years in radius; charge and spin widen
it to a branch ending at $a/M=1.10092$. Its whole interior is in contact. Only the transparent
seam absorbs no momentum, and it is the one adopted. It predicts zero ringdown damping-time
deviation, testable at twice present exposure.

## 1. Introduction

Every observer sits inside a horizon. Ours is cosmological: the expansion carries distant things
away faster than their light can return, and what lies beyond that surface is out of reach
permanently. Nothing is trapping anything, so the cause differs from a black hole's, while the
causal structure is the same one and so is the mathematics that describes it. The horizon is not
far away in the usual sense. It surrounds you.

The cosmology paper this accompanies takes CPT, the symmetry that swaps matter for antimatter and
reflects space and runs time backwards, to hold of the universe rather than only of the physics
inside it. The Big Bang then has a far side, and what is over there is our own universe with all
three reversed. Einstein's field equations come out of that symmetry, with the metric a
fixed background at every step and nothing gravitational ever promoted to an operator, so they
were never waiting on a quantum theory of gravity. The same picture caps the dark matter's mass
at 491.6 PeV, and the cap is derived rather than fitted.

One structural consequence comes before any result, because several of the results are instances of it. The cosmology paper's §2.1 splits every field into the average of
its two sheet readings and their difference,

$$\Phi_c=\frac{\Phi+\Theta\Phi}{2},\qquad \Phi_q=\Phi-\Theta\Phi,$$

which are the closed-time-path variables and obey $[\Phi_c,\Phi_c]=[\Phi_q,\Phi_q]=0$ and
$[\Phi_c,\Phi_q]=i\Delta$. Since $\Theta^2=1$, the average is exactly the fold-even part of the
field and the difference exactly the fold-odd part. The fold's parity is therefore the
classical-quantum split itself: what survives the quotient is the classical content, and what the
quotient discards is where the commutator lives.

Two of this paper's results are instances of that, and reading them together is the point of
saying it here. Section 3's silence is the statement that the cross-sheet commutator vanishes
outside a horizon, so the quantum variable has nothing to act on there and the classical world is
classical for a reason rather than by assumption. And the two modes that destabilise the maximal
hole of Section 6 are fold-odd, which is to say they are quantum-sector modes: they do not appear
in the classical geometry at all, which is why the fold removes them without anything being tuned.
A.8 then closes the loop the subtitle opens. The fold's map is a half-period thermal shift, so at
a horizon in equilibrium the cross-sheet correlator is the direct one shifted by $i\beta/2$, which
fixes the two-sheet squeeze at $\tanh r=e^{-\beta\omega/2}$ with nothing left to choose. So the
quantum half of the split has an amplitude, and that amplitude is a temperature.

The obvious objection to a mirror sheet is that nobody has ever seen one. This paper's first
result answers that objection with a calculation, and the calculation is short. The cosmology
paper's §2.1 builds its algebra on the premise that a point and its fold image are causally
disjoint. On the geometry we actually inhabit that premise is free: elliptic de Sitter space puts
every point spacelike to its own antipode, so the commutator between a field at a point and the
same field at the folded image vanishes identically. The two sheets are correlated everywhere and
can signal nowhere. The silence is a theorem.

![Why. de Sitter is conformal to a piece of the Einstein static cylinder, with round
three-spheres for spatial slices and a conformal time running over an interval of length $\pi$.
The antipodal map of the embedding is the antipodal map of that $S^3$ together with
$T\mapsto\pi-T$, and two points antipodal on a sphere are at geodesic distance $\pi$ whatever
their position, so the image of $p$ sits on the right-hand edge at every conformal time. The
horizontal axis is therefore distance along $S^3$ rather than the polar angle, because the
conformal square hides the angular part of the separation and that part is the whole argument.
Radial null rays are then lines at forty-five degrees, and both of $p$'s cones run out of
conformal time having covered less than half of the way round: the future one at $T=\pi$ and the
past one at $T=0$. The shortfall is $\pi-|\pi-2T_p|$, which is positive for every point of the
spacetime and reaches zero only at conformal infinity. Equivalently and with no picture,
$Z=X\cdot Y/\ell^2$ is one at coincidence and exceeds one for timelike separation, and
$Z(X,-X)=-1$. A map turning through only half as much of the sphere fails this.](fig_companion_silence.pdf)

If the seal is exact, the useful question is whether it breaks anywhere. There is exactly one kind
of place, and finding it takes one line. Carry the same construction to a black hole, where the
fold's map is the wedge reflection $J:(U,V)\mapsto(-U,-V)$, and

$$
dU = 2U,\qquad dV = 2V \qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad dU\,dV = 4UV,
$$

so the radial part of the separation between a point and its image turns timelike exactly when
$UV>0$. That region is the interior of the hole, and nothing else. Whether the full
four-dimensional separation follows is a second question, taken up below. The cross-sheet
commutator is identically zero outside a horizon and nonzero inside, and it changes over at the
horizon because $UV$ changes sign there, with nothing tuned to put it there. Operators that fail
to commute can signal, so if the two halves of a folded universe can touch anywhere at all, the
only place is inside a black hole, where no outside observer can ever look.

Contact does not begin at the horizon either, and how far in it begins is a pure number. The fold
pairs each point with its antipode, so reaching the mirror sheet's causal region means covering
half a turn of the transverse sphere as well as falling, and causality caps what a curve can
cover. Section 5 works the cap out. It reaches the inner half of the interior at every
charge, and reaches nothing outside it when the hole is uncharged, and the same calculation in $D$
dimensions gives four large spacetime dimensions and no others, with small ones a separate case that 5.2 works and that the count constrains rather
than forbids. Since the fold is a free involution, that region is also where the quotient carries
closed causal curves, so what is being located is the causality-violating core of the folded hole,
sealed behind a horizon and then behind half of what is left.

![Kruskal diagram of an eternal hole. The fold's wedge reflection is the point reflection through
the origin, so it swaps the two exteriors and swaps the two interiors. Under it the null
coordinates scale as $dU=2U$ and $dV=2V$, so the cross-sheet commutator goes as $UV$: it vanishes
identically in the exteriors, where a point and its image $\Theta x$ are spacelike separated, and
is nonzero in the shaded interior regions, where $y$ and $\Theta y$ are not. Contact does not open
at the horizon, because reaching the image's causal region also means covering half a turn of the
transverse sphere, and A.15 shows causality allows that only at $r\leq M$. The inner shaded band
is that region, and both boundaries are exact. The curve that achieves it passes through the
bifurcation surface at the centre, which is the only route the geometry allows.](fig_companion_kruskal.pdf)

The results below are of two kinds, kept apart throughout. The division is between what the fold determines and what it merely permits,
and it does not line up with the division between our own horizon and a black hole's. One of the
determined results is about black holes.

**What the fold determines at our own horizon.** There the fold has no free choices in it.
The reflection is fixed by the wedge-reflection theorems, and the transverse part is then the
antipodal parity their composition leaves behind, chosen by nothing. Out of that comes a
reciprocal law: writing $R$ for the ratio of the correlation between a point and a reflected point
to the correlation between that point and an unreflected one, $R$ times its own reflection is
exactly one, for every state, every metric on the horizon and every free involution. The proof is
one substitution. Its consequence is that $\log R$ is odd under the same reflection, which on a
round horizon exchanges an angle with its supplement and so puts every even multipole at zero
identically. The equality at ninety degrees is the law's own fixed point. Section 3 states it.

No temperature is put in by hand, and requiring a geometric object to be a thermal half-period
shift returns one. Be exact about which half of that is the content: the match is made against a
state whose period is already $2\pi/H$, so what it establishes is the half, that the fold's map is
the square root of the thermal transformation and not an addition to it, with primitivity picking
the fundamental period and no odd multiple of it. Three readings of the mirror sheet as a single copy
of our own space are then tested and each fails on stated grounds, one of them against the orbital
decay of the Hulse-Taylor binary pulsar, measured to three decimal places.

**What the fold determines at a black hole.** Where the two sheets can reach each other at all is
fixed by causality and by the antipodal map, with no seam, no coefficient and nothing adopted. It
is the inner half of the interior, at every charge, and in four spacetime dimensions and no
others. For an uncharged hole it is that and nowhere else; at charge the inner half is proved and
the exclusion beyond it is open, which 5.1 states and A.15 works. Section 5 has it.

**What the fold only permits.** Everything else at a black hole rests on something the theory does
not supply, and after Appendix A.11 that something is a premise rather than a number. A.10 now fixes
the transverse involution at every stationary vacuum horizon, so that is no longer the open item.
The seam coefficient it controls is fixed by no symmetry of the fold, because the fold's two sign flips cancel in
the corner term at every value of it, which closes a third attempt to derive it and closes it for
a better reason than the two the appendix withdraws. What the same action does supply is a
selection, on one stated premise. The seam conserves energy at every coefficient and conserves
momentum only at the transparent one, so exactly one member of the family is a topological
interface rather than a material one, and the paper adopts that member on that ground. Reject the premise and one sentence goes.

That single commitment is where the interesting objects come from. A hole formed by collapse has
no past singularity, no second exterior and no bifurcation surface, so none of this machinery
describes it. A hole possessing a past has all three. The fold therefore sorts black holes into
two classes, and the sorting is invisible from outside: a transparent seam predicts ordinary Kerr
behaviour either way, and the no-hair theorem guarantees that mass, charge and spin are all an
exterior observer gets. Two holes of the same mass and spin, one of each class, are identical to
every measurement made outside them.

Such a hole would do two things. It would take matter from our side and its
mirror, which is antimatter, from the other, and the two would meet in the one region both
exteriors can influence. They annihilate there, and whether they finish before the singularity is
a question whose answer does not depend on the hole's mass, from a stellar remnant to a quasar.
Nothing from it can ever reach an outside observer. It is no source of luminosity and no way
around the Eddington limit, and it should be quoted with that limit attached or not quoted at all.

And it would not grow any faster for having two sides. The opposite is the natural guess, and this paper made it twice before checking. Both
exteriors draw on the mass they share, so a doubled supply would multiply the exponent of the
growth law rather than its rate, and over the window in which JWST finds its overmassive early
black holes that is a factor of $10^5$ in final mass. Section 6.1 shows the factor is not
available, and the two arguments that remove it are a sign and a sum. The fold reverses time
orientation, so each sheet's infall deposits its mass on its own side of the seam and our exterior
never meets theirs. And where the contact does let matter across, fold invariance makes the two
streams equal and a crossing is a swap, so we keep a fraction of ours and receive the same
fraction of theirs and the total does not move. The mirror sheet could add to our hole only by
carrying negative energy on its own clock.

Whether any such hole exists is the hard part, and it is not the question it looks like. It looks
like a question about the primordial density field, since the class seems to need formation
through a throat rather than by collapse, which the primordial black hole literature simulates and
calls type II-B. But a type II-B hole evolves from regular data and has no past region for the mirror sheet to occupy, so counting them counts the wrong thing. What the fold asks is whether it
maps a hole to itself, and A.15 shows that single statement forces the map and hands over the
second exterior with it. It also counts the class. In Schwarzschild-de Sitter, the one exactly
solvable background with both a black-hole horizon and a cosmological one, no involution serves
both below the Nariai limit and one does at it. Charge and spin widen that point to a branch and
change nothing else, so the class is the boundary of the parameter space rather than its interior:
maximal holes, black-hole horizon coincident with cosmological, out to a spin $a/M=1.10092$ that
is the same number in every de Sitter background. Section 6 reports it.

That is a thin result, and the section stands on it. It is a uniqueness statement where a
tail estimate would have been a guess. Two other calculations, run for different reasons, pick out
the same configuration: the region where the sheets can touch grows with mass and fills the whole
interior exactly there, and the two modes that destroy such a configuration in the sector where
its instability lives are odd under the fold and do not descend to the quotient. That second one
reaches less far than it reads, and 6.2 says how much less: it holds inside a static patch, the
non-spherical sectors had no instability to remove, and the Euclidean negative mode survives every
isometry, so the semiclassical decay is untouched. And a hole is two-sided exactly when its
horizon is the cosmological horizon, which makes Sections 2 and 6 one structure seen at the two
ends of one parameter. Sections 2 and 3 are untouched in any case, because they never needed a
black hole.

That separation is deliberate and it is the shape of the paper. Sections 2, 3 and 5 hold the
forced results, which stand on geometry alone: the horizon law at our own horizon, and where a
point can reach its own image at a black hole's. Sections 4 and 6 are what the fold only permits.
Section 4 rests on a seam coefficient the theory does not supply, and Section 6 on one hypothesis
that A.15 reduces as far as it can be reduced and then counts.

Two sections sit outside that division and are named here.
Section 7 carries what the fold commits the matter sector to, and it is conditional in a third
way: on the production model and state selection imported from the cosmology paper, which is why
the decay scale it quotes is marked conditional wherever it appears. Section 8 takes up the one
measurement that looks as though it should decide whether the mirror sheet is a copy of our degrees
of freedom or a place carrying its own, and shows why it does not, for the same reason that makes
the silence above a theorem.

## 2. When the time directions lose coherence

Before any of that, the two sheets have to be two. Hartle and Hawking's wavefunction is
real-valued, and a real wavefunction contains both an expanding and a contracting WKB branch [1].
Inhomogeneous modes can decohere them, as in the work of Halliwell, Hawking and Kiefer [2-5]. Here
the two branches are compared by an explicit overlap instead. Two settings appear below and they
are not the same calculation: the closed-de Sitter throat, and the radiation-dominated bang the
cosmology paper adopts. Each carries its own notion of time, so a threshold quoted in one does not
transfer to the other, and the sections below say which is in use.

The closed-de Sitter calculation uses standard no-boundary branch states and does not require the
matter model of Boyle, Finn and Turok, whose cosmology the cosmology paper adopts. Identifying
those branches with the CPT-related copies is an additional step, and the next paragraphs separate
what supports it from what does not. The cosmology paper's own numbers do not depend on the
outcome, since that paper adopts its cosmology as a separate input and uses none of the patch
algebra.

What supports it is that one map does both jobs. In the embedding, $\alpha$ sends $X\mapsto-X$, so
it carries the right static patch $X_1>|X_0|$ to the left one and simultaneously sends
$X_0\mapsto-X_0$, hence $t\mapsto-t$, exchanging the contracting and expanding phases of
$a(t)=\cosh Ht/H$. Both exchanges are the same involution acting on two coordinates at once, so
the assignment of the right patch and the expanding branch to one sheet, and the left patch and
the contracting branch to the other, requires no second map and makes no choice twice.

One further thing follows, and one does not. An antiunitary relates the two patch algebras:
conjugation composed with a geometric map. What relates the two branch states is also conjugation:
§2.1 obtains them as complex conjugates because the Wheeler-DeWitt operator is real. Both are
conjugation, and $\alpha$ performs it. Its time reversal sends $\eta\to-\eta$, and the
environment's frequency function obeys $\Omega_n(-\eta)=\overline{\Omega_n(\eta)}$.

That identity is algebraically empty on its own and we do not offer it as evidence: every function
of the form (even $+\,i\,$odd)$/$even satisfies it. What is not empty is that $\Omega_n$ has that
form. Its real part carries the $\eta$ dependence only through an even denominator and its
imaginary part is odd, and the reason is narrower than the reality of the Wheeler-DeWitt operator,
which alone gives only the empty conjugate relation. A real equation together with a real datum at
$\eta=0$ produces the form: $\Omega_n(0)=(n^2-1)/n$ is real because of the no-boundary condition
at the throat, and $a(\eta)=1/(H\cos\eta)$ is even there. Given the form, the Gaussian
$\psi_n\propto\exp(-\Omega_n\chi^2/2)$ on one branch is the antilinear image of the other's.

That the no-boundary condition holds exactly is checkable, and it settles
what kind of object these states are: $\Omega_n(0)=(n^2-1)/n$ is real at every $n$ with the
Wronskian normalised to one, which is the Euclidean condition, and the Euclidean state on closed
de Sitter is Bunch-Davies. The two branch states are therefore the same Bunch-Davies state
conditioned on the two branches, and no state on any other space, and its restriction to a static
patch is KMS at $T=H/2\pi$ by Gibbons-Hawking, which A.6 already uses.

What is still not shown is the identification itself, and the obstruction is specific. The two
pairs are different partitions of the same manifold: the patch pair is $X_1>|X_0|$ and its image,
the branch pair is $X_0>0$ and $X_0<0$, and the right static patch meets both branches. One
involution exchanging the members of each does not make them one pair. Conditioning a state on a
WKB branch is also not the same operation as restricting it to a region, and we have now done the
calculation that was to settle it. It settles it in the negative, cleanly enough to stand as a result in its own right.

The right static patch is the causal diamond of a worldline and reaches past and future infinity:
solving $X_1^2=H^{-2}+X_0^2-(X_2^2+X_3^2+X_4^2)$, the patch condition $X_1>|X_0|$ bounds only the
transverse part, so the patch contains points at every global time. The branch label is
$\mathrm{sign}(\dot a)$, hence $\mathrm{sign}(X_0)$, and the patch straddles both signs in equal
measure. An operator localised in the patch therefore has support on both branches, and no element
of the patch algebra determines the branch label.

![One state, split two ways, and the two ways cross. Left: closed de Sitter in conformal
coordinates, global time $\eta$ running up and the polar angle $\chi$ measured from the observer's
worldline. The right static patch is the diamond $\chi\le\pi/2-|\eta|$, which is a split by causal
position, while the branch label is the sign of $\dot a$ and so of $\eta$, a split by global time.
The branch line runs straight through the patch. Right: the patch's angular extent against global
time, with the two halves shaded. The conformal four-volume divides $0.500$ to $0.500$, and it is
exact, because the patch condition and the metric are both even in $\eta$, so
$\eta\to-\eta$ carries each half onto the other. All four cells are occupied, which is why an
operator localised in the patch has support on both branches and no element of the patch algebra
fixes the branch label. One involution exchanges the members of each pair, and that is not enough
to make the two pairs one pair.](fig_companion_splittings.pdf)

So the branch conditioning is not an operation on the patch algebra at all. It acts on the
gravitational wavefunction, whose split is by global time; the restriction acts on the matter
algebra, whose split is by causal position. The two do not fail to commute so much as fail to
compose, and the identification cannot be made this way.

What survives is exact and is what we claim. There is one state, the Bunch-Davies state, and it
carries two splittings: by causal position into the two patches, and by global time into the two
branches. $\alpha$ exchanges the members of each. The patch restriction is KMS at $T=H/2\pi$ and
the branch conditioning returns complex conjugates. Those are true statements about a single state
with two splittings that one involution relates, and the splittings cross rather than coincide.
The horizon results of §§2 and 3 use the first splitting, and the cosmology paper's results use
neither, taking their inputs from the adopted cosmology instead, so nothing below rests on an
identification we have just shown cannot be made. Nothing has to be asserted to match, because the contracting
branch's environment state is $\alpha$'s image of the expanding one: one structure appears in two
sectors, and no map from a region to a solution is required.

The limit is the setting. This is the free nonconformal environment of §2.1 on closed de Sitter,
and the check is that the antilinear lift acts as conjugation on that environment's frequency
function. It has not been carried to the interacting matter sector at a radiation bang, which is
where §2.1, and the cosmology paper's §3 numbers, live, and where the same argument would need the
corresponding mode functions. The step is therefore exhibited on the free de Sitter environment
and open at the bang, which is a smaller gap than the one it replaces and a specific one.

### 2.1 A closed-de Sitter overlap

Closed FRW minisuperspace with $\Lambda=3H^2$ has a classically allowed region beginning at the
no-boundary throat $aH=1$. For $N_f$ minimally coupled massless scalar fields, the exact mode
function gives

$$
\Omega_n=
\frac{n(n^2-1)-i\tan\eta\,\sec^2\eta}{\tan^2\eta+n^2},
\qquad
\operatorname{Im}\Omega_n=
-\frac{\dot a\,(aH)^2}{\dot a^2+n^2}.
$$

Both branches carry complex-conjugate Gaussian environment states. With $A\equiv aH$, their
overlap is

$$
\log|\langle E_-|E_+\rangle|
=-\frac{N_f}{4}\sum_{n\ge2}n^2
\log\!\left[
1+\frac{A^4(A^2-1)}{n^2(n^2-1)^2}
\right],
$$

with large-$A$ asymptote $-(\pi/12)N_fA^3$. Appendix A.7 derives the sum. Summed over all
harmonics, for one minimally coupled scalar, the overlap reaches $e^{-1}$ at $aH=1.95376$,
$Ht=1.28984$.

That is the ungauged number, and the fold does not leave it standing. The cosmology paper's
Appendix C.2 adopts Harlow and Numasawa's argument that a spacetime inversion cannot be a global
symmetry and must be gauged [6]. The fold has a linear involution available to gauge, and it is
not $\Theta$: $\Theta$ is antilinear and so is the modular conjugation, so their composition is
linear and implements $P_\perp$. Gauging it restricts physical states to its invariant sector.
Care is needed over which involution acts. $P_\perp$ is $\mathrm{diag}(+1,+1,-1,-1,-1)$ in the
embedding, so on a closed slice it sends $(X_1,X_2,X_3,X_4)\mapsto(X_1,-X_2,-X_3,-X_4)$: it fixes
one spatial direction and reverses three, and is not the antipodal map of $S^3$, which reverses
all four. It therefore does not multiply a harmonic level by a sign, it splits it. Degree-$k$
harmonics decompose under the $SO(3)$ acting on $(X_2,X_3,X_4)$ into $l=0,\dots,k$, and $P_\perp$
acts on the $l$ multiplet as $(-1)^l$, so the invariant dimension at level $n=k+1$ is $\sum_{l\
\mathrm{even}}(2l+1)$ rather than $n^2$ or nothing. At $n=2$ the four harmonics split as one
invariant and three not. That is the correct one-particle decomposition and it is not the mode
count the overlap uses.

Restricting physical states removes no oscillator from the product. The branch states are Gaussian
in each mode coordinate, $\psi_\pm(q)\propto e^{-(a\pm ib)q^2/2}$, and a Gaussian in $q^2$ cannot
tell $+q$ from $-q$, so the projector $(1+U(P_\perp))/2$ acts on it as the identity whatever the
parity of the harmonic it belongs to. Each mode keeps its overlap factor $(1+b^2/a^2)^{-1/4}<1$
instead of having it replaced by one. Two quanta in an odd mode are invariant as well.

So the threshold is the ungauged one,

$$
aH=1.95376,\qquad Ht=1.28984,
$$

which is the figure quoted above recomputed independently from the displayed sum. The two agree to
five figures and we quote both, and pick neither silently.

![The overlap of the two branch states on closed de Sitter, against the scale factor in Hubble
units. The cosmology paper's 4.1 gives $\log|\langle E_-|E_+\rangle|$ as a sum over harmonics
weighted by $d_n$, and the whole question is which $d_n$. Counting every oscillator gives $n^2$
and the solid curve; counting only the $P_\perp$-invariant harmonics gives the sum of $2l+1$ over
even $l$, which at $n=2$ is one against four, and the dashed curve. Both reach $e^{-1}$, one at
$aH=1.95376$ and the other at $2.43912$, a threshold nineteen per cent later in proper time. This
subsection argues the solid one applies, because the branch states are Gaussian in each mode
coordinate and a Gaussian in $q^2$ cannot tell $+q$ from $-q$, so the projector acts as the
identity on every mode whatever the parity of its harmonic. The dashed reading is drawn for
comparison and is not the one adopted.](fig_companion_overlap.pdf)

Three countings of the harmonics are available and one of them is right. Deleting the odd-parity
oscillators moves the threshold to $aH=2.43912$ and $Ht=1.53984$, nineteen per cent later in
proper time, and that counting is not adopted: deleting them needs a restriction on the field
itself, where the states would be the natural place to look, and that is a stronger hypothesis
than the one the cosmology paper's C.2 adopts and one this paper does not derive. Keeping whole
levels of odd $n$ gives $2.59538$ instead, and that is the parity of the full antipodal map rather
than of $P_\perp$, which is not the map the fold quotients by. Nothing has to be
added back: gauging a discrete group introduces sectors classified by $H^1(M;\mathbb Z_2)$, and
the cosmology paper's A.3 establishes that this vanishes on the cover, so there is one bundle, no
twisted sector, and the invariant subspace is the whole physical space. The cosmology paper's A.3
records that vanishing as a negative, against a holonomy route to the stabilising rule; here it is
what makes the gauged answer clean. Recorded for whoever revisits the restricted counting: the
ratio of restricted to unrestricted exponent tends to one half at large $A$, and both ways of
dropping modes approach it, 0.4999 and 0.4998 at $A=40$, so that limit does not distinguish which
half has been dropped and cannot be used to choose between them.

No explicit $\hbar$ or $G$ appears in the exponent, and the background Hubble rate sets the time
scale. The threshold declares a measure of decoherence. It does not make time appear
discontinuously and it does not select a branch. Conformally coupled free fields have real widths
in this calculation and do not contribute to the separation. Both the environment and the chosen
overlap threshold move that separation.

The $RP^3$ harmonic restriction is a separate topology-dependent comparison, and it is easily run together with the comparison above. The cover does
not acquire a restriction merely because its fields carry an antilinear CPT relation; an
antilinear relation between two sheets restricts nothing on either. What restricts is the linear
involution inside it, once gauged, and that has nothing to do with the topology of the quotient.
One restriction comes of choosing a different spacetime, the other follows from a symmetry this
paper already commits to.


## 3. Horizon law, and three tests of what the mirror sheet is

One statement about our own horizon is settled before any question about the mirror sheet arises, and
it holds whichever answer that question gets. Let $B$ be the bifurcation surface of the horizon,
let $P_\perp$ be the free involution of $B$ the fold supplies, and let $W_B$ be the restriction of
the two-point function to $B$. Write

$$
R(x,y)=\frac{W_B(x,P_\perp y)}{W_B(x,y)}
$$

for the correlation across a reflected separation over the correlation across the direct one.
Replacing $y$ by $P_\perp y$ exchanges the numerator with the denominator, since $P_\perp^2=1$, so

$$
R(x,y)\,R(x,P_\perp y)=1
$$

identically. That substitution is the entire proof and nothing else enters it, so the law holds
for every state, every correlator, every metric on $B$ and every free involution. On a round $B$
with an isotropic state $P_\perp$ carries an angle to its supplement, so $\log R$ is odd about
ninety degrees, every even multipole of it vanishes identically, and the value at ninety degrees
is one, which is the law's own fixed point.

![The law and what it kills, for the conformal scalar on de Sitter. A.10's two kernels differ
only in the sign of the transverse term, $Z_{\alpha,J}=-(1-r^2)\cosh\delta t\mp r^2\cos\gamma$,
so their ratio is $(c-b\cos\gamma)/(c+b\cos\gamma)$ with $c>b>0$. Sending $\gamma$ to its
supplement swaps numerator and denominator, so $\log R$ is odd about ninety degrees, exactly, at
every radius and every boost; the two curves are one unboosted case and one boosted one. Right:
its Legendre coefficients. Since $P_\ell(-x)=(-1)^\ell P_\ell(x)$, every even multipole integrates an odd
function against an even one and vanishes. The even bars are drawn at the panel floor because
they are zero, the largest being $3\times10^{-17}$ against odd coefficients of order one. A
kernel without the antipodal symmetry, run as a control, puts its even
coefficients at $0.52$.](fig_companion_reciprocal.pdf)

Being true of everything, the law forbids nothing by itself, and A.10 says so in those terms
before saying what does carry the argument. Away from $B$ the boost separation has not yet dropped
out, and there, requiring the geometric image kernel to be a thermal half-period shift of the
Bunch-Davies kernel fixes the period: the mismatch is $4\times10^{-17}$ at $\beta=2\pi/H$ and
$0.115$ ten per cent away on either side, a factor $2.6\times10^{15}$ worse. No temperature was
put in anywhere. What that does and does not establish is §3.4's subject, A.10 has the derivation,
and A.12 collects how far the law reaches beyond the surface: its algebraic form on any algebra
with an involution, the relative entropy it supplies as a measure of a state falling short of its
own fold image, and what that measure says about §2.1's branch weights.

Section 2 asked when the two time directions come apart. The rest of this section asks whether
there are two of them at all. The cosmology paper's §2.1 left three readings of the fold's
antilinear map $\Theta$, each of which makes the mirror sheet a single copy of our own space: quotient
spacetime by $\Theta$, impose a reality condition on the quotient, or keep both sheets and pair
the contour legs. They are not equivalent. Three tests below separate them, and the three come
from unrelated parts of physics. The first is algebraic and asks which states the quotient admits;
the second is thermodynamic and tests the first law; the third is dynamical, against a
binary-pulsar measurement. Nothing connects the three arguments except the reading they select.

Each names the ansatz or response law it tests, and a failure constrains that choice, leaving
every other quotient untouched. The scope matters and is kept throughout. What it does not
diminish is that three independent lines of attack, from three different parts of physics, fall
the same way.

### 3.1 A constant image-state ansatz

The first test is positivity. For the de Sitter-invariant conformally coupled scalar ansatz

$$
W=\tfrac12 i\Delta+A\,G_{\rm sym}(Z)+B\,G(-Z),
$$

positivity requires $A\geq1$ and $A^2-B^2\geq1$. Hadamard normalisation at coincidence requires
$A=1$, and therefore $B=0$. A nonzero constant image amplitude cannot satisfy both conditions.
Positive image states outside the Hadamard class, including the alpha-vacuum family, are not
excluded by positivity alone [7]. The result also leaves open smooth, state-dependent
finite-frequency correlations outside this two-amplitude ansatz. Appendix A.8 gives the mode
covariance and the two inequalities.

### 3.2 Entropy-temperature pairing

The second test is thermodynamic. The proposed elliptic pairing $(T_H,\mathcal A/8G)$, where
$\mathcal A$ is the cover horizon area, fails the Smarr relation $M=2TS$ and the first law
$dM=T\,dS$. For example, in Schwarzschild units $G=M=1$, $T_H=1/(8\pi)$ and $\mathcal A=16\pi$:
the proposed pair gives $2TS=1/2$ and $T_Hd(\mathcal A/8G)=dM/2$. The two-copy pairing
$(T_H,\mathcal A/4G)$ and 't Hooft's pairing $(2T_H,\mathcal A/8G)$ both give $2TS=M$ and the
correct first law. The quotient two-point function used in the comparison has imaginary period
$\beta_H/2=2\pi R$ at the central worldline, half the Hawking period, so its even sector realises
't Hooft's $2T_H$ rather than the two-copy $T_H$. Away from the centre, all harmonics contribute
and no single temperature describes the response. What thermodynamics rejects is therefore the
stated temperature-entropy assignment. The quotient geometry survives it.

### 3.3 A complete reciprocal time-symmetric response

Thermodynamics rejected the pairing without touching the geometry, so the third test is dynamical
and looks at what the quotient does to radiation. The antipode reverses time orientation, and so
exchanges the retarded and advanced kernels. Within the affine family $\alpha G_{\rm
ret}+(1-\alpha)G_{\rm adv}$, invariance selects

$$
G_{\rm sym}=\frac{G_{\rm ret}+G_{\rm adv}}{2}.
$$

For the stationary problem considered here, reciprocal in the sense that the kernel is unchanged
when its two arguments are swapped, this kernel is Hermitian. With a complete steady-state cycle,
or vanishing all-history boundary terms, integration by parts gives zero total dissipative work.
A.9 derives that vanishing, and checks the Wheeler-Feynman exchange of the retarded and advanced
kernels under the antipode numerically alongside it. If this kernel is the binary's complete
effective gravitational response, with no independent homogeneous drive or environmental
contribution, the binary has no secular radiation damping. The observed orbital decay of PSR
B1913+16 excludes that response: Weisberg and Huang report an observed-to-GR ratio of
$0.9983\pm0.0016$ [8].

Quotient descent does not fix the complete response. An invariant homogeneous solution can change
a lifted field without changing its source equation. Nor does antipodal parity imply local time
parity: on $\mathbb R\times S^1$ with $A(t,\theta)=(-t,\theta+\pi)$, the homogeneous wave $h=\sin
t\cos\theta$ is A-even, while $\partial_t h=\cos t\cos\theta$ is even in time at fixed position. A
suitable local source can exchange work with this wave, with cancellation in the antipodal region.
This scalar counterexample defeats the symmetry inference; it does not compute a gravitational
self-force.

A twisted Green kernel is odd under the antipode in each argument and even under its simultaneous
action on both arguments. Its identity distribution is the twisted delta. There is no
contradiction of the form $\delta=-\delta$.

One observational test survives, and within its premises it is exact: a complete reciprocal
time-symmetric response has no secular dissipative work and fails the pulsar measurement. A more
general quotient response requires its own boundary and source problem. Friedman and Higuchi's
locally defined quotient theory makes the distinction between local physics and global
identification explicit [9].

### 3.4 What the horizon law does not predict, and what would break it

Everything in Sections 2 and 3 is exact and none of it predicts a measurement, and this section states that plainly. Section 5's contact result is forced too and
is not covered by what follows; it predicts no measurement either, but for a different reason,
which is that the region it describes is sealed behind a horizon.

Every cosmological observable is built from same-leg correlators, $W(x,y)$ or $W(x,x)$: galaxy
clustering, the microwave background spectra, detector noise, the stochastic gravitational-wave
background. The reciprocal law is a statement about the cross-leg kernel $W(x,P_\perp y)$, which
lives in the doubled Hilbert space. That object is an influence functional only if the mirror sheet
is a bath the observer is coupled to, and it is not, because we have access to one sheet. Two
attempts to find an observable anyway both fail, and for compatible reasons: the galaxy-clustering
identification compares the wrong object and is excluded outright if forced, and the discrepancy
selects no axis, so it cannot produce a number-count dipole at any amplitude.

So the horizon law is valuable because it is exact, forced and independent of the state.
Predicting is not what it does. It belongs in the family of Bisognano and Wichmann's duality
theorem [10] and of the thermofield-double structure of the eternal black hole [11]: results that
fix what a theory must look like without telling an experimenter what to do. The number to take from this paper is Section 4's ringdown null, which a running instrument is
testing. It is not a consequence of the reciprocal law either.

The family resemblance is not an identity, and the stronger claim was tried and dropped. Reading
$\Theta$ as the Tomita-Takesaki modular conjugation would need a local net of algebras, a wedge, a
Wightman vacuum and a modular flow implemented by boosts, and none of those is established here.
What is claimed is a structural theorem about modular-like structure on a specific region, and no
more.

That withholding can be made sharper than a list of missing ingredients, and the sharpening cuts
both ways. The three properties one would naturally check, that the involution squares to one,
that it carries the algebra onto its commutant, and that it fixes the state, do not between them
identify a modular conjugation. A finite standard pair shows it. Take the $2\times2$ matrices with
the Hilbert-Schmidt inner product, $M$ acting by left multiplication,
$\rho=\mathrm{diag}(0.8,0.2)$, $\Omega=\rho^{1/2}$, and $\Theta(X)=PX^{\dagger}P$ with
$P=\mathrm{diag}(1,-1)$. All three properties hold to machine zero, and the relation that actually
defines the conjugation, $S(A\Omega)=A^{\dagger}\Omega$ with $S=J\Delta^{1/2}$, fails by $1.7889$.
No choice of positive polar factor rescues it either, since $\Theta S$ has eigenvalues $-2$ and
$-\tfrac12$ and a positive operator has neither. The failure mode is a parity twist of $J$, which
is exactly what $\Theta=J\circ P_{\perp}$ is by construction.

The same pair carries the repair, and that is the more interesting half. $\Theta$ is the modular
conjugation of a *different* purification, $\Omega'=\rho^{1/2}P$, whose restriction to either
factor is the same $\rho$. Against $\Omega'$ the twisted conjugation has zero defect and the
untwisted one has $1.7889$: the two have exchanged roles. The global vector has moved, overlapping
the old one at $0.6$, and a cross-sheet correlation has changed sign from $+0.4$ to $-0.4$, while
neither sheet's own state has moved at all. A parity-twisted fold is therefore the modular
conjugation of a parity-twisted purification, indistinguishable from the ordinary one on either
sheet alone and differing only where the two sheets are correlated. That is this section's silence
stated in the other language.

Both statements are finite-dimensional. Neither is a continuum construction, neither supplies a
Hadamard field state on the patch, and neither removes anything this subsection says is missing.
What they do is replace "not established" with a specific thing to establish, and name the state
it would have to be established for.

Two things would break the horizon law. The first is the wedge-reflection theorems. A.10 takes $J$
to be fixed by them and $P_\perp$ to be whatever their composition leaves; if the fold's setting
does not meet their hypotheses, the transverse involution stops being forced and the whole
"nothing is chosen" claim goes with it. The second is the Hadamard condition, which several
results above assume and which the alpha-vacuum family does not satisfy, as 3.1 notes. Both are
carried in the open, and either one failing takes this half with it.

## 4. Minimal black-hole response

For black holes, the minimal implementation retains ordinary Kerr perturbation dynamics and the
absorbing horizon boundary condition. We add no seam interaction. At the order treated, it
therefore predicts no additional horizon reflectivity and no fold-induced shift of the Kerr
quasinormal spectrum.

The status of that boundary condition differs between two cases, and the difference matters below. Where the geometry is two-sided, which covers the cosmological horizon
and an eternal hole, A.11 *derives* it: a constant reflectivity puts an image term between
mirror-placed points that are spacelike separated, and no admissible state is singular there. A
hole formed by collapse has one exterior, so there are no mirror-placed points and that argument
does not run; for such a hole the absorbing condition is adopted, as it is throughout the ringdown
literature, rather than derived here. The prediction is the same in both cases and only its
standing changes, from a result to the standard choice. A.17 asks the question that identifying
the two singular branches invites, whether matter may transit the locus and make the Hawking
temperature dispensable, and finds the obstruction is the fold's own.

The boundary condition is a physical commitment of this implementation. Flux conservation and
covariance under the fold do not select it: the allowed seam matrices include a continuous range
of reflectivities. The remaining structure does not narrow that range: symmetry under the fold and
closure of the constraint algebra admit the whole family. Nor does topology. On the de Sitter
cover, which is simply connected with $H^1$ vanishing for every coefficient group, there are no
periods to quantise a coefficient against (the cosmology paper's A.3). That argument is about the
cover, which is the wrong space for a gauged spacetime involution, where sectors live on the
quotient; the conclusion survives the correction; the reasoning does not.

$P_\perp$ acts on the closed slice as $(X_1,X_2,X_3,X_4)\mapsto(X_1,-X_2,-X_3,-X_4)$, whose fixed
set is $X_2=X_3=X_4=0$, two points, so it is not free there even though it is free on $B$. Writing
$X_1=\cos\theta$ and the rest as $\sin\theta\,\hat n$, the map is $\hat n\mapsto-\hat n$ at fixed
$\theta$, so the quotient is the suspension of $\mathbb{RP}^2$. The suspension isomorphism then
gives $H^1=0$ there as well, which is what the argument needed. It also gives $H^2=\mathbb Z_2$,
which the degree-one argument never looked at; for the group $\mathbb Z_2$ itself $H^2(\mathbb
Z_2;U(1))=0$, so no discrete torsion arises from the group, and whether the space's $H^2$ does
anything here we leave open, asserting neither way; that argument belongs to $S^3\times\mathbb R$
and we do not carry it to a Kerr exterior.

So no symmetry or topological argument on the content already present fixes the coefficient, and
what would is either additional field content, of the kind a gauge holonomy or a compact scalar
would supply, or a condition of a different type. A.11 tries one of the second kind, Hadamard
regularity, which would settle the corner term's coefficient with no further content at all. It
does not survive. Both forms of that argument are withdrawn there and transparency is adopted, as
the rest of this section and the conclusions say. A positive reason for that choice does exist
and it is short. The scale-free seams form a one-parameter group under composition, and the
transparent value is its identity: it composes with any other seam and leaves that seam where it
was, and it is the only member that does. Adopting it therefore adds no boundary term to the
action rather than adding a particular one, which is what having no seam means as an operation on
the theory. A.11 has the composition law. Those are all internal routes, and an external
one fails too. What the failures leave behind is more useful than a list of closures, because two
of them are requirements that any proposed seam law must meet, ours or anyone's.

The first is ultraviolet. A dimensionless coefficient in the corner term forces a
frequency-independent reflectivity, and no physical interface reflects identically at arbitrarily
short wavelength. A.11 tried two routes to that conclusion and keeps neither: asking for finite
relative entropy against the transparent case is circular, because the reference it compares
against is the seam it selects; and the image-kernel argument that replaced it is empty, because
the image term and the singularity it forbids never occupy the same region. Transparency is
adopted there, and A.11 says what deriving it would take. Completing a seam action in the
ultraviolet comes before fixing its coefficient for any seam that reflects. For the corner term
the question does not arise, because nothing here reflects: within the corner term $r$ cannot
depend on frequency, and the transparent member $\kappa=1$, $t=1$ is the one this paper adopts.
A.11 records that the boundary condition is a commitment chosen from a continuous range that flux
conservation and covariance leave open, and that two attempts to close that range by regularity
have failed. The requirement applies undiminished to the reflective proposals, which carry a scale
the corner term does not and therefore still owe their own matching law. A.11 also records what a
seam carrying the horizon's own scale would commit to, since that is the one way out, and it is
not a free one.

The second is structural, and it says what a seam would have to be, where the usual argument says
what it cannot be. Iyer-Wald entropy survives at a bifurcation surface because the Noether charge
is built from the Killing field and its derivative: the field vanishes there while the
antisymmetrised derivative does not. A connection inherits that surviving piece through the second
term of its Lie derivative; a matter field does not, since its Lie derivative is $\xi^a\partial_a$
of the field and dies with $\xi$. Computed in Kruskal, the one-form's Lie derivative holds at
$-\kappa_{\rm sg}A_U(0)$ at every distance while the scalar's falls away as the surface is
approached. So for the minimally coupled scalar term examined here, that Lie-derivative
contribution vanishes at the bifurcation surface. It does not follow that any seam contributing to
horizon entropy must be connection-valued: a nonminimal coupling $f(\phi)R/16\pi G$ gives
$S=(4G)^{-1}\int_B f(\phi)\,dA$ through the curvature derivative in Wald's formula even when
$\mathcal L_\xi\phi=0$, as Iyer and Wald's dilaton example shows. The classification is withdrawn
and the statement is restricted to the action studied. It remains the reason the Chern-Simons
precedents, which are connection-built, look close and do not transfer. A dynamical matching law
for a general rotating-horizon seam remains unconstructed here, and the same requirement falls on
any proposal with nonzero reflection, which must supply that law before its waveform can be
compared with the data.

Two constructions inside the CPT-symmetric programme bear on this commitment. Tzanavaris, Boyle
and Turok's black mirror [12] identifies horizon points while keeping the exteriors distinct, a
third reading: neither the one-copy quotient tested in 3.1 to 3.3 nor the two-sheet structure used
here. Those tests target a quotient and do not bear on it. The difference that does is sharp. The
black mirror makes the horizon a locus of identification; the fold identifies no points anywhere,
and the fixed-point set of its map does not lie on the horizon. These are different boundary
conditions on the same exterior dynamics, so they differ in the seam matching law, the horizon
algebra and the entropy each supports. That is the law left unconstructed above. Constructing it
decides between them; null results both readings survive do not.

Amaro Seoane [13] derives a horizon reflectivity for that model equal to the generalised Boltzmann
factor, fixed by $T_H$. Its reach is bounded by one thing. The constant-horizon condition gives
$\omega=0$ rather than a computed complex quasinormal spectrum, and the inspiral signature is
spin-dependent, so no universal sign for a damping-time shift follows. A second bound was
arithmetic, and it was wrong. The generalised Boltzmann factor carries the co-rotation term,
$|R|=\exp[-|\omega-m\Omega_H|/2T_H]$, and dropping $m\Omega_H$ reverses the spin dependence. For
the fundamental $l=m=2$ mode, with the Berti-Cardoso-Will fit [14]
$M\omega=1.5251-1.1568(1-a)^{0.1292}$ this paper already uses, the amplitude reflectivity rises
with spin across the range that matters: $9.8\times10^{-3}$ at $a=0$, where $\Omega_H$ vanishes
and the two expressions agree, then $0.148$ at $a=0.7$ and $0.444$ at $a=0.9$, where the horizon
returns about a fifth of the incident energy, against the $10^{-6}$ of [13]. It is not
monotone all the way to extremality on this fit, which gives $0.662$ at $a=0.95$ and $0.372$ at
$a=0.99$, so the rise is stated for the interval computed and not extrapolated past it. Dropping
$m\Omega_H$ returns the $10^{-6}$ for the same mode.

![What the co-rotation term does. Solid, the generalised Boltzmann factor as written,
$|R|=\exp[-|\omega-m\Omega_H|/2T_H]$; dashed, the same expression with $m\Omega_H$ dropped.
Both use the Berti-Cardoso-Will fit [14] for the fundamental $l=m=2$ mode. The two agree only at
zero spin, where $\Omega_H$ vanishes, and thereafter run in opposite directions: $\omega$ and
$m\Omega_H$ both grow with spin and their difference shrinks, so the reflectivity rises rather
than falls. At $a/M=0.9$ the horizon returns about a fifth of the incident energy where the
dropped-term expression gives $10^{-6}$. The shaded band is what a damping-time excess
$\delta\hat\tau_{220}=0.14$ would need under the most generous model, in which all ringdown
damping is horizon absorption; the corrected curve enters it above $a/M\simeq0.87$. No exclusion follows either way, for the reason given below: a reflectivity magnitude is not a quasinormal
spectrum.](fig_companion_reflectivity.pdf)

No exclusion follows. Under the most generous damping model, in which all ringdown damping is
horizon absorption and returning $|R|^2$ lengthens the damping time to $\tau/(1-|R|^2)$,
reproducing $\delta\hat\tau_{220}=0.14$ needs $|R|=0.35$. A reflectivity fixed by $T_H$ supplies
that at $a\gtrsim0.9$ with no extra heat at all: the horizon it asks for is $0.77\,T_H$ at $a=0.9$
and $0.39\,T_H$ at $a=0.95$, cooler than Hawking, where the earlier figure had it several times
hotter. The earlier claim that a $T_H$-fixed reflectivity could not reach the reported excess was
an artefact of the dropped co-rotation term. What survives is narrower and cuts both ways: a
reflectivity magnitude is not a quasinormal-mode calculation, so nothing here either excludes the
black mirror or shows it accounts for the excess. The gain is that the two readings now differ
somewhere reachable. At low spin the absorbing horizon adopted here returns nothing and a black
mirror returns about $10^{-4}$, a gap no instrument will reach; at $a\gtrsim0.9$ they differ by a
fraction of order one. High-spin ringdown is therefore where the structural difference between
them becomes an observational one.

Appendix A.14 now supplies the quasinormal-mode calculation that sentence says is missing, and it
changes the arithmetic rather than confirming it. The linear response of the fundamental $\ell=2$
Schwarzschild mode to a horizon reflectivity is $M\,d\omega/d\mathcal
R_h=0.00735845-0.0646771\,i$, and since $\tau=-1/\mathrm{Im}\,\omega$ that is a fractional change
of $-0.727\,\mathcal R_h$ in the damping time against $+0.0197\,\mathcal R_h$ in the real
frequency. The damping answers thirty-seven times harder than the pitch, so a ringdown search for
a reflecting horizon should be pushed on the decay time.

Two things follow that a magnitude alone could not give. The damping response is linear in the
reflectivity and carries a sign, so a reflecting horizon can shorten a ringdown as readily as
lengthen one, and which it does turns on the phase of the reflection rather than its size. And the
absorption model used above, $\tau\to\tau/(1-|\mathcal R|^2)$, is quadratic and signless, so it is
wrong at leading order: at $|\mathcal R|=0.1$ the linear term is $7.2$ times the quadratic one and
at $0.01$ it is $73$. Reproducing $\delta\hat\tau_{220}=0.14$ therefore needs $|\mathcal
R_h|=0.19$ at linear order rather than the $0.35$ the quadratic model asks for, a factor of $1.8$
in reflectivity, which moves the horizon temperature the excess would imply.

Two limits on that, and the first is the one that matters. The calculation is Schwarzschild at
$\ell=2$ while the discussion above is at $a\gtrsim0.9$, where the coefficient will differ and is
not computed here; what carries over is the structure, that the leading term is linear and signed,
and not the number. And $\mathcal R_h$ is the reflectivity in the power-coefficient normalisation
A.14 states, which is not the same as the amplitude reflectivity quoted against unit waves in the
tortoise coordinate; the two differ by $e^{2\rho}$, and a response quoted without its convention
is not a number.

Betzios, Gaddam and Papadoulaki's near-horizon scattering model is a further concrete alternative
[15]. Its small corrections are compatible with present echo bounds, so null searches do not yet
distinguish the two implementations. Reflection alone also does not imply a resonance pole; a
prompt constant reflection coefficient is a counterexample.

LIGO-Virgo-KAGRA's GWTC-3 analysis reports a damping-time deviation
$\delta\hat\tau_{220}=0.14^{+0.11}_{-0.11}$ when multiplying posteriors and $0.13^{+0.21}_{-0.22}$
under hierarchical combination [16]. These are 90% credible intervals. The first excludes zero;
the second includes it. The analysis discusses positive bias and possible prior effects, and does
not claim a violation of GR. We do not convert the quoted interval width into a
one-standard-deviation error.

Stated the other way round, this is this paper's sharpest near-term commitment, left implicit by a null. Every gravitational-wave signature the fold
could produce, a horizon reflectivity, the $\ell\to\ell\pm1$ mixing of A.13, the
$\omega\to\omega^*$ spectral action of A.14, requires a nonzero seam weight, and this
implementation sets that weight to zero. So it predicts an ordinary Kerr ringdown: no echo, and a
damping-time deviation of exactly zero. Zero lies outside the posterior-multiplication interval
and inside the hierarchical one, so the prediction is neither excluded nor comfortable. Holding
the central value fixed, shrinking the interval far enough to separate the two cases needs roughly
twice the current exposure, which places the decision inside a single observing run. If the excess
firms up, the transparent seam adopted here is excluded and the reflecting branch set out above
takes its place, with the spin dependence that distinguishes them. If the ringdown settles onto
Kerr, the reflecting proposals are the ones in difficulty.

A robust intrinsic departure from the predicted Kerr response, after the relevant astrophysical
and waveform effects are accounted for, would challenge the adopted minimal implementation. It
would not identify reflection as the cause or prove a particular quotient geometry. Echo-like
propagation effects must likewise be distinguished from a new horizon reflection law.

Setting the seam weight to zero IS a choice within this action, and A.11 makes it explicitly,
having withdrawn the two arguments that would have removed the choice; the structure a nonzero
weight would carry is worked out separately anyway, because a seam action carrying a scale is not
excluded and its reader is owed the shape a reflecting implementation would have. A.10, A.13 and
A.14 do that in three steps. A.10 asks what the kernel ratio of A.6's worked instance becomes at a
general bifurcate Killing horizon, and how much of it survives without de Sitter's maximal
symmetry. A.13 asks what a latitude-dependent horizon response does to the multipoles. For a
*scalar* field that is an $\ell\to\ell\pm2$ mixing at fixed $m$, forced by the evenness of the
transverse parity and switched on by spin alone; it does not carry over to gravitational
perturbations, whose spin-weighted harmonics admit $\ell\to\ell\pm1$. A.14 asks what the
antilinear lift's action $\omega\to\omega^*$ requires of a reflecting horizon's spectrum: for a
constant reflectivity, a complex value breaks the $\omega\to-\omega^*$ mirror symmetry and a real
value preserves it. None of the three supplies an amplitude; the matching law above would have to
do that. They constrain the shape a reflecting implementation must have, and the minimal
implementation adopted here sets all three to zero.

## 5. Where the two sheets can touch

Section 4 adopted a transparent seam and predicted an ordinary Kerr ringdown. That is what the
fold does at a horizon it is given. This section asks the prior question: where in a spacetime can
a point influence its own fold image at all? That one has an exact answer, it needs no seam
coefficient, and nothing in it was adopted.

Section 1 separated two readings of "touch", and this is where the difference bites. We mean the
diagonal: $x$ causally connected to $\Theta x$, which is what a local cross-sheet term
$\int\!\phi(x)\phi(\Theta x)$ would need. A.15 has the derivation, the charged and higher
dimensional cases, and the limits; what follows is the result and what it means.

### 5.1 Condition, and the answer

Outside a horizon, nothing. A point and its image are spacelike separated at every pair, which is
A.11's theorem and the reason the silence of Section 1 is a theorem.

Inside one, the question is not settled by the radial separation alone. Under the wedge reflection
the Kruskal coordinates scale as $dU=2U$ and $dV=2V$, so the radial part of the separation turns
timelike wherever $UV>0$, which is the interior. But the fold's transverse map is the antipode: it
sends the unit vector $\hat n$ to $-\hat n$, so the angular separation between a point and its
image is exactly $\pi$ in every dimension, and a connecting curve has to cover that as well as
fall. Causality caps what it can cover. From $ds^2\leq0$,

$$
r^2\,d\Omega^2\;\leq\;F(r)\,(dT^2-dX^2),
$$

so the angular path of any causal curve is at most its length in the conformally rescaled
two-dimensional metric $g^2(-dT^2+dX^2)$ with $g=\sqrt F/r$. The connecting curve gets two
interior legs, one in the white-hole interior and one in the black-hole interior, and no more.
Working the bound out gives

$$
\Delta\phi_{\max}(r)=2\pi-4\arcsin\sqrt{\frac{r}{2M}},
$$

which reaches $\pi$ at $r=M$. So the two sheets can reach each other in the inner half of a
Schwarzschild interior and nowhere else in the universe.

Put in words, with the arcsines set aside: the antipodal map bills half a revolution, the fold's
own symmetry splits that evenly between the two legs, so each leg must supply a quarter
revolution, and a quarter revolution is exactly what a causal curve can just manage starting from
half the horizon radius. The bound is attained and not merely approached, by a null geodesic
sitting at $X\equiv0$ and spiralling, which is what makes the statement an equivalence rather than
half of one.

Charge says which half of that sentence is robust. A charged hole has two horizons, and the same
calculation gives $r\leq(r_++r_-)/2$, at every charge, always exactly half way across the interior
band. **The result is about the inner half of the interior; half the horizon radius is the
uncharged special case.**

The half that is not robust is the exclusion. Reaching the inner half is proved at every charge,
by an explicit $E=0$ null geodesic that attains $\pi$ there, but ruling out everything outside it
needs the angle-maximising curve to sit at $X\equiv0$, and A.15 shows the condition for that,
$C(r)=r(2\kappa-f')+2f<0$, holds throughout a Schwarzschild interior and fails at every nonzero
charge in a shell outside the Cauchy horizon, with no threshold below which it is safe. So at
charge the inner half is a floor on the contact region rather than the whole of it, and whether
the region reaches into $(M,r_+)$ is open. The gap runs the forgiving way, since a maximiser that
leaves $X\equiv0$ delivers more angle and can only enlarge the region, so nothing claimed here
shrinks if it is closed against us.

![Left, the contact condition once the interior is measured in its own units. One leg from
radius $r$ out to the horizon integrates to $2\arcsin\sqrt{(r_+-r)/(r_+-r_-)}$, so with $x$ for
that depth fraction the two-leg budget is $4\arcsin\sqrt{x}$ and the charge has gone entirely:
one curve, not a family. It crosses the bill of $\pi$ at $x=1/2$ because
$\sqrt{x}\geq\sin(\pi/4)$ is the condition, which is why the answer is the inner half of the
band at every charge and not approximately so. Right, the same result stated two ways. As a
fraction of the band it is one half and does not move; as a fraction of the horizon area it is a
quarter at zero charge and better than three quarters near extremality. Anything thermodynamic
would have to survive charge, and the quarter does not, so it is a coincidence of $r_-=0$.](fig_companion_contact.pdf) A.15 traces both to one algebraic fact about $r^2|f|$ being a quadratic
whose roots bound the region, and shows what happens when it is not: a positive cosmological
constant pushes the boundary outward, reaching the whole interior at the Nariai point. Hold on to
that endpoint. Section 6 arrives at it from an unrelated direction.

### 5.2 Why four dimensions

Run the same calculation in $D$ dimensions and it selects the dimension, which is the one place
the fold says anything about how many there are. A Schwarzschild interior supplies exactly
$\pi/(D-3)$ of turning to any causal curve crossing it, horizon to singularity, whatever the mass.
The bill is $\pi$ and does not move with the dimension, because it is fixed by the antipodal map
being a free involution of a sphere. Two legs give $2\pi/(D-3)$ against it:

| $D$ | budget | where the sheets can touch |
|---|---|---|
| 4 | $2\pi$ | the inner half of the interior |
| 5 | $\pi$, approached and never reached | nowhere |
| $\geq6$ | $\leq2\pi/3$ | nowhere |

![The budget and the bill. The curves are the angular path available to a causal curve on the two
interior legs joining a point to its fold image, $(4/n)[\pi/2-\arcsin(r/r_h)^{n/2}]$ with $n=D-3$,
plotted against depth from the horizon at $r/r_h=1$ to the singularity at $0$. The flat line is
what the antipodal map asks for, $\pi$, which does not depend on the depth or the dimension because
it is fixed by the map being a free involution of a sphere. Contact happens where a curve lies
above the line. In four dimensions that is everything inside
the midpoint of the interior; in five the curve approaches the line without ever reaching it, and in
six and above it falls short at every depth. Four is the only case with a contact region.](fig_companion_dimension.pdf)

Four dimensions is the only one in which the two sheets touch at all, with a factor of two to
spare, which is why the contact region here is a region and not a point. Both sides of that
comparison are fixed by what the objects are: the bill by the antipode being an involution, the
budget by the falloff of $f$. In five the budget equals the bill exactly and a curve approaches it
without ever reaching it, so the contact set is empty there too; above five it falls short
outright.

That is a statement about large dimensions, since it runs on Tangherlini holes. If the extra
dimensions are instead compactified at a scale $R$, the budget depends on the hole. For $r_h\gg R$
the hole is a uniform black string, and that product is not an approximation: a four-dimensional
Ricci-flat metric times a flat direction is Ricci-flat, so the string is an exact vacuum solution,
checked numerically to the finite-difference floor against a warped control four orders larger.
The compact directions then enter the causal bound only as $-dK^2$ under the square root, so
moving in them subtracts from the angular progress and never adds to it: the maximiser sits still
in $K$ and the budget is the four-dimensional $2\pi$ exactly.

For $r_h\ll R$ the hole does not see the compactification, it is Tangherlini in $D=4+n$, and its
budget is $2\pi/(n+1)$ against the same bill of $\pi$, which one compact dimension already reduces
to equality and two put out of reach. So small extra dimensions are not excluded by any of this.
What follows is that the fold can only reach inside holes large compared with them, and that its
contact switches off below that scale. Which solution describes the hole settles the crossover,
and that is the Gregory-Laflamme question [34]. A uniform string is linearly unstable once the
compact circumference is long compared with the horizon: for the neutral five-dimensional string
the threshold wavenumber is $k_{\rm GL}r_h\simeq0.876$ [35], so a periodic unstable mode fits once
the circumference passes $2\pi/0.876\simeq7.17$ horizon radii, which is $r_h/R\simeq0.876$.

That threshold locates a linear instability, and where the phase boundary sits is a separate
question. Gregory and Laflamme offer the fragmentation
picture as a conjecture, and Lehner and Pretorius, evolving the instability numerically [36], find
a cascade of necks and bulges rather than a clean handover to a localised hole at one radius. So
this section claims only what the numerics carry, and that is enough: a crossover
exists, it sits at $r_h/R$ of order one, and above it the fold has its four-dimensional budget
while below it the hole is genuinely higher-dimensional and the budget falls short. Where the
handover finishes is a question for that literature and nothing here turns on the answer. The
threshold quoted is theirs and we have not re-solved the eigenvalue problem. Whether the fold acts
on the compact factor is separate and would add to the bill rather than to the budget.

The same budget answers a different question at the other horizon. Beyond a cosmological horizon
$f=1-r^2/L^2$ is negative and the turning available is $\int_L^\infty dr/(r\sqrt{|f|})=\pi/2$ per
leg, so two legs supply exactly the $\pi$ the bill asks for and supply it only as $r\to\infty$. De
Sitter sits on the threshold, and it sits there in every dimension, since $f$ does not know $D$. At
finite radius the budget falls short by $L/r$, so a point in a $\Lambda$-dominated universe never
reaches its own image and the image stress vanishes identically there. Adding a mass changes that
answer. The horizon comes in to $r_c<L$, the run begins where $|f|$ is smallest, and the budget per leg becomes
$\pi/2+2M/L$, which opens contact at $r_*=L^2/2M$, a proper time $L\ln(L/2M)$ away. For holes
between $6.6\times10^{10}$ and ten solar masses that is $27.47$ to $50.08$ Hubble times in the
future, so nothing in the observable universe or its past is in contact. What remains there is not
zero but the de Sitter scale $1/16\pi^2L^2$, $2\times10^{-71}$ of the dark energy.

That places the argument in a small and old genre, and A.15 says where. Most entries assume
something about matter or observers. The closest relative assumes neither and is a century old:
Weyl's observation that the Maxwell action is the simplest integral invariant that exists and
exists only in four dimensions. His selection rests on the degree of a form and this one on a
causal budget, and we claim no more than that they are independent.

### 5.3 What the region is

A sharper reading of the same result needs nothing new. The fold pairs every point with a distinct
one, so it is a free involution, the quotient is a manifold, and on it $x$ and $\Theta x$ are the
same point. Then "a point can influence its own image" says there is a closed causal curve through
that point. What the inner half of the interior locates is the causality-violating core of the
folded hole: closed timelike curves strictly inside it, the closed null geodesic exactly on its
boundary, nothing outside, and the whole of it sealed behind a horizon and then behind half of
what is left.

The sign of that stress no longer rests on a single slot. The calibration was made by moving the
coupling off conformal at zero mass, and the same Hadamard coefficient carries $m^2$, so the test
available is a mass at conformal coupling where the coupling slot is empty. Summing the massive
conformally coupled modes on the same geometry, with the fold first checked against the
massless closed forms to $6\times10^{-4}$, the null-null component is negative at every mass and
close to proportional to $m^2$. So A.15's self-censoring reading holds for a massive field
whatever its coupling. The two slots do not diverge at the same rate as the caustic is
approached, so the proportionality to $-V_0$ calibrated on one is not a constant shared by both and
what transfers is the sign. Those rates are accounted for in A.19. Read in the distance to the caustic rather than in
$1+\cos\tau$, which is its square, the coupling slot goes as $\delta^{-4.010}$, exactly what
the caustic-order rule and two derivatives predict, and the mass slot as $\delta^{-2.037}$.
That is one power softer because a mass carries one power of the world function, and one more
because the conformal coupling is, on this geometry, the value at which the leading mass parts
cancel.

Two things travel with that and A.15 states both. First, the fold reverses time orientation, so
the quotient is not time-orientable and the loop closes with the transported arrow flipped, which
makes the phrase a convention that has to be argued for. That reversal is a theorem of the fold rather than a posit. The modular flow of a wedge
is the boost, whose generator gives $dX_0/ds=X_1$. Sampling four thousand points of the right
static patch and four thousand of its antipode returns a positive value at every point of the
first and a negative one at every point of the second, while a rotation generator returns no
sign and points outside both patches return both. Its two limits are checked in a finite-dimensional model where each modular operator
is built from $S(a\Psi)=a^\dagger\Psi$ rather than written down: the entropy is constant along the
flow to twelve figures, and $J$ commutes with the flow, so what runs the two patches oppositely is
$\Delta_{A'}=\Delta_A^{-1}$ and not anything $J$ does. Second, surfaces of constant $r$ are
spacelike inside, so this chronology horizon is spacelike rather than the null kind Hawking's
theorems produce, and those assume the orientability this quotient lacks.

A second place joins the sheets structurally, and A.16 identifies it as the singularity, since the
map exchanges the future and past branches of the curvature hyperbola. That appendix works out
what a matter transit there would require. That is a different sense of joining from the one this
section uses, and the two should not be run together.

There is also a reason to think the fold polices itself. The world function between a
point and its image vanishes linearly at the boundary, computed from the connecting geodesics in
A.15, and a simple zero of it makes the image term of a free field's two-point function diverge
there as an inverse power. If that survives a full calculation the stress tensor blows up on the
contact sphere and the fold censors its own closed curves the way a chronology horizon does. It is
tempting to read a power off that, and we did: $\sigma$ linear in the distance $s$ from the
contact sphere, the parametrix image term going as $\Delta^{1/2}/\sigma$, a stress tensor
quadratic in derivatives, therefore $s^{-3}$. That counting needs the Van Vleck factor
$\Delta^{1/2}$ to be finite on the contact sphere, and it is not. The fold's transverse factor is
the antipodal map on a round $S^2$, and the antipode is a conjugate point of every geodesic
leaving a point, because the whole one-parameter family of great circles refocuses there. On
Nariai this is explicit, $\Delta=\varphi^2/(\sin\varphi\sinh\varphi)$ with its first caustic at
$\varphi=\pi$; on the Schwarzschild contact orbit the first angular zero is at contact and not
before it. The image point does not sit near a caustic, it sits on one, so the parametrix is being
expanded about the one place the expansion does not hold.

So the power cannot be read off the parametrix, and the question stays open. A caustic is where
the approximation fails, and the exact two-point function has to take its place, which asks nothing of a mode sum because no Van Vleck factor appears in one. Two things had to be in hand before the sum could be
done, and both now are. The state is the first. It is not an image sum over a $\mathbb Z_2$
quotient, since the fold is antilinear and sends $(q,p)$ to $(Dq,-Dp)$, so each parity sector of
the quotient carries an identically zero symplectic form; what two exteriors related by an
antiunitary map carry is a two-sheet state, and A.8's propagation now fixes which one. At a
bifurcate Killing horizon it is the thermofield double at $\tanh r=e^{-\beta\omega/2}$, carrying
the transverse parity that separates $J\circ P_\perp$ from $J$, and that parity is the insertion
of $(-1)^{\ell}$ on the cross-sheet term. The insertion and the antipodal map are one operation.
The second thing is a geometry in which the sum closes, and A.18 supplies one.

A.18 uses the smallest geometry carrying the feature at issue, which is not Schwarzschild and is
not offered as it: null geodesics leaving a point spread over every great circle and refocus
together after exactly $\pi$ of transverse angle, the same one-parameter connecting family the
contact sphere has. A generic null pair in it returns the known power $-1$ to four figures, which
is the control that could have failed. The image pair returns $-3/2$, sharpening on that value as
the window closes on the caustic, and removing the antipodal parity by hand removes the divergence
outright. So the caustic steepens the divergence by half a power: the
image term goes as $s^{-3/2}$ and the stress tensor as $s^{-7/2}$, against the withdrawn count's
$s^{-3}$.

The self-censoring reading is back with a steeper exponent than the one it lost. The sign, which
is what decides whether the divergence closes the region or merely marks it, takes a second model,
and A.19 supplies one. Point-split against
the image kernel, the fold's own pullbacks assemble the energy density into the wave operator,
which annihilates the two-point function, so in A.18's geometry the density vanishes identically.
That zero belongs to the constant transverse radius: let the radius vary and there is nothing for
