the cancellation to be. Flat space is that case and is exact, its fold being the time reflection
composed with the spatial point reflection, and the image energy density there is
$(8r^2+6\xi^2)/4\pi^2D^3$ with $D=4r^2-\xi^2$, positive everywhere and diverging positively at
contact. The exponent is the generic $-3$ rather than A.18's $-7/2$, because a straight line is
the only geodesic joining a point to its image and there is no caustic.

So one model has the power and the other has the sign, and a black hole has both and is neither.
Each result is right in a geometry the paper is not about, which is the limit to carry away from
both. What would settle it on Schwarzschild is radial: A.19 carries the fold through
for any static spherically symmetric metric and finds the transverse sphere paying for the
angular part of the potential exactly, at every one of them, so no $\ell$ survives in the density
and what is left is a two-dimensional object in $r$ alone.

### 5.4 How far a point is from its own image

Section 5 has used the separation between a point and its own image only at its zero, and the whole field is computed here, since that is where any local effect of the fold would have to live. Write $s(x)$ for it: a scalar determined by the geometry and the fold with nothing chosen,
whose vanishing at $r=M$ is the contact result restated. Away from there it has
a profile. On the $t=0$ slice the geometry is the Einstein-Rosen bridge, the fold carries the
point at $(r,\theta)$ to the mirror point at $(r,\pi-\theta)$ at the same Killing time, and the
connecting curve must both cross the bridge and turn through $\pi$ on the sphere. The pairing is
of points and not of observers, so the field exists along any worldline through them, null ones
included. That is a geodesic on a surface of revolution, so Clairaut's relation applies and the
answer is a one-dimensional integral. In units $2M=1$ the separation is $\pi$ at the horizon,
$6.054$ at $r=2$, $8.519$ at $r=3$ and $23.80$ at $r=10$, monotone outward, with $|\nabla s|\to2$
far away because leaving the hole moves you away from your image twice over.

Two features follow, and the first carries a qualifier. On this slice
the floor is $\pi r_h$ exactly, half the horizon circumference, because the angular half-turn has
to be paid for and the sphere is smallest at the throat. That is a bound on *spacelike* separation
across the bridge and not on separation in general: 5.1's whole point is that inside $r=M$ a point
and its image are causally connected, so the separation there is null or timelike and the exterior
floor says nothing about it. The two statements are about different regions and different signs.
And the whole structure is switched on by mass. In empty de Sitter the same invariant is
$Z(x,\mathcal Ax)=-1$ at every point, so the separation is the same everywhere and its gradient
vanishes identically; there is no local fold structure in an empty universe at all.

How the separation reaches that floor takes one more line, and ties the field to something familiar. For a static observer the redshift factor is $\sqrt f$ with
$f=1-r_h/r$, so $f$ is the square of the local clock rate, and near the horizon

$$s-\pi r_h\;\longrightarrow\;2\sqrt2\,\coth\!\left(\frac{\pi}{2\sqrt2}\right)r_h\,f
=3.51656\,r_h\,f .$$

The clock is a reading of $f$ and not a restriction on it: $f$ is a property of the point, so the
law holds at every point whatever passes through it, and a null geodesic crossing $r$ meets the
same separation field a hovering mass does.

![How far a point is from its own fold image. Left:
the separation across the exterior on the $t=0$ slice, against the floor $\pi r_h$ that the angular half-turn requires and which nothing on that slice goes below. Far from the hole the curve
runs at slope two, because leaving the hole moves a point away from its image twice over. Right:
the approach to the floor divided by $f=1-r_h/r$, the square of the local clock rate, flattening
onto $2\sqrt2\coth(\pi/2\sqrt2)=3.5166$. The two integrals are redone in R for the figure and
reproduce the values quoted here to four decimals, which makes the figure a check on its own
numbers as well as a picture of them.](fig_companion_fold_reach.pdf)

The coefficient is a pure number with no mass in it, and it is a limit rather than a fit: the
residual against it falls linearly in $r/r_h-1$ over four decades, while a near-miss such as
$9\pi/8$ leaves a residual that does not go to zero at all. So the fold's reach above its floor
and the local clock rate are not two facts about the geometry. They are one quantity, and the
horizon is where both arrive: the clock stops exactly where the separation to one's image can fall
no further.

That is a geometric fact and not yet a force. For the field to act it has to enter an effective
action, and the only place it does is the image term of a two-point function, whose coefficient
A.8 drives to zero. It is worth seeing exactly what that zero covers, because it is both broader
and narrower than it first reads. Broader: the exclusion of a constant, unsmoothed image kernel is
not a de Sitter statement at all. Such a kernel is singular wherever a point is null-separated
from another point's *image*. The Hadamard condition is not a prohibition on singularities away
from coincidence, since it carries the ordinary lightcone singularity at $\sigma(x,y)=0$; what it
does not allow is a singularity at a pair that is not null-related in the first place, and the
image-null locus is full of such pairs, including spacelike ones. That argument is local and
survives away from maximal symmetry.

Narrower: nothing in that argument reaches a smooth image correlation, and A.8 now records why.
Squeeze a single smooth mode, $v=\cosh r\,u+\sinh r\,\bar u$, and the two-point function changes
by $2\sinh^2 r\,\mathrm{Re}[u(x)\overline{u(y)}]+\sinh 2r\,\mathrm{Re}[u(x)u(y)]$. If the mode is
fold-real, so that $u(\Theta y)=\overline{u(y)}$, the second term is an image correlation and
nothing was inserted to make it one. The Bogoliubov map is canonical, so positivity and the
commutator survive; the change is a finite sum of products of smooth mode functions, so the
short-distance singularity survives and the state stays Hadamard; and the local stress moves. So
positivity and Hadamard do not forbid the fold a local effect, and the silence of Section 3 does
not extend from commutators to the stress tensor on those grounds.

An exclusion is not what is absent here. A law that selects the state is. The squeeze parameter
and the choice of mode are free and the stress shift changes sign with the parameter, so nothing
here predicts an effect either. What can be said is that the argument for silence at this level
has been removed, and that no argument for an effect has replaced it.

## 6. Two classes of black hole, and whether the second one exists

Section 5 puts the contact region inside a hole possessing a past, and collapse does not make
such a hole. A star that falls in produces one exterior and one horizon, and the region
that would hold the mirror sheet's matter never exists. So the fold does not modify every black
hole. It sorts them into two classes: one-sided holes, which is everything we have ever observed,
and two-sided holes, which have both sides in place.

All of it rests on A.15's hypothesis (H), which that appendix reduces to a single statement: the
fold maps the hole to itself. That is a commitment, and a programme that replaces the interior with structure at the horizon denies exactly it. A hole with
no interior has no past region for a mirror sheet to occupy and no bifurcate Killing horizon for
an involution to fix, so the second class would be empty and Sections 5 and 6 would describe
nothing. Sections 2 and 3 are untouched either way, since they never needed a black hole. This
paper does not settle which picture is right and does not claim to. What it does is put the two on
the same fork: the minimal implementation predicts an ordinary Kerr ringdown with no echo and a
damping-time deviation of exactly zero, which is the observable where a horizon carrying structure
and a horizon with an interior behind it part company. Everything else follows, the second
exterior included, so the sorting is not a question about formation histories at all. A.15 then
counts how many holes satisfy it, and 6.2 reports the count.

Nothing in the rest of this section uses the depth, which Section 5 has already settled. It
matters here only for what it rules out: no local cross-sheet coupling can act anywhere an outside
observer could ever look.

The order here is consequences first and the count second, because the count is a short answer and
the consequences are what it is an answer about.

### 6.1 What a two-sided hole does

Everything here is worked at arbitrary mass. That is how the consequences come out, and the first
of them says nothing except that the mass does not matter. Section 6.2 then
finds that the one family in which the count can be done holds a single two-sided hole, and it is
the maximal one, where an accretion picture does not apply. Whether sub-maximal ones exist outside
that family is open. So read what follows as the structure's consequences. Nothing in it is known
to be there.

That leaves a question to answer, since the overmassive holes are not hypothetical. They exist, so a cosmology that is right has to be consistent with them.
This one is, and by having nothing to say: they are one-sided, one-sided holes in a folded
universe obey ordinary general relativity, and the fold modifies none of it. Consistency is not
explanation, and the two routes by which the fold might have explained them are both shut
and were shut by checking. The formation channel is withdrawn in 6.2 below, for reasons that are
about structure rather than about counting. And the other route, an enhanced primordial tail, is
closed in the cosmology paper: the linear fold map adds no connected three-point function to the
Gaussian free state, so the fold supplies no bispectrum to steepen a tail with. What the fold does
offer here is a constraint rather than a mechanism, and it runs from the data to the theory.

Such a hole is fed from both sheets. Since the fold reverses charge, our side supplies matter and
the mirror supplies antimatter, and the two streams meet inside. That meeting is a weaker
condition than the contact of A.15 and should not be confused with it. Both exteriors of a
two-sided hole share one interior, so infalling material from either arrives in the same region
and can meet anywhere in it; A.15's $r\leq M$ is the stronger statement that a point is causally
connected to its own fold image, which is what a cross-sheet interaction would need. The
annihilation below uses only the first. Whether they finish annihilating before the singularity
has an answer that does not depend on the mass, which is unusual for a black-hole calculation.
Eddington-limited accretion gives $\dot M\propto M$, the infall time from horizon to singularity
goes as $M$, and the interior volume as $M^3$, so the interior density falls as $1/M$, the
reaction rate falls as $1/M$, and the rate multiplied by the time is flat. The computed slope of
optical depth against mass is $9.6\times10^{-17}$ across ten decades, and breaking the volume
scaling on purpose moves it to $+0.50$, so the flatness belongs to the geometry and survives the
arithmetic being broken.

The optical depth is flat at roughly $46.7$, and that level is not robust: it rides on an
annihilation cross-section and an accretion efficiency, and moving either by a decade runs it from
single digits to the high hundreds. What survives the whole range is the conclusion rather than
the figure. The optical depth exceeds unity everywhere in the box, so the streams annihilate
before the singularity at any mass and any plausible cross-section, and the interior of such a
hole is radiation rather than baryons. So the independence is what to quote.

A hole fed from both sheets would also grow faster, and the change would land in the exponent
rather than the rate. Eddington-limited growth is exponential with e-folding time $t_{\rm S}$, so
a supply multiplier multiplies the e-fold count over a fixed window while leaving the rate alone.
Between $z=20$ and $z=7$, a window of $0.582$ Gyr in the Planck cosmology, a hole at radiative
efficiency $0.1$ and Salpeter time $50.1$ Myr gets $11.63$ e-folds one-sided; double the supply
and it gets $23.3$, ending $1.1\times10^5$ times heavier from the same seed. A factor that size,
sitting next to the overmassive holes the James Webb telescope has found at one to two orders
above the local relation, warrants checking rather than adopting, and it would be
natural to carry as a free parameter with observation left to bound it. The fold does not
deliver it. It forbids it twice, and both arguments are short enough to give here.

The first is the time orientation. Take a null shell falling in from our side at Kruskal
$V=V_0>0$. Ingoing Vaidya has $8\pi T_{vv}=2\dot m/r^2$ with every other component of the Einstein
tensor vanishing and Misner-Sharp mass $m(v)$ exactly, so a stream of positive energy is one whose
mass function rises toward the future, and the shell leaves $M+\delta$ at $V>V_0$ with $M$ behind
it. The fold sends that shell to $V=-V_0$ and reverses time orientation with it, so the mirror sheet's
future is our decreasing $V$ and their shell leaves $M+\delta$ at $V<-V_0$. Our exterior is the
whole of $U<0$, $V>0$, and never meets that region. Both sheets end at $M+\delta$, each from its
own infall. Turn the second shell round, so our exterior gains what they drop, and that needs
$\dot m<0$ in the mirror sheet's own advanced time and so a negative energy density on their clock; one
sign has to serve both sheets, so a fold-invariant state cannot have it both ways. The step from
"their infall is the image of ours" to a doubled exponent counts one shell twice.

That argument assumes the sheets are disjoint images glued along a bifurcation surface, which is
the one thing Section 5 spends its length denying, so it is not the end of the matter. Inside the
contact radius a point and its fold image are joined by a causal curve, and matter crossing there
reaches our hole without passing through our exterior. Let $p$ be the fraction that crosses. Here
the second argument bites, and it is only bookkeeping. Fold invariance makes the two sheets'
infall equal, and a crossing is a swap and not a duplication, so our hole receives $(1-p)$ of our
own stream and $p$ of theirs, which is $(1-p)F+pF=F$ for every $p$. The version that gains, in
which we keep all of our own inflow and add $p$ of theirs, turns $(F,F)$ into $((1+p)F,(1+p)F)$
and creates $2pF$ from nothing. The multiplier is one whatever the contact geometry turns out to
be, and the transmitted-fraction reading fails for the same reason the symmetric reading did: it
counts one stream twice.

![What a second supply would have given, and the ledger that removes it. Left: growth from a
$100\,M_\odot$ seed between $z=20$ and $z=7$, Eddington-limited at radiative efficiency $0.1$. A
supply multiplier does not add to the mass, it multiplies the exponent, so $11.6$ e-folds become
$23.3$ and the final hole is $10^5$ times heavier. That is the prize, and it is why the section
checked rather than adopted. Right: the two-channel ledger that forbids it. Fold invariance makes
the two sheets' infall equal, and a crossing inside the contact radius is a swap, so our hole
receives $(1-p)F$ of our own stream and $pF$ of theirs for a total of $F$ at every $p$. The
alternative, keeping our own and adding theirs, is the dashed line, and it creates $2pF$ from
nothing.](fig_companion_growth.pdf)

What, if anything, the mirror sheet does double is the next question,
because a whole extra exterior ought to show up somewhere. Section 3 rules out the mirror sheet being
a copy of our own space on three counts, so it is a place carrying its own content, and the total
content of the two-sheet geometry genuinely is twice what either sheet accounts for. It shows up
nowhere, and the reason is the same one every time. Our future horizon is $U=0$ and theirs is
$V=0$: two different null surfaces, each of area $16\pi M^2$. Their infall lands in their future
interior, which is our past interior: different events. Their radiation contributes at events we
do not occupy, which is why $N_{\rm eff}$ does not move. Nothing is shared except the bifurcation
surface, and a two-surface carries no energy. The doubling is real and it is invisible, and those
two facts are the same fact.

So accretion cannot tell a two-sided hole from a one-sided one, and it cannot do so for reasons
that survive every quantity this paper has left uncomputed. Section 4 reached the same verdict
through the other channel, where a transparent seam rings down as Kerr whatever the hole's class,
which is the null prediction stated there against GWTC-3. Both of the observational routes we can
construct return nothing. What the section keeps is the classification itself, which none of this
touches: the fold sorts holes into those with a past region and those without, and 6.2's one
derived member of the second class is the maximal hole. The little red dots are not evidence for
it, and after this they were never going to be.

A doubled exponent over this window is a factor of $10^5$, which is exactly the size of effect a
theory wants to be able to claim, and the two arguments that remove it are a sign and a sum.
Neither needed a measurement and neither will go away. Section 6.2 makes the point sharper still:
every member of the class this paper can derive is maximal, and far too large for any accretion
argument to touch, so even had the ledger come out the other way there would have been no object
to apply it to. The arithmetic stays in because it is short, because it is the first thing anyone tries unaided, and because recording flatly what a structure cannot do is worth more to
this paper than the effect would have been.

### 6.2 Counting, and why the obvious channel was the wrong question

None of the above has an object to describe unless the second class is populated. This subsection
counts it. The count is small, and the answer has a shape an abundance argument does not: it is a
uniqueness statement, and it is reached from the fold's own condition rather than from a rate.

Formation through a throat rather than by collapse has a literature and a name. Kopp, Hofmann and
Weller [17] showed that a large enough perturbation closes its overdense region off into what that
field calls a separate universe, and Uehara, Escrivà, Harada, Saito and Yoo [18,19] have since
simulated the collapse numerically and classified the outcome by whether the trapping horizon
bifurcates, which is their type II-B. Type II-B reads like the channel that populates the second
class, and it is not that channel, for two independent reasons, so nothing here counts it as a
mechanism.

The first reason is structural. A type II-B hole evolves from regular data on a spacelike slice, and
such a spacetime has no white-hole region. What the throat opens onto is reached to the future, an
inflating baby universe, and matter falls from our side into it rather than arriving from it. That
is not a second exterior feeding a shared interior, which is what 6.1 uses, and it is not the past
interior $P$ that Section 5's contact needs. It is wrong on the terminology too. A bifurcating
trapping horizon is an apparent horizon splitting into branches in a dynamical spacetime, while
A.10's reduction and A.15's boost gauge both need a bifurcate *Killing* horizon, with a Killing
field vanishing on a two-surface. Sharing the word is the whole of the resemblance.

The type II-B arithmetic is kept because it answers a different question correctly.
Normalised to the observed rate of ordinary primordial black hole formation, and separating the
two thresholds by the twenty per cent that arXiv:2401.06329's profiles imply, a Gaussian curvature
field leaves four type II-B holes in every hundred thousand, rising to $7\times10^{-4}$ at a
stretched-exponential index $p=1.5$, one per cent at an exponential tail $p=1$, and eleven per
cent at $p=0.5$. The threshold for type II-B sits above the threshold for ordinary formation, so
softening the tail raises both rates and the ratio gains far less than a rare-tail intuition
suggests: ordinary holes per type II-B one run from about five at $p=2$ to five hundred at
$p=0.5$. What none of those numbers measure is the population of this section.

What does populate it is a question about the fold and not about a tail. A.15 reduces the
requirement to one statement, that the fold maps the hole to itself, and shows that statement
carries the rest: the map is then forced, and the second exterior, the past interior and the
bifurcation surface arrive with it rather than having to be manufactured. No formation channel has
to supply a white-hole region, which is why no formation channel was ever going to settle this.

A.15 then counts the class, and the count is one. Schwarzschild-de Sitter is the one exactly
solvable background carrying a black-hole horizon and a cosmological one together, so it is where
the question can be settled rather than argued. Below the Nariai limit it admits no single
involution serving both horizons: the two wedge reflections compose to a timelike translation, so
identifying them closes timelike curves, and the surface gravities differ as well. At Nariai the
geometry is the product $\mathrm{dS}_2\times S^2$, both bifurcation surfaces sit in one
$\mathrm{dS}_2$ at $X_0=X_1=0$, and the single linear map $(X_0,X_1,X_2)\mapsto(-X_0,-X_1,X_2)$
fixes both while reversing time orientation and carrying our static patch to the other one.

So the second class has exactly one member in the uncharged family, and it is the largest hole
that family holds: $M=1/(3\sqrt\Lambda)$, about $2.2\times10^{22}$ solar masses at the observed
$\Lambda$, with a horizon ten billion light years in radius. Charge and spin both widen the point
to a branch without changing its character. In Reissner-Nordström-de Sitter the fold-invariant set
is the whole Nariai branch, from the uncharged case to the ultracold point at
$Q=1/(2\sqrt\Lambda)$ where all three horizons meet; the extremal branch is excluded because a
zero-surface-gravity horizon has no bifurcation surface for a wedge reflection to act on. Rotation
does the same. The transverse map is an exact isometry of Kerr-de Sitter at every spin and every
$\Lambda$, for the same reason it works in Kerr, and the merger of the outer pair runs from $a=0$
with $\Delta_r''<0$ along all of it, so the near-horizon radial factor is $\mathrm{dS}_2$ and
never $\mathrm{AdS}_2$. That branch ends where $\Delta_r$ acquires a triple root, where the
quartic factorises as $-(\Lambda/3)(r-r_0)^3(r+3r_0)$ and $\Lambda r_0^2=2\sqrt3-3$, giving

$$
\frac{a}{M}=\frac{3}{4\sqrt{2\sqrt3-3}}=1.1009173688,
$$

the same number in every de Sitter background and past the Kerr extremal bound, which a positive
$\Lambda$ permits.

The branch between those two ends is a single curve and can be written down. In units where
$r=M\rho$ and $a=M\alpha$, imposing $\Delta_r=\Delta_r'=0$ and eliminating $\Lambda$ leaves one
quartic in $\rho$ alone,

$$
\rho^4-3\rho^3+2\alpha^2\rho^2-\alpha^2\rho+\alpha^4=0,\qquad
9\Lambda M^2=\frac{27(\rho-\alpha^2)}{\rho^4},
$$

whose largest real root traces the class. At $\alpha=0$ it is $\rho^3(\rho-3)$, so $\rho=3$ and
$9\Lambda M^2=1$, which is Nariai; the root disappears at $\alpha=1.1009173688$, and the numerical
branch meets the closed form there to $7\times10^{-16}$. Solving it this way matters, because
hunting roots of $\Delta_r$ directly loses two thirds of its digits exactly where three of them
coincide, which is the end of the branch and the only place the endpoint can be read off.

![The fold-invariant class across spin. Each point is a Kerr-de Sitter hole whose black-hole and
cosmological horizons coincide, which is what a single involution serving both requires. At zero
spin the curve starts at Nariai, $9\Lambda M^2=1$, the one member A.15 finds in the uncharged
family. It ends at $a/M=3/(4\sqrt{2\sqrt3-3})=1.10092$, where the double root becomes triple, and
that ratio is the same in every de Sitter background because both $a/M$ and $9\Lambda M^2$ are
fixed numbers there. The shaded strip is past the Kerr extremal bound, which a positive
$\Lambda$ permits: the branch crosses $a/M=1$ at $9\Lambda M^2=1.3756$ and keeps going.](fig_companion_spinbranch.pdf) So the class is better described as
the boundary of the parameter space than as a point: maximal holes, black-hole horizon coincident
with cosmological, carrying charge and spin up to that value. Every member is still maximal. That
is not a population of objects inside a universe. It is a statement about a universe, and as a
population count it is thin.

Followed across the family rather than inspected at its endpoint, that stops looking like an
isolated accident. The fold is present throughout, because the cosmological horizon always has a
wedge reflection, and that is the map Section 2 uses at our own horizon. What changes with mass is
whether the same map also fixes the black hole, and A.15 shows it cannot while the two horizons
have distinct bifurcation surfaces. So a hole is two-sided exactly when its horizon is the
cosmological horizon, which happens at the maximum mass and nowhere else. The second class was
never a separate population. Sections 2 and 6 are looking at a single structure from its two ends,
and Section 5's contact radius climbing from half the horizon radius to all of it is the parameter
between them made visible.

Two of the earlier claims do not survive that. The falsifier those drafts advertised, a
measurement of the primordial curvature tail, was measuring the wrong quantity and is withdrawn.
Sections 2 and 3 are untouched because they never needed a black hole, and Section 5's contact
condition is untouched. What changes is that the object it describes is one configuration rather
than a class with an abundance.

![Two quantities that were computed for unrelated reasons, across the Schwarzschild-de Sitter
family from Schwarzschild at the left to Nariai at the right. In blue, the contact radius of
Section 5 over the black-hole horizon radius: it is exactly one half at $\Lambda=0$, which is
$r\leq M$, and climbs to one, so the region where a point can reach its own fold image grows until
it fills the interior. In green, the ratio of the two horizons' surface gravities, which decides
whether one involution can serve both and so whether the fold can fix the hole at all. The first
is a causality calculation about angular budgets; the second is a statement about horizon
structure. Neither was derived from the other and both reach one only at the endpoint.](fig_companion_nariai.pdf)

The second is that two other calculations, run for different reasons and neither looking for the
other, single out the same configuration. Section 5's contact radius, swept against $\Lambda$,
runs from half the horizon radius at $\Lambda=0$ to $0.991$ of it at $9\Lambda M^2=0.9999$, so the
one hole the fold can fix is also the one whose interior is entirely in contact, with no outer
shell left where the sheets cannot reach.

And the objection a maximal configuration invites, that it does not last, turns the same way,
though not as far as it first appears to. Unfolded Nariai is unstable: the sphere-radius
perturbation has exactly three modes and two of them run away, which is the known decay toward
Schwarzschild-de Sitter. Both runaways are odd under the fold map and neither descends to the
quotient, so the folded solution does not carry them. Section 9 sets out how narrow that is, and
it is narrow in three ways. The mode that does survive is bounded inside a static patch and
unbounded outside one. The non-spherical sectors had no instability to remove, their potentials
being nonnegative at every $\ell\geq1$. And the Euclidean negative mode is a constant on both
spheres of $S^2\times S^2$, invariant under every isometry, so the semiclassical decay is
untouched. Two runaway modes go, in one sector, inside one patch. That is a real result and it is not the same thing as making Nariai stable.

What is open now is whether anything outside this family qualifies, and whether the embedding
argument carries into the warped rotating near-horizon geometry. Neither is a question about a
tail.


## 7. Matter rule, neutrino masses and the conditional decay scale

Sections 2 to 6 concern what the fold does to geometry. This one concerns what it commits the
matter sector to, and it is here rather than in the cosmology paper because that paper needs only
the conclusions while the conditions they rest on take several pages. Nothing in Sections 2 to 6
depends on any of it.

We use the gravitational-production state and radiation history of Boyle, Finn and Turok [20,21].
In the adopted small-Weyl-coupling branch, matching the observed dark-matter abundance gives
$M_1=4.916\times10^8$ GeV. This number is conditional on that production model and state
selection.

Stabilising the sterile species with a $\mathbb Z_2$ is an additional matter-theory assumption.
The quotient admits a nontrivial sign bundle, but the bundle pulls back trivially to the simply
connected physical cover. We have not constructed a continuation from the Euclidean boundary data
to an interacting cover action and measure that enforce the same species rule. The phenomenology
therefore retains the stabilisation rule those authors impose.

When that rule is exact, the sterile particle cannot decay. Its forbidden Yukawa column makes the
light-neutrino mass matrix rank at most two in the stated seesaw approximation. The two measured
oscillation splittings then leave one light neutrino massless. For normal ordering and the
oscillation inputs used here, $\Sigma m_\nu=58.78\pm0.32$ meV, with the independent input errors
propagated at first order. Which ordering holds is specified separately; exact stabilisation alone
does not choose it.

![What the rule does to the spectrum. Left, the full range. When the rule is exact the sterile
partner's Yukawa column is forbidden, the light mass matrix has rank at most two, and one light
neutrino is exactly massless; the weakly broken version below lifts that zero to at most
$2.1\times10^{-55}$ eV for the example lifetime, fifty-three orders of magnitude below the
lighter of the two measured splittings. Right, the same spectrum on a linear scale in meV. With
$m_1$ at zero the two splittings fix the sum with nothing left to choose,
$\Sigma m_\nu=\sqrt{\Delta m^2_{21}}+\sqrt{\Delta m^2_{31}}=58.78\pm0.32$ meV for normal
ordering, drawn as the two masses laid end to end.](fig_companion_neutrino.pdf)

The double-beta interval in the cosmology paper's §3.3 spans the remaining unknown Majorana phase.
The fold does not select a point within that interval.

A concrete weakly broken implementation adds a small gauge-invariant Yukawa column
$-y_\alpha\overline L_\alpha\widetilde H N_{1R}+{\rm h.c.}$, with the other two sterile neutrinos
heavier and no other open $N_1$ decay channel. This is an additional matter interaction. The fold
does not imply it. Write $q=y^\dagger y$ and $H^0=(v+h+iG^0)/\sqrt2$, with $v=246.22$ GeV. At tree
level and $M_1\gg m_W,m_Z,m_h$ [22],

$$
\Gamma_0=\frac{qM_1}{8\pi},\qquad
{\rm Br}(h\nu):{\rm Br}(Z\nu):{\rm Br}(W\ell)=1:1:2,
$$

where the charged channel includes both charges. A pure Higgs-neutrino branching assignment is
therefore unavailable in this example. The same column adds $\delta m_\nu=-v^2yy^T/(2M_1)$.
Relative to the rank-two tree-level baseline, its smallest mass obeys

$$
m_{\min}\leq\|\delta m_\nu\|_2
=\frac{4\pi v^2\hbar}{M_1^2\tau},\qquad \tau=\hbar/\Gamma_0.
$$

Duran, Morrison and Profumo [23] computed the gamma-ray and neutrino emission from decaying
ultra-heavy sterile neutrinos across generalised CPT-symmetric cosmologies, and report a reach to
lifetimes near $10^{27}$ s for very-high-energy Cherenkov telescopes and $10^{30}$ s for neutrino
telescopes. The example lifetime used below is inside that second window, so the value is
testable. For that example $\tau=10^{28}$ s, $\|y\|\simeq1.8\times10^{-30}$ and the bound is about
$2.1\times10^{-55}$ eV, reached twice, once from each of the two definitions above and the same
number both ways. This bounds the added tree-level mass term; radiative masses of the baseline are
a separate question. With the adopted radiation history and only this new interaction, the
inverse-decay population and the decay depletion are negligible at the accuracy of the abundance
benchmark. Thus a long-lived decay need not spoil the abundance or the
effectively massless light-neutrino result, although exact stabilisation and a nonzero decay
cannot hold simultaneously.

For a cold parent at rest, the neutral hard channels have

$$
E_{\nu,b}=\frac{M_1^2-m_b^2}{2M_1}\simeq\frac{M_1}{2},\qquad b=h,Z.
$$

Borah, Das, Okada and Sarmah [24] reach the same kinematics from the other direction. They take a
heavy right-handed neutrino of $440$ PeV and show its decay reproduces the KM3NeT flux at the
observed $220$ PeV, reporting that the lifetime required for the best fit saturates existing
gamma-ray bounds. The mass differs from $M_1$ here by ten per cent. The logical direction is what
differs: they select the mass to fit the measured energy, whereas $M_1$ above is fixed by matching
the dark-matter abundance in the adopted production branch, with the half-mass energy following.
We claim no priority for the decay interpretation of that event, which is theirs. What the fold
adds is that the scale was not chosen to land there.

Both neutral hard channels, $h\nu$ and $Z\nu$, therefore give 245.8 PeV at the computational mass
benchmark, a physical scale of roughly 245 PeV. Electroweak radiation and boson decay produce a
continuum and change the endpoint weight at this energy [25]. Halo velocities, extragalactic
redshift, propagation and detector response must also be included to predict a measured spectrum.
Neither that spectrum nor an event rate is calculated here. Two consequences of the $1:1:2$ ratio separate, one closing a hope and the other opening a test.

Flavour cannot do the assigning that the cosmology paper's §3.2 falsifier needs. The channel ratio
is flavour-blind: all three channels carry the same $|y_\alpha|^2$, and the $1:1:2$ comes from
gauge and Higgs structure, with the Yukawa column playing no part, which stays free. Since the
oscillation-averaged transfer is linear, the Earth-side compositions the decay can reach are
exactly the convex hull of the images of pure-$e$, pure-$\mu$ and pure-$\tau$ sources, which is
the image of the whole source simplex. Every astrophysical source composition is itself a point of
that simplex, so the decay reproduces any of them by choice of Yukawa column, and pion-decay,
muon-damped, neutron-decay and equal-flavour sources are all reachable. An Earth-side flavour
measurement therefore cannot separate this component from an astrophysical population, and the
cosmology paper's §3.2 directional requirement stands undiminished.

What the ratio does fix is the split between the hard line and everything else, and that is a
discriminant. Of four decays one gives $h\nu$ and one $Z\nu$, both feeding the line at $M_1/2$,
while two give $W\ell$, whose charged lepton never reaches Earth and whose products land in the
continuum. The line-to-continuum normalisation is therefore set by tree-level structure and is
independent of the free Yukawa column, where an astrophysical population has no reason to hold a
fixed ratio between a line and its own continuum.

How visible that is can be settled without the fragmentation calculation, by asking how much the
answer depends on it. Write the endpoint continuum as $N_{\rm tot}(n+1)(1-x)^n$ with $x=2E/M_1$,
so the continuum inside a window $\delta$ below the endpoint is $N_{\rm tot}\delta^{\,n+1}$ while
the line contributes $0.5$ regardless. At the thirty per cent resolution of the cosmology paper's
§3.2 and a cascade multiplicity of $10^2$ to $10^3$, the line is a feature of order tens of per
cent of the endpoint continuum for a fall-off $n=3$ to $5$, and is swamped at $n=2$. Across a grid
spanning both unknowns it clears a fifth of the local continuum at $81$ per cent of the grid
points at that resolution and at $50$ per cent at fifty per cent resolution. Those are fractions
of points on a scan carrying no measure. They are neither probabilities nor a detector forecast.
The claim they support is only that the $1:1:2$ structure fixes the tree-level neutral-channel
yield at $0.5$ per decay; what fraction of that is observable at an endpoint needs electroweak
showering, propagation and detector response, none of which is done here. Visibility is real in
the tree-level sense and genuinely conditional on everything downstream of it.

The part that needs nothing is the lock. That $0.5$ is fixed by the $1:1:2$ tree-level ratio and
by nothing else: not the Yukawa column, not the lifetime, not the halo profile, not the
fragmentation. The prediction is therefore not a ratio but a constraint, that the line strength
and the decay rate are tied at a fixed fraction, two hard neutrinos per four decays. An
astrophysical population has no reason to tie a line-like feature to its own continuum
normalisation at any fixed value, which is what makes this a discriminant where flavour is not. It
is a population-level statement, since measuring a lock requires both the line and the continuum
it is locked to, so it belongs with the cosmology paper's §3.2 directional requirement, where the
single-event test is a different instrument. The two sit in the same regime and need the same
order of exposure, which is the useful part: a population that can place the events on the sky can
also weigh the line against its own continuum, and the two discriminants come together or not at
all. KM3NeT's broad reconstructed event-energy interval [26] is not evidence for this decay
assignment; no likelihood preference over an astrophysical population is claimed.

The distinction is experimentally useful. A spectrum and flux calculated for a specified lifetime
and flavour direction can be tested with neutrino and photon observations. A channel exclusion
would constrain that implementation, while exact stabilisation predicts no decay signal. Neither
observation would by itself establish or reject the entire two-copy geometry.



## 8. What the mirror sheet is, and why the radiation budget does not decide it

Everything so far concerns what the fold does, at a horizon and then to the matter sector. This
section concerns what the fold *is*. The obvious measurement does not reach it, for a reason followed below, and the answer left standing is sharper than either of the two names on
offer.

The fold keeps two sheets and relates them by an antilinear map. The question the
cosmology paper leaves open is whether the mirror sheet is a **copy**, a relabelling of the same
degrees of freedom seen under the involution, or a **place**, a second region carrying its own
stress-energy. Two results in this paper bear on it and neither is in that paper.

Section 3 removes the copy. All three readings that make the mirror sheet a single copy of our own
space fail, on positivity, on the first law, and against the orbital decay of a binary pulsar, so
the mirror sheet is not a relabelling. And A.8 says what kind of place it is, which turns out to be a
third option neither name covers. At a bifurcate horizon in equilibrium the two-sheet state is a
thermofield double: the mirror sheet carries its own algebra, which is the commutant of ours, and every
expectation in it is fixed by the state on our side. A place, then, and a purification rather than
an independent world. Whatever it holds is determined by what we hold, without being the same
degrees of freedom relabelled.

None of that changes the radiation arithmetic below, and the reason it does not is the point of
the rest of this section. The two readings disagree about how much radiation the universe
contains, and that quantity has been measured.

A copy contributes nothing to the expansion rate: the mirror sheet is the same content described
twice, so the effective relativistic count is the Standard Model's, $g_*=106.75$, which is what
the cosmology paper uses. A place contributes its own. Recombination tests that more cleanly than
the bang does, since the radiation budget there is measured directly as $N_{\rm eff}$, the
effective number of neutrino species. A mirror sheet carrying the same content adds its entire
radiation density while adding no photons we can see, and that is exactly the quantity $N_{\rm
eff}$ counts. Writing the radiation density as $\rho_{\rm rad}=\rho_\gamma(1+0.2271\,N_{\rm
eff})$, the Standard Model's $1.691\rho_\gamma$ is duplicated, which in $N_{\rm eff}$ units is an
addition of $7.45$:

$$
N_{\rm eff}^{\rm copy}=3.044,\qquad N_{\rm eff}^{\rm place}=10.49.
$$

Planck measures $N_{\rm eff}=2.99\pm0.17$. The copy reading sits $0.3$ standard deviations from
that; the place reading is excluded at forty-four.

![The arithmetic that looks decisive. Writing $\rho_{\rm rad}=\rho_\gamma(1+0.2271N_{\rm eff})$
with $0.2271=(7/8)(4/11)^{4/3}$, the Standard Model's $1.691\rho_\gamma$ duplicated is an addition
of $7.45$ in $N_{\rm eff}$ units, which is the whole Standard Model counted twice. Against
Planck's $2.99\pm0.17$ the copy reading sits a third of a standard deviation away and the place
reading forty-four. **The paper does not claim that exclusion.** Duplicating the density requires
the two sheets' stress-energy to add at the same events, and the fold puts them at different ones,
which is the same fact that makes the silence of Section 1 a theorem. Both readings therefore
predict the left-hand point and Planck does not separate them. The figure is here to show how
large a gap the argument would have carried had it applied, and how little that is worth without
co-location.](fig_companion_neff.pdf)

That arithmetic is correct and it does not apply here, which is the point of the rest of this
section. The step from "a place carries its own stress-energy" to "the radiation density at our
recombination is doubled" needs the two sheets' stress-energy to add *at the same events*. Nothing
in the fold puts them there. Everything else in this paper insists on the opposite: Section 1's
silence theorem, A.11's proof that one point in each wedge is always spacelike separated, and A.15
entirely. On de Sitter the map sends $X^0\mapsto-X^0$, so a point and its image are not even
contemporaneous. The Friedmann equation at our last-scattering surface is sourced by the stress
tensor at our events, and a sheet occupying other events contributes nothing to it whichever
reading is taken. So $N_{\rm eff}=3.044$ under both, and the forty-four standard deviations
exclude a co-located mirror sector, which is a model this paper does not propose. The caveat
travels with it: what is tested is silence at recombination, while the $g_*$ commitment is about
the bang.

An obvious caveat, that $N_{\rm eff}$ constrains recombination while the $g_*$ commitment is about
the bang, is about *when* and not about *whether*, and the second is the question that bites. Are
the sheets ever co-located at all? Geometry answers no, everywhere except the bang itself, which
is the one surface they share.

What survives runs the other way, and is worth more than the test. If the geometry forbids the
place reading from contributing locally, then the cosmology paper's $g_*=106.75$ follows from the fold instead of being chosen, and its dark-matter ceiling of $491.6$ PeV does not carry
the factor-of-two ambiguity that this section was written to resolve. That question dissolves
rather than being decided by data. That is a smaller claim than a passed measurement and a
defensible one.

Two things would put a real test back. An argument that the sheets are co-located at the bang in
the sense the production calculation needs, which would make $g_*$ the observable and the
abundance the measurement, and which we have not constructed. Or a mechanism by which the mirror sheet gravitates non-locally, which the fold as built supplies no route to. Until one of those
exists, this paper's empirical weight sits on Section 4's ringdown null, which a running
instrument is already testing.

The first of those two routes is the interesting one and it is where the surviving test lives, so it is followed one step here. The bang is the single surface the two sheets share, and $g_*$
enters the abundance calculation there rather than at recombination. So if co-location at the bang
can be argued, the ambiguity returns as a shift in the produced abundance and therefore in the
mass, and that shift is already quantified. It moves the dark-matter ceiling from $491.6$ to
$526.9$ PeV and the two-body neutrino line from $245.8$ to $263.4$ PeV, a fixed

$$
\frac{\Delta E_\nu}{E_\nu}=2^{1/10}-1=7.18\times10^{-2}
$$

of wherever the line sits. Quoting a shift like that in units of the propagated theory width, which is the natural thing to
do, describes the theory error and not what an experiment faces.
The limiting uncertainty on a cascade line position at these energies is the absolute energy
scale, which carries contributions from the inelasticity distribution at $x\sim10^{-6}$, the ice
absorption model, photomultiplier saturation and the shape of the background, and is a common-mode
systematic of order fifteen to twenty-five per cent. A common-mode systematic does not average
down with exposure, so the significance saturates below one standard deviation at any event count.
So the line test requires absolute calibration better than seven per cent to reach one standard
deviation at all, and better than two and a half per cent for three. No existing detector is
close, so the test should be quoted as deferred to a detector that is, and never as a sensitivity.
It is now doubly conditional, on that calibration and on the bang co-location argument nobody has
made, and we would rather say so than carry it as an asset.

## 9. Conclusions

A horizon surrounds every observer. Ours is cosmological rather than gravitational, and the
mathematics sealing its far side is the mathematics that seals a black hole. This paper asked what
a mirror-symmetric universe does at such a surface, and the answer divides sharply into what the
symmetry determines and what it merely permits.

At our own horizon nothing is chosen. The reflection is fixed by theorems and the transverse part
is the antipodal parity their composition leaves behind, and out of that comes a reciprocal law.
Take the correlation across a reflected separation over the correlation across the direct one,
then multiply by the same ratio with the reflection applied once more, and the answer is exactly
one. Every state, every metric on the surface, every free involution, one substitution to prove
it. Its logarithm is therefore odd about ninety degrees, so all even multipoles vanish identically, at every order. Nobody put a temperature into any of this and one comes out, and it is
worth being exact about which half of that is the content. The match is made against a state whose
period is $2\pi/H$ already, so what it can establish is that the fold's map is a HALF-period shift
and not a whole one, which makes it the square root of the thermal transformation rather than an
addition to it. That the square root is what a geometric involution has to be, and that
primitivity then picks the fundamental period rather than an odd multiple of it, is the part that
was not put in.

Three readings of the mirror sheet as a single copy of our own space were tested and all three fail,
by algebra, by thermodynamics, and against the orbital decay of a binary pulsar measured to three
decimal places. Three unrelated attacks falling the same way is worth more than any one of them.

Past that, one thing is not supplied and we say so. Carrying the fold to a black hole
requires a choice the theory does not make, and the coefficient it governs is adopted rather than
derived. Two arguments that would have derived it are withdrawn alongside the calculations
refuting them, and a third route is closed rather than merely failed: the seam action is
fold-invariant at every value of the coefficient, so no symmetry can pick one out. What does pick
one out is a premise about the seam rather than a symmetry of it. Energy crosses the seam freely
at every coefficient and momentum does so only at the transparent one, whose stress tensor is
continuous, so exactly one member of the family is a topological interface rather than a material
one. The adoption rests on that premise, which the fold does not supply, and rejecting it removes one sentence.

The next result needs none of that, and the two are independent, though they sit next to each other. Where the two halves of a folded universe can touch is settled by causality alone,
with no seam, no coefficient and no choice. Outside any horizon they cannot, because a point and
its image are spacelike separated and the commutator vanishes; the silence is a theorem. Inside a
hole possessing a past the product of the null coordinates changes sign and the radial separation
turns timelike, but that much is two-dimensional and too generous. The fold's transverse map is
antipodal, so contact needs half a turn of the sphere as well as a fall, and causality caps the
angular path of any causal curve at $2\pi-4\arcsin\sqrt{r/2M}$. That reaches $\pi$ at the midpoint
of the interior, which for an uncharged hole is $r=M$ and for a charged one is $(r_++r_-)/2$, the
same fraction at every charge. So the sheets touch in the inner half of the interior, and nowhere
else when the hole is uncharged, the mass cancels, and the one curve that does it never leaves the interior: it touches the
horizon once, at the bifurcation surface, which at the outer edge of the region is the only route
there is. Since the fold is a free involution, that region is where the quotient carries closed
causal curves, so what the calculation locates is the folded hole's causality-violating core.

The same calculation run in $D$ dimensions selects the dimension. A Schwarzschild interior
supplies exactly $\pi/(D-3)$ of turning to any causal curve crossing it, whatever the mass, and
the connecting curve gets two legs of it. The antipodal map bills $\pi$. The budget covers the
bill at $2\pi$ in four dimensions. In five it equals it, which a curve approaches and never
attains, so the contact set is already empty there; above five it falls short outright. Four large
dimensions is the only case in which the two sheets reach each other at all. Nothing was tuned to
arrange that. The bill is fixed by the antipode being an involution of a sphere and the budget by
$f$ having a simple zero and falling as $r^{-(D-3)}$, and the two meet in the dimension we are in,
with a factor of two to spare. Everything this paper says about a black hole is empty one
dimension up and two. Small extra dimensions are a separate case and Section 5.2 works it: above
the compactification scale the budget is the four-dimensional one and nothing changes, below it
the hole is genuinely higher-dimensional and the contact set is empty, so what the count
constrains is the size of hole the fold can reach inside rather than the existence of the
dimensions. Where the cosmology paper derives a ceiling on a mass, this derives a depth, and
neither has anything fitted in it.

Whether the fold then closes the core it locates is a further question, and two counts of it
disagree. A parametrix count gives the region a divergent stress tensor at $s^{-3}$, and that
count does not apply here: the contact sphere is a caustic, where the Van Vleck factor the count
needs is infinite. A mode sum needs no such factor, and in a model geometry carrying the same
degeneracy it puts the divergence back half a power steeper, at $s^{-7/2}$. The state summed over
is the one the propagation had already fixed, so nothing was added to reach that. The sign, which is what
decides whether the divergence closes the region or merely marks it, comes from a second model in
which the whole calculation is closed form: the image energy density there is positive everywhere
and diverges positively at contact. That model has no caustic, so it has the sign and not the
power, while the one that has the power has no sign. Neither is a black hole, and what would settle it there is
now known to be radial: the transverse sphere supplies the angular part of the potential at every
static spherically symmetric metric, so the sphere and its caustic drop out of the sign entirely.

Such a hole would swallow matter from our side and antimatter from the mirror, and whether they
finish annihilating before the singularity has a mass-independent answer, which is an odd thing
for a black-hole calculation to have. What it would not do is grow any faster than a one-sided
hole, which 6.1 settles rather than assumes: the fold's own time reversal puts each sheet's infall
on its own side of the seam, and where the contact lets matter across, a swap of equal streams
leaves both totals where they were. A doubled exponent over the window the overmassive early holes
occupy would have been the difference between twelve e-folds and twenty-three, a factor of $10^5$,
and it is not available.

Whether the universe contains one is where this half of the paper stops. The natural way to ask
it is as a question about the tail of the primordial density field, counting holes that form
through a throat, and that count answers the wrong
question: such a hole evolves from regular data and has no past region for the mirror sheet to be,
and the bifurcating trapping horizon its literature describes is an apparent horizon splitting in
a dynamical spacetime, not the bifurcate Killing horizon the fold needs. What the fold actually
asks is whether it maps a hole to itself, and A.15 shows that one statement carries everything
else, the second exterior included.

It also counts the class. Schwarzschild-de Sitter is the one exactly solvable background holding a
black-hole horizon and a cosmological one at once, and below the Nariai limit no single involution
serves both: the two wedge reflections compose to a timelike translation, so identifying them
closes timelike curves. At Nariai one does, because the geometry is a product and both bifurcation
surfaces sit in a single two-dimensional de Sitter where one linear map fixes them together. So
the class is the maximal hole, $2.2\times10^{22}$ solar masses at the observed $\Lambda$, its
horizon ten billion light years in radius. Charge and spin widen that point to a branch and change
nothing else about it, out to a spin $a/M=3/(4\sqrt{2\sqrt3-3})=1.10092$ that is the same number
in every de Sitter background. Where a survey would go there is the boundary of
the parameter space instead, and every object on it is a whole universe's worth of geometry.

That is less of a special case than it sounds. The fold acts at the cosmological horizon whatever
the mass, which is the map Section 2 works with, and a hole joins the second class precisely when
its own horizon has become that one. Sections 2 and 6 turn out to be looking at a single structure
from opposite ends of a single parameter.

Two further calculations, neither run for this purpose, arrive at the same configuration. The
contact radius, swept against $\Lambda$, climbs from half the horizon radius to $0.991$ of it as
Nariai is approached, so the hole the fold singles out has no shell left where the sheets fail to
reach. And Nariai on its own decays: its sphere-radius perturbation has three modes and two of
them run away. Both runaways change sign under the fold and neither descends to the quotient, so
in that sector the configuration the fold singles out is also the one it holds still.

It reaches less far than it first reads. The surviving
mode is $X_2=\cosh t\sin\theta$, and on the hyperboloid $X_2^2=\ell^2+X_0^2-X_1^2$, so it is below
$\ell$ exactly inside the static patch $X_1>|X_0|$ and unbounded outside it. So what was shown is
a static-patch claim and not a global one. Radiative sectors are a separate matter, and there the
fold does no work at all: their potentials are $\ell(\ell+1)$ and $\ell(\ell+1)-2$, nonnegative at
every $\ell\geq1$, so there was no instability there to remove. Nor does the fold empty those
sectors, which the spherical calculation on its own might be read as implying. A separated
mode carries a temporal parity of its own, the mode equation is invariant under $t\to-t$, and the
total parity is $\tau(-1)^{k+\ell+a+s}$ with $\tau$ free, so every harmonic keeps exactly one
temporal branch. And the Euclidean section is untouched, because the Ginsparg-Perry direction is
the relative radius of the two spheres of $S^2\times S^2$, constant on both factors, and a
constant is invariant under every isometry, so no free involution can project it out. The fold
removes two runaway modes in one sector inside one patch. It does not make Nariai stable.

One question looks as though data should settle it and does not, and the reason is worth more than
the answer would have been. The radiation budget would separate a copy of our degrees of freedom
from a place carrying its own only if the two contributed at the same events, and the fold puts
them at different ones, which is the same fact that makes the silence a theorem. So $N_{\rm eff}$
is $3.044$ under either reading and Planck does not discriminate. The question itself is settled elsewhere in this paper, so the null result here is not agnosticism: Section 3's three tests remove the copy, and A.8 makes the mirror sheet a purification, an algebra
that is the commutant of ours with every expectation in it fixed by our own state. What that
leaves is not nothing: if the geometry forbids the mirror sheet from contributing locally, the
cosmology paper's $g_*=106.75$ is forced rather than chosen, and its dark-matter ceiling carries
no factor-of-two ambiguity on that account. The same choice shifts the predicted neutrino line by
seven per cent, which is a real prediction and not a near-term one, since that measurement is
limited by an absolute energy scale uncertain at fifteen to twenty-five per cent and no amount of
exposure reduces a common-mode systematic.

This work also commits itself somewhere a current instrument can reach. Every gravitational-wave
signature the fold admits needs a seam that reflects, and the implementation defended here sets
that to zero, so it predicts ordinary Kerr ringdown and a damping-time deviation of exactly zero.
Zero sits outside GWTC-3's posterior-multiplied interval and inside its hierarchical one, and
roughly twice the present exposure separates those two readings. That is the one place in this
work where the model can be killed inside a single observing run, and it is killed by an absence
rather than a discovery.

One thing that falls out of settling the seam's spectral response is worth more to a
gravitational-wave reader than the null prediction, because it applies whatever the fold turns out
to be. A.14 computes $M\,d\omega/d\mathcal R_h=0.00735845-0.0646771\,i$ for the fundamental
$\ell=2$ Schwarzschild mode, so a horizon reflectivity moves the damping time by $-0.727\,\mathcal
R_h$ and the real frequency by $+0.0197\,\mathcal R_h$: thirty-seven times harder on the decay
than on the note. The response is linear and signed, so a reflecting horizon can shorten a
ringdown as readily as lengthen one, and the absorption model in common use,
$\tau\to\tau/(1-|\mathcal R|^2)$, is quadratic and signless and therefore wrong at leading order.
At $|\mathcal R|=0.1$ the linear term is $7.2$ times the quadratic one. A searcher should take the
derivative from this paper whatever they make of the fold.

One thing this paper arrived at late concerns the divide itself. Since $\Theta^2=1$, the average of a field's two sheet readings is exactly its
fold-even part and the difference exactly its fold-odd part, so the fold's parity is the
classical-quantum split: where the sheets agree the difference vanishes and the physics is
classical, and where they disagree is where the commutator lives. No interpretation has been laid
over the algebra to get there. The algebra says it once the second contour leg is a place.

Nor is the amplitude of the quantum half free. Fold-invariance forces $\langle\Theta
A\Theta\rangle=\langle A\rangle$ for Hermitian $A$, so wherever $\Theta$ exchanges two things
their expectations are equal, which is how the cosmology paper's crossing selects a definite
occupation; and where $\Theta$ fixes the object instead, the same identity says nothing. So the
selection has a domain, and a horizon lies outside it. There it arrives from the other direction
and arrives complete: the fold's map being the half-period shift makes the equilibrium cross-sheet
correlator the direct one shifted by $i\beta/2$, hence a thermofield double with $\tanh
r=e^{-\beta\omega/2}$ and $\sinh^2r=1/(e^{\beta\omega}-1)$ exactly. A bifurcate horizon's
two-sheet squeeze is its temperature's, with nothing to choose.

Two ends of the fold then meet at one frequency. The crossing's ceiling
$\mathrm{arcsinh}(1/\sqrt2)$ is the horizon's own value at $\beta\omega=\ln3$, to
$8\times10^{-22}$, and the agreement is arithmetic rather than arranged. Two assumptions stand behind saying it, equilibrium at the horizon and the fold being $J\circ P_\perp$ rather than $J$, and A.8 carries both. Against that, the fold does not quantise
gravity and does not need to: the divide it was meant to explain comes out of the fold's own
parity, and the one number that looked adopted turns out to be a temperature.

What stays open is named where it arises. The seam coefficient is adopted. The reduction Appendix
A.10 rests on is not proved there, since a restriction to a codimension-two surface is not
automatic. Section 6's count is made inside the Kerr-Newman-de Sitter family, and nothing outside
it has been looked at; and the embedding argument is not done inside the warped rotating
near-horizon geometry, which the uncharged case has in full. And the interior algebra, which is
the one place the two sheets can actually reach each other, is not built here: whether anything
crosses the singularity, whether Hawking radiation is needed if it does, and what a two-sided hole
looks like from outside are one question wearing three faces, and nobody has done it.

Rotation is half settled. The fold map carries to Kerr exactly: the wedge reflection built from
the horizon generator, composed with the flow of the axial Killing field through $\pi$ and the
equatorial reflection, is an involution because both of those preserve the generator, and it is
free and an exact isometry at every spin. The contact condition's algebra transfers to a rotating
interior entrywise under that complete map, while every partial map breaks it. What does not
transfer is the angular budget on a distorted sphere, which is its own calculation, nor the
reading, because the bang is a spacelike surface crossed in time and a ring is crossed in space,
and no further algebra will settle a question that was never algebraic.

What this paper adds, said once. The fold is not ours. The fold comes from the
CPT-symmetric cosmology, the elliptic interpretation of a black hole from Gibbons [29] and Sanchez
and Whiting [30], the gauging of a spacetime inversion from Harlow and Numasawa [6]. What is new
is one question and its answer: where, if anywhere, can a point reach its own fold image. Outside
a horizon the answer is nowhere, and it is a theorem rather than a failure of imagination. Inside
one it is the inner half of the interior, at every charge, in four large spacetime dimensions and
no others, from a causal budget, which A.15 places against the four-dimensions genre and against
its closest relative, a century old and resting on the degree of a form rather than on causality.
Everything else here follows from that question being asked precisely.

The fold's map near a hole it fixes is forced and carries no free parameter; the second exterior
arrives with it rather than having to be made; the holes it can fix are the maximal ones alone,
out to a spin that is the same number in every de Sitter background; and the configuration it
singles out is the one it holds still.

One more item belongs on that list with a qualification attached to it. The state that map forces is the state whose image term diverges on the very
region the same map creates, so the fold polices its own causality violation and nothing
was added at any step to make it do so; that last link is shown in models and not yet at a hole.

A last observation, offered as one. Jacobson's derivation of the Einstein equation from the
Clausius relation puts two things in by hand: a temperature at every local Rindler horizon, and an
entropy proportional to area with a universal constant. The fold supplies the temperature, §3
having fixed the half-period shift with nothing left to choose. It supplies the horizons too, and
all of them at once, because the wedge reflection and the transverse antipode are each
frame-dependent while their composition $-\mathrm{Id}$ is not, so a single map is the fold of every
Rindler wedge through its fixed point, checked over two hundred random boosts and orientations to
$3\times10^{-14}$. A.19's contact locus is then a surface on which null geodesics focus, which is
the class of surface the argument runs on.

It supplies the entropy law as well, from the
entanglement of the thermofield double the map forces rather than from Bekenstein-Hawking, which
is what keeps the argument off its own tail. Both halves of that input, since Jacobson needs the
coefficient to be the same at every horizon and $\beta=2\pi/\kappa$ is not. Carrying $\kappa$
through the WKB mode count in the observer's proper variables, the Jacobian $d\omega=\kappa
\,d\Omega$ cancels the $1/\kappa$ in the density and $S/A$ holds to one part in $10^9$ while
$\beta$ runs from $251$ to $0.025$, four decades. The wedge metric in boost coordinates has no $\kappa$ in it
and the modular temperature is $2\pi$ there at every wedge, which is the same theorem $J$ is read
from. What $S/A$ does carry is the cutoff, as $1/\epsilon^2$, and that divergence is the
dimension-two constant no dimension-zero input could have returned. One condition comes with it:
because of that $1/\epsilon^2$, the cutoff has to be a single proper length rather than one per
horizon, a cutoff tracking $\kappa$ moving $S/A$ by a factor of $52$ over the same range. The
mode-counting prescription is not a condition: weighting the transverse density by
$e^{-k\epsilon}$ or $1/(1+k^2\epsilon^2)$ moves $S/A$ from $2.137$ to $1.629$ and $1.931$ while
the $\kappa$ independence holds at four parts in $10^{10}$.

Universality is the remaining input and the geometry supplies it. Jacobson needs a horizon at
every point in every null direction, while the fold's own fixed points are isolated. What the
balance needs at a generic point is weaker than a global symmetry: a map whose differential is
$-\mathrm{Id}$ and which is an isometry to the order the balance is computed at. The geodesic
symmetry $\exp_pv\mapsto\exp_p(-v)$ is one. In normal coordinates the metric's quadratic term is
built from $R$ and its cubic term from $\nabla R$, so that symmetry is exact through second order,
and Cartan's theorem makes the third-order failure real wherever $\nabla R\ne0$. Measured by
geodesic shooting on a surface with $\nabla K\ne0$, the residual $G_{\mu\nu}(v)-G_{\mu\nu}(-v)$ has
fitted exponent $2.9994$ against $3$, and its coefficient extrapolates to $0.168936$ against the
closed form $\tfrac13(\nabla K\cdot v)(|v|^2\delta_{\mu\nu}-v_\mu v_\nu)$, which gives $0.168939$.
On a surface whose cubic part is harmonic, so that the curvature varies while $\nabla K$ vanishes
at the point, the exponent moves to $5.0406$ and the cubic is gone: what the residual tracks is
$\nabla R$ at the point and nothing else. Set against the second-order term the balance uses, the
ratio extrapolates to $1.4435$ times the patch size and vanishes with it, and the field equation is
that limit.

The parity gives the same answer a second way. A map with differential $-\mathrm{Id}$
multiplies a rank-$n$ tensor by $(-1)^n$, and the balance holds $R_{kk}$, $T_{kk}$ and a transverse
area element, every one of them even. The boost field is rank one and flips, which is the wedge
swap itself.

What the fold does not supply is the value of the constant, and the reason is dimensional. Every
structural input here carries mass dimension zero while $\eta=1/4G$ carries two, so no symmetry
argument could produce it. One route to it is closed: A.19's locus sits a proper time
$(\pi/2+1)M$ inside the horizon, thirty-eight orders of magnitude above the Planck length, and in
flat space, where the local Rindler horizons live, the Van Vleck determinant is one everywhere and
no such locus exists. So the field equations follow from the fold and one constant where they had
followed from a temperature, an entropy law and a constant. That the argument does not assume its
own conclusion is a graph property and is treated as one: thirty derived nodes over six
declared roots, acyclic, with no path from the field equations reaching general relativity or
Bekenstein-Hawking, and both ways of breaking it detected when planted. The sixth root is the
Hadamard condition on states, which the graph did not carry until it was asked to, and which is a
regularity condition, not one of general relativity's properties: it is what equilibrium at the
horizon reduces to, and it belongs on the input list without belonging in the ledger.

Two things that read like postulates about gravity are not on the root list, and the reason in
each case is that what the argument uses is narrower than the name. The equivalence principle is
not assumed, because the eikonal characteristics of a field on a metric are that metric's
geodesics: the Hamiltonian system in $(x,p)$ built from $g^{ab}$ and the second-order Christoffel
system agree to $2\times10^{-12}$ over unit affine parameter, the mass cancels out of the path,
and a matter action written with a different metric sends the rays along that one's geodesics
instead. Diffeomorphism invariance is not assumed either. Its gravitational use, the contracted
Bianchi identity, is a theorem about the Levi-Civita connection of any metric, measured at
$2\times10^{-5}$ of the Einstein tensor's own size on a generic curved metric and falling as
$h^2$, with a torsionful connection breaking it at order unity; its matter use is the covariance
of the matter action, which is part of the metric postulate rather than beside it. What is left
is a Lorentzian signature, one shared metric, and two measured constants.

The signature entry is not indifferent either. A parity is
invariant only if the map cannot be deformed continuously to the identity, and in four Euclidean
dimensions $-\mathrm{Id}$ can be: rotating by $\theta$ in the $1$-$2$ plane and by $\theta$ in
the $3$-$4$ plane at once is an isometry throughout and arrives at $\theta=\pi$. In Lorentzian
signature every $\Lambda\in O(1,3)$ has $|\Lambda^0{}_0|\ge1$, with a smallest sampled value of
$1.0000001$ against a largest of $4969$, so no continuous path of isometries carries $+1$ to
$-1$ and $-\mathrm{Id}$ is $PT$. Counted over all five four-dimensional signatures the rule is
sharper than the two-way comparison suggests: $-\mathrm{Id}$ lies in the identity component of
$O(t,s)$ exactly when $t$ and $s$ are both even, so the fold keeps a parity exactly when the
number of time directions is odd, and $(0,4)$, $(2,2)$ and $(4,0)$ all fall to an explicit path
while $(1,3)$ and $(3,1)$ admit none. In the wrong signature the fold is a removable rotation with no
fold-even and fold-odd sectors, so the signature is the condition under which the fold is a
symmetry at all.

The other entry gets the same treatment and comes out the same way. One metric for the horizons
and for the matter is not a convenience. The Clausius step needs a stress tensor that is conserved,
and Noether's second theorem hands you conservation with respect to the metric the matter action
was written with and no other. Take a scalar action on $\tilde g$ and compute
$\nabla_a T^a{}_b$ with each metric's own Levi-Civita connection and the identity closes at
$2\times10^{-7}$ on all of them; take the same stress tensor and use the horizons' connection
instead and the residue is $0.099$, which is $4\times10^5$ times the floor the own-connection
cases sit at. Two metrics differing by a constant factor share a connection, and there the residue
is $7\times10^{-15}$ at a factor of $1.7$, so what the first number measures is the connections
disagreeing rather than the size of the difference between the metrics. None of that says a
universe could not carry two metrics. It says the derivation would stop where it stands and hand back no
field equation at all, which is what it looks like for an assumption to be load-bearing.

Where that constant might have come from is worth two sentences, because both places have now been
looked at. In the late universe §5.2's budget is short at every finite radius, so the image stress
is zero and sources nothing. At the bang, $\Theta:\eta\to-\eta$ is an isometry only for $a$ odd
in $\eta$, which admits a fluid exactly where $-3w$ is an odd integer and excludes dust, so the
fold requires a hot bang and permits any $\Lambda$. The one remaining route runs through $m^2$,
which shares the Hadamard slot with $(\xi-1/6)R$ and so keeps an image stress alive for massive
content. Rescaling $\eta=s/\sqrt\gamma$, $p=q\sqrt\gamma$ removes $\gamma=M_1a_1$ from the
crossing, identically to seven figures over two decades in $\gamma$, leaving
$\rho_{\rm img}=\gamma^2H(s)/a^4$ with $s$ proportional to $a$. Constancy in $a$ then demands
$H\propto s^4$ and returns $cM_1^4$, which is $81.4$ orders of magnitude above the observed
$\rho_\Lambda$. The fold has no scale near $2.2$ meV, so $\Lambda$ is a boundary datum
and nothing here was ever going to produce it.

None of that needed the seam coefficient, and none of it is
a measurement. It is what taking CPT seriously turns out to commit you to at a horizon, which is
more than we expected it to commit anyone to, and less than a theory of the interior.

Two of these results are one result read at different distances. The fold's parity is the
classical-quantum split, so the classical world is the part of a two-sheet field that survives
the quotient, and no limit in $\hbar$ has to be taken to reach it; at a horizon the weight of
the part that does not survive is a temperature. That is why the cosmology paper gets the field
equations out of a symmetry with the metric left as a background, and it is why asking where
the two sheets can touch has an answer at all. They touch inside a hole with a past, across the
inner half of its interior, in four dimensions and in no others, and the nearest test of that
is an exactly Kerr ringdown with zero damping-time deviation, decidable at about twice present
exposure. What the two papers share is the shape of the answer: gravity did not have to be
quantised for any of it, and the symmetry was doing the work.



## Appendices

The appendices are numbered from A.6 because A.1 to A.5 belong to the cosmology paper.

**A.6 A worked instance of the in-in dictionary.** §2's dictionary, worked on one pair. Take the
massless conformally coupled scalar on dS$_4$ and put the observer on the central worldline, *r* =
0. There the transverse sphere degenerates, the transverse parity acts trivially, and *A* = *J*
exactly, so the fold's image kernel and the thermal contour's cross-leg propagator are the same
function of time:

> $$
> G_{\mathrm{img}}(t)=\frac{H^2}{16\pi^2}\operatorname{sech}^2\!\left(\frac{Ht}{2}\right)
> =W(t-i\beta/2)=G_{+-}^{\sigma=\beta/2}(t),
> \qquad \beta=\frac{2\pi}{H}.
> $$

The three expressions agree in the stated Bunch-Davies conformal-scalar example. Define the
analytic half-period continuation $F_{\rm half}(t)=F_W(t-i\beta/2)$. With the convention
$F(E)=\int dt\,e^{-iEt}F(t)$, strip analyticity gives $F_{\rm half}(E)=e^{\beta E/2}F_W(E)$.
Identifying this analytic continuation with a geometric image $W(x,\alpha y)$ additionally
requires the state's geometric reflection and parity implementation, including index transport for
fields with spin; KMS alone does not establish that identification for every state, mass or spin.
With the spectral density $\rho$(*E*) = *E*/4$\pi ^2$, fixed by the Gibbons–Hawking detector
response, the reconstruction $\int_0^\infty dE\,\rho(E)\cos(Et)/\sinh(\beta E/2)$ returns the same
sech$^2$, and a five-energy numerical check reproduces $F_{\rm img}=e^{\beta E/2}F_{\rm BD}$.
These are implementation checks of the stated analytic conformal-scalar identities. No claim of
physical precision attaches to them. The half-period identity itself is checked to
$4\times10^{-17}$ at four times, and fails by $0.115$ ten per cent away in $\beta$.

The superscript on $G_{+-}$ is not a convention here. A thermal contour may be displaced downward
by any $\sigma$ in $[0,\beta]$ without changing a physical correlator, and it carries two cross
kernels, one for each ordering of the legs. For one mode their difference is
$i\sin\omega t\,\sinh(\omega(\beta/2-\sigma))/\omega\sinh(\beta\omega/2)$, reproduced against
the mode sum to $6\times10^{-16}$ and vanishing at every separation only at $\sigma=\beta/2$. With
$\omega=1.3$ and $\beta\omega=2\ln3$, so that $1/\omega$ is the unit for the three numbers that
follow, at $\sigma=\beta/3$ the two kernels still differ by $0.216$, and at $\sigma=0$ by
the whole commutator $i\sin\omega t/\omega$ whatever the temperature.

Since $\Theta$ is antilinear
with $\Theta^2=1$, exchanging the sheets must leave the cross kernel alone, so the fold admits one
member of the family. The selection is the Bose factor doing the work, through
$(1+n)e^{-\omega\sigma}=ne^{\omega\sigma}$: with the thermal occupation dropped and only the
vacuum piece kept, the two kernels differ at $\beta/2$ as much as anywhere else, by $0.256$ against
$0.496$ at $\sigma=0.2\beta$, a ratio near a half where the thermal case drops to machine zero. No mode is indifferent, and modes with $\beta\omega$ near two are
the most sensitive, losing $0.0259$ of the coincident correlator for a displacement of one per cent
of $\beta$. The equal-time two-sheet matrix at $\sigma=\beta/2$ has the fold-even and fold-odd
combinations as eigenvectors, with eigenvalues $\coth(\beta\omega/4)$ and $\tanh(\beta\omega/4)$
in units of $1/2\omega$ whose product is $1$ to twelve figures at every temperature, giving the
manuscript's ratio $4\tanh^2(\beta\omega/4)$, which is $0.2872$ at the crossing ceiling
$\beta\omega=\ln3$ and $1$ at $\beta\omega=2\ln3$.

One caution belongs with this. The
quantum-quantum correlator of the symmetric contour does not vanish: it is
$\tanh(\beta\omega/4)\cos\omega t/\omega$, which is $0.905$ in units of $1/\omega$ at coincidence
for $\beta\omega=6$, and the familiar statement that it vanishes belongs to $\sigma=0$, where the
computation returns zero and falls to it monotonically. What vanishes at $\beta/2$ is the
quantum-quantum commutator, which is the statement §2 makes. Off the
central worldline the two maps part company. For two points at radii $r_1$ and $r_2$ subtending an
