angle $\gamma$ at the centre, with time separation $\Delta t$, the embedding invariants differ by

$$
Z_\alpha-Z_J=-2r_1r_2\cos\gamma,
$$

independent of $\Delta t$, which reduces to $-2r^2$ at equal radii and $\gamma=0$. A relative
discrepancy needs a denominator, and the two choices behave differently. Referred to the fold
kernel it is $r^2/[(1-r^2)\cosh^2(\Delta t/2)]$, which is 9.9% at $r=0.3$ and 178% at $r=0.8$ for
$\Delta t=0$; referred to the thermal kernel it is $-r^2$, bounded by unity. Both the invariants
above and these two figures are reproduced from the kernels. The two differ by
$(1-Z_J)/(1-Z_\alpha)$ and that factor carries the apparent growth.

Only the ratio is invariant. At $\Delta t=0$ and $\gamma=0$ the invariants are $Z_J=-1+2r^2$ and
$Z_\alpha=-1$, so $1-Z_\alpha=2$ identically and

$$
G_\alpha=\tfrac12,\qquad G_J=\frac{1}{2(1-r^2)},\qquad G_\alpha/G_J=1-r^2,
$$

in units where the prefactor is dropped. The fold's image kernel is constant in radius. Divergence
at the de Sitter horizon belongs to the thermal contour's cross-leg kernel, and at $r=1/\sqrt2$
the fold kernel is half of it. The transverse parity flips the sign of the $r^2$ term, which is
what cancels the radial dependence. This is a statement about $\gamma=0$ and does not generalise
as it stands. At the horizon $1-Z_J\to1-\cos\gamma$ and $1-Z_\alpha\to1+\cos\gamma$, so each
kernel is singular exactly where its own map brings the two points together: the thermal contour's
at coincident directions, the fold's at antipodal ones. Neither is the better behaved overall. The
general statement is the horizon ratio,

$$
G_\alpha/G_J\to\tan^2(\gamma/2),
$$

independent of everything else and reproduced to $4\times10^{-7}$. Written with unit direction
vectors this is

$$
\frac{G_\alpha}{G_J}\to\frac{|\hat n_1-\hat n_2|^2}{|\hat n_1-(-\hat n_2)|^2},
$$

the squared chord between the two points over the squared chord from one to the other's antipode.
$J$ leaves the transverse direction alone, while $\alpha$ carries it to the antipodal one, so each
kernel is controlled by the separation of its own pair of arguments. The three features the
cosmology paper's A.1 states then need no calculation here: each is the ordinary short-distance
singularity of a two-point function, appearing at the angle its own identification selects. The
$1-Z_\alpha=2$ identity is specific to $\gamma=0$ and $\Delta t=0$; the $\tan^2(\gamma/2)$ ratio
holds at every angle. Both are statements about the conformally coupled massless scalar in the
Bunch-Davies state, and nothing is claimed for spin two or for other states. The $\cosh^{-2}$
confines the effect to coincident times, decaying as $4e^{-|\Delta t|}$ once $|\Delta t|\gg1$.

Angular dependence needs care here. The invariant difference is exactly $P_1(\cos\gamma)$, but the
discrepancy is not, because $\cos\gamma$ also enters its denominator. Writing $b=r_1r_2$ and
$a=1+\sqrt{(1-r_1^2)(1-r_2^2)}\cosh\Delta t$, the fold-referred discrepancy
$-2b\cos\gamma/(a-b\cos\gamma)$ expands as $-(2b/a)\sum_{n\ge0}(b/a)^n\cos^{n+1}\gamma$, with no
alternation because the denominator carries $-b\cos\gamma$; the thermal-referred form
$-2b\cos\gamma/(a+b\cos\gamma)$ gives the same series with $(-1)^n$ inserted, so every multipole
appears, suppressed by powers of $b/a$, which at equal radii and $\Delta t=0$ is $r^2/(2-r^2)$.

The dipole dominates only while that is small: the quadrupole is 3.1% of it at $r=0.3$, 23% at
$r=1/\sqrt2$ and 53% at $r=0.9$. The angular zero holds exactly at every radius and every time
separation, and the numerator alone carries it: the discrepancy vanishes at $\gamma=\pi/2$ and
reverses sign across it whatever the higher multipoles do. That zero does not weaken with radius
and is the sharp feature.

Contamination by higher multipoles belongs to the variable. The fold does not produce it. The
discrepancy is a difference of kernels; the ratio is not, and by the reciprocal law of A.10
$\log(G_\alpha/G_J)$ is odd about $\gamma=\pi/2$ for any Hadamard state, so its even multipoles
vanish *identically* at every radius and every time separation, rather than only while $b/a$ is
small. That is algebra and not a measurement: a projection at $r=0.9$, where the quadrupole of the
discrepancy is 53% of its dipole, returns even coefficients below $10^{-15}$ for four unrelated
profiles, which tests the quadrature and not the claim. The quantity that matters is the truncation error, since the logarithmic endpoints make the series converge in $L^2$ but not
pointwise there, and a truncated reconstruction rings within $O(1/\ell_{\max})$ of $\gamma=0$ and
$\pi$. The dipole dominance that has to be argued for in the difference is exact in the logarithm,
away from the two endpoints.

Two readings have to be excluded. The discrepancy depends on $r_1$, $r_2$, $\gamma$ and $\Delta t$
alone, so it is invariant under a rigid rotation of both points and selects no axis; $P_\perp$ is
a parity and parity commutes with rotations. The fold cannot generate a directional dipole of the
kind reported in radio and quasar number counts, at any amplitude. Neither is this a correction to
ordinary clustering: the objects compared are the cross-leg kernels $G_{12}$ of the cosmology
paper's §2.2, while the galaxy two-point function is built from the same-leg propagator. Read as a
fractional correction to $w(\gamma)$ in a narrow redshift slice it would give tens of per cent by
$z\simeq1$ and more than unity by $z\simeq2$, which observed clustering excludes outright, and
that rules out the identification rather than the fold. The cross-leg kernel enters the noise and
dissipation sector, which is where the force-noise ports below place it.

For scale, $r$ is the areal radius in units of the de Sitter radius, so in the flat slicing it is
physical distance in horizon radii. Taking $H_\Lambda=H_0\sqrt{\Omega_\Lambda}$ and a $\Lambda$CDM
distance-redshift relation for orientation only, $r=0.3$ is $z\simeq0.40$, $r=0.8$ is
$z\simeq1.4$, and the horizon $r=1$ is $z\simeq2.0$. The largest angular scales of the microwave
background lie well outside this range: a $60^\circ$ separation on the last-scattering surface
corresponds to $r\simeq2.6$, so nothing in this comparison bears on the large-angle correlation
anomalies.

That same structure governs a quantity which is not a consistency check. For a comoving scalar
charge the local and image force-noise kernels form a two-port matrix with fold-even and fold-odd
eigenvalues $A_0\pm A_I$. On the central worldline the image kernel is exactly minus the
half-period shift of the local one, so $A_I=-A_0=-1/12\pi^2$ and the even port vanishes
identically. The $-2r^2$ breaks that identity. Carrying the discrepancy into the kernel and
integrating, with $\int dt\,{\rm sech}^6(t/2)=32/15$, gives an even-port lift against an unchanged
odd port $1/6\pi^2$. Referred to the fold kernel the lift is $-r^2/[15\pi^2(1-r^2)]$; referred to
the thermal kernel it saturates, approaching $-1/12\pi^2$ as $r\to1$, exactly half the odd port.
Under that thermal referencing the even channel is lifted off zero at every $r>0$ and stays the
smaller of the two at all radii; referred to the fold kernel it instead reaches the odd port at
$r=\sqrt{5/7}$, which is the same normalisation artefact again, with no physical crossover behind
it. The degeneracy is exact only on axis, the misalignment enters at second order, and the sign is
fixed. Carrying a propagator-level discrepancy into the force-noise kernel is a leading-order
identification rather than a spin-2 derivation; the lifting of the zero and its order in $r$ do
not depend on that step, but the coefficients do.

**A.7 The first-tick sum and its asymptote.** The closed form quoted in §2.1 and its large-$A$
limit, derived. Closed de Sitter in conformal time has *a* = 1/(*H* cos $\eta$), *aH* = sec $\eta$
and $\dot a$ = tan $\eta$. Write $\chi$ = *a$\varphi$*, and take scalar harmonics $-\nabla^2
Y_n=(n^2-1)Y_n$ of multiplicity $n^2$. The environment is then *n* $\ge$ 2, since *n* = 1 is the
homogeneous mode and belongs to minisuperspace, and

> $$
> \begin{aligned}
> \chi_n''+(n^2-2\sec^2\eta)\chi_n&=0,\\
> v_n&=C_n e^{in\eta}(\tan\eta+in),\\
> |C_n|^2&=\frac{1}{2n(n^2-1)},\\
> \psi_n&\propto\exp(-\Omega_n\chi^2/2),\\
> \Omega_n&=-i\frac{v_n'}{v_n},\qquad
> \Omega_n'=i(\omega^2-\Omega_n^2).
> \end{aligned}
> $$
> $$
> \begin{aligned}
> \Omega_n&=\frac{n(n^2-1)-i\tan\eta\,\sec^2\eta}{\tan^2\eta+n^2},
> &\Omega_n(0)&=\frac{n^2-1}{n}\in\mathbb R,\\
> \operatorname{Re}\Omega_n&=\frac{n(n^2-1)}{\dot a^2+n^2},
> &\operatorname{Im}\Omega_n&=-\frac{\dot a\,(aH)^2}{\dot a^2+n^2},\\
> \tan\theta_n&=-\frac{\dot a(1+\dot a^2)}{n(n^2-1)}.
> \end{aligned}
> $$
> $$
> \psi_-=\bar\psi_+
> \Longrightarrow
> D_n=|\langle\bar\psi|\psi\rangle|
> =\left(\frac{\operatorname{Re}\Omega_n}{|\Omega_n|}\right)^{1/2}
> =(1+\tan^2\theta_n)^{-1/4}=(1-r_n^2)^{1/4}.
> $$
> $$
> \log|\langle E_-|E_+\rangle|
> =-\frac{N_f}{4}\sum_{n\ge2}n^2
> \log\!\left[1+\frac{A^4(A^2-1)}{n^2(n^2-1)^2}\right],
> \qquad A\equiv aH.
> $$
> With $u=n/A$, the large-$A$ limit is
> $$
> -\frac{N_f}{4}A^3\int_0^\infty u^2\ln(1+u^{-6})\,du
> =-\frac{\pi}{12}N_fA^3.
> $$

Each environment mode is a Gaussian whose frequency function solves the Riccati equation; the
no-boundary condition is that $\Omega$_n be real at the throat, which the exact mode function
delivers, and because the Wheeler–DeWitt operator is real the two branch-conditioned environment
states are complex conjugates, so the branch overlap is the Gaussian integral $\int \psi ^2$ and
not $\int$|$\psi$|$^2$. The squeezing phase is exactly odd in $\dot a$ mode by mode, which is the
time-odd order parameter, while |*D*| depends on $\dot a ^2$ alone, as time reversal requires;
summing the mode overlaps over the $S^3$ tower with degeneracy $n^2$ gives the closed form. At
large *A* the summand depends on *n* only through *u* = *n*/*A*, so the sum becomes
$A^3\int_0^\infty u^2\ln(1+u^{-6})\,du=\pi A^3/3$, and the asymptote is already good to 0.07 per
cent at *A* = 10: the sum is $-261.627$ against $-261.799$, a gap of $0.066$ per cent. The
integral is $\pi/3$ to $2\times10^{-16}$ and the sum is converged in the truncation.

The main paper keeps a six-line pointer in place of this material, and the reason is not
presentational: of the conclusions this programme reversed under adversarial review, four of five
were here and one was in the cosmology paper. It is the least settled part of the work and the
part the cosmological results do not depend on. Keeping it here lets the paper carry the bound,
and lets this document carry the horizon question at whatever length it needs.

**A.8 The image term: what positivity and Hadamard forbid, what they allow, and what sets its
size.** §3.1's two inequalities, derived. Let $\mathcal{A}$ be the antipodal map (*A* is reserved
here for the amplitude), take the conformally coupled massless scalar on dS$_4$, where the
Bunch–Davies kernel is elementary, and consider the following real, de Sitter-invariant
two-amplitude ansatz on one static patch:

> $$
> \begin{aligned}
> W(x,y)&=\tfrac12 i\Delta(x,y)+A\,G_{\mathrm{sym}}(Z)+B\,G(-Z),\\
> G(Z)&=\frac{H^2}{8\pi^2(1-Z)},\qquad Z(x,\mathcal A y)=-Z(x,y).
> \end{aligned}
> $$
>
> The antipode pairs each cover mode with itself, antilinearly:
> $$
> y_n\circ\mathcal A=-\bar y_n,
> $$
> $$
> \langle a^\dagger a\rangle=\nu,\qquad
> \langle aa\rangle=\langle a^\dagger a^\dagger\rangle=\kappa,
> \qquad A=2\nu+1,\quad B=-2\kappa,
> $$
> $$
> \sigma=\begin{pmatrix}\nu+\tfrac12+\kappa&0\\0&\nu+\tfrac12-\kappa\end{pmatrix},
> \qquad
> \Omega=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\-1&0\end{pmatrix},
> $$
> $$
> \sigma+\frac{i}{2}\Omega\succeq0
> \Longleftrightarrow\nu\ge0\ \text{and}\ \nu(\nu+1)\ge\kappa^2
> \Longleftrightarrow A\ge1\ \text{and}\ A^2-B^2\ge1.
> $$
> Since $G(-Z)=H^2/[8\pi^2(1+Z)]$ is regular as $Z\to1$, Hadamard normalisation gives
> $A=1$ and hence $B=0$.

Within the displayed de Sitter-invariant two-amplitude ansatz, the mode blocks may be chosen
diagonal in this basis and are described by the 2 × 2 covariance $\sigma$. Positivity, $\sigma$ +
(*i*/2)$\Omega$ $\ge$ 0, then says that the spectral weight of the symmetric part must dominate
the commutator $\tfrac12$*i*$\Delta$ mode by mode, which in the amplitudes is $A^2$ $-$ $B^2$
$\ge$ 1.

That equivalence is checked against direct diagonalisation on a 301 × 601 grid in
($\nu$,$\kappa$). Criterion and eigenvalues agree at all $180901$ points, both classes are
populated, $112865$ against $68036$, and three wrong criteria each produce tens of thousands of
mismatches. At three points the grid lands exactly on the boundary, and at one of them the two
sides round opposite ways, so the count without a matched tolerance is $180900$.

One thing that argument does not do is forbid the fold an image correlation altogether, and the gap is named here. The ansatz tested above is a
constant amplitude multiplying the whole spectrum, which is why the ultraviolet can refuse it: it
puts a singularity on the image lightcone, at pairs that are not null-related in the first place.
A finite, smooth image correlation is a different object, because it adds only smooth terms and
leaves every singularity where it was. Squeeze a single mode, $v=\cosh r\,u+\sinh r\,\bar u$, and
the two-point function changes by

$$\Delta W(x,y)=2\sinh^2\!r\,\mathrm{Re}\!\left[u(x)\overline{u(y)}\right]
+\sinh 2r\,\mathrm{Re}\!\left[u(x)u(y)\right],$$

and if the mode is fold-real, $u(\Theta y)=\overline{u(y)}$, the second term is an image
correlation without anything having been inserted to make it one. The Bogoliubov map is canonical,
$\cosh^2r-\sinh^2r=1$, so the commutator and positivity are untouched; $\Delta W$ is a finite sum
of products of smooth mode functions, so the short-distance singularity is untouched and the state
remains Hadamard; and the local stress moves. Constancy across the spectrum is what the argument
above excludes, not image correlation as such. Nor is the fold confined to a parity-blind
spherical mode: a transversely odd $\ell=1$ mode, for which $u(\Theta x)=-\overline{u(x)}$, flips
the sign of the image coefficient and still gives a nonzero stress shift.

Nor does it need a mass, which was the first thing suspected of it. Running the same construction
at $m=0$ on the Nariai background leaves the image correlation intact, with a stress shift whose
sign varies with position on the sphere as well as with the squeeze. What does fail at $m\to0$ is
something else, and the distinction is the useful part: the Euclidean reference state's
homogeneous mode has variance going as $\Lambda/m^2$ and does not survive the limit. A genuine
massless canonical pair does exist. On the $\mathrm{dS}_2$ factor the homogeneous equation $\ddot
q+\tanh t\,\dot q=0$ has the constant and the Gudermannian $\mathrm{gd}\,t=\arctan\sinh t$ for
solutions, and $u_\alpha=\sqrt{\Lambda/16\pi^2\alpha}\,(1-i\alpha\,\mathrm{gd}\,t)$ carries unit
Klein-Gordon norm for every $\alpha>0$, the $\cosh t$ of the circle cancelling against the
$\mathrm{sech}\,t$ of the current. So the zero mode is a state to be chosen and not a barrier to
having one, and what the massless limit kills is the reference, not the field. A massless scalar
is not a photon and nothing here is a Maxwell calculation.

That family is where the fold's remaining freedom now sits, and the fold does not reduce
it. Since $\mathrm{gd}$ is odd and $\Theta$ acts on a mode as $(\Theta u)(t)=\overline{u(-t)}$,
the pair $u_\alpha$ is fold-real at every $\alpha$, so $\Theta$-invariance selects no member. The
ambiguity is the familiar infrared one of a minimally coupled massless field in de Sitter, wearing
this model's clothes, and it does not touch the conformally coupled case worked above, where $\xi
R$ acts as a mass and there is no zero mode to argue about.

What that leaves is a selection problem rather than an exclusion, and it is worth being exact
about which kind, because the fold is not silent on selection in general. For an antiunitary
$\Theta$ with $\Theta^2=1$, a $\Theta$-invariant state and a Hermitian $A$, $\langle\Theta
A\Theta\rangle=\overline{\langle A\rangle}=\langle A\rangle$. That is antilinearity and
hermiticity and nothing else. Where $\Theta$ *exchanges* two things it therefore forces their
expectations equal, which is how the cosmology paper's contact condition $\langle
N_+\rangle=\langle N_-\rangle$ arises at the bang: a consequence rather than a prescription, and
with it a definite occupation.

Where $\Theta$ *fixes* the object instead, the same identity constrains nothing, and a fold-real
mode at a horizon is that case. A squeezed vacuum with real $r$ has real Fock amplitudes, so it is
$\Theta$-invariant at every $r$ while its occupation $\sinh^2 r$ runs freely; giving the
amplitudes a phase makes the invariance bite again, so the check is not vacuous. The fold selects
where it exchanges and not where it fixes.

So the squeeze parameter is not a foundational gap waiting for a new principle. The state at a
horizon is whatever the selected bang state evolves into, which makes this a propagation question
and not a question about what selects. What survives as genuine freedom is the phase the bang
leaves per mode, and that is a smaller and better-posed object than a free amplitude. It is also
bounded rather than open. A squeeze is measured by its occupation, $\langle N\rangle=\sinh^2r$, so
the occupation the bang selects is a squeeze it selects, $r=\mathrm{arcsinh}\sqrt{n_*}$ with
$n_*=(1-\sqrt{1-P})/2$ the cosmology paper's floor; and since $n_*\leq\tfrac12$,
$r\leq\mathrm{arcsinh}(1/\sqrt2)=0.65848$, a pure number. Reading that as a ceiling here needs two
things neither paper establishes, that the crossing's invariance reaches the mode and that its
squeeze is inherited rather than regenerated, and both are stated rather than assumed. That
propagation is short, because Section 2 has already done its hard half. The fold's map is the
square root of the thermal transformation at the primitive period, so for a state in equilibrium
at a horizon the cross-sheet correlator is the direct one shifted by half a period, which is a
consequence and not a choice. Carrying it through for one mode of frequency $\omega$ at inverse
temperature $\beta$,

$$W\!\left(t-\tfrac{i\beta}{2}\right)=\frac{\cos\omega t}{2\omega\sinh(\beta\omega/2)},$$

real and even, which is the fold-real property the fold needs; the two exponentials
collapse to a single coefficient because $(1+n)e^{-\beta\omega/2}$ and $ne^{\beta\omega/2}$ are
equal. A two-mode state with that cross correlator is the thermofield double, and the thermofield
double is a two-mode squeezed vacuum at

$$\tanh r(\omega)=e^{-\beta\omega/2},\qquad
\langle N\rangle=\sinh^2 r=\frac{1}{e^{\beta\omega}-1},$$

the second identity exact rather than approximate. So the squeeze the fold carries at a horizon is
fixed by the mode frequency and the surface gravity and nothing else. The amplitude left open
above is not open.

Two things travel with that and both are stated rather than buried. The fold is $J\circ P_\perp$
and not $J$, and the embedding settles that: on the de Sitter hyperboloid
in $\mathbb R^{1,4}$ the two factors compose to $-\mathrm{Id}$ to machine zero, $J$ alone has a
three-dimensional $+1$ eigenspace whose restricted form is positive definite and therefore a whole
two-sphere of fixed points, $P_\perp$ alone fixes a hyperbola, and a $\pi$ rotation put in place
of the parity fixes two points. A fold pairs two sheets, so a fixed point is a point that is its
own mirror and none is allowed; total inversion is the only one of the four with no $+1$
eigenvalue, and its differential on the tangent space has trace $-4$. Scaling one transverse
direction by $1.1$ inside the reflection breaks the isometry by $0.210$, so the enumeration is over
isometries and not over maps. What travels with the choice being forced is that the state is the
thermofield double carrying an extra transverse parity: the
marginals on either sheet are unchanged while the cross-sheet term picks up $(-1)^{\ell}$ per
multipole, which is why a stress evaluated at a single squeeze comes out with opposite signs at
the pole and at the equator. And equilibrium at the horizon is not a theorem about every state,
though it is a weaker assumption than that sentence used to admit.

The Hadamard condition, which A.9's
parametrix already imposes on every state computed with here, makes the singular part of a
two-point function a matter of geometry and the state-dependent part smooth, and the temperature
comes from the singular part. On a patch of proper size $r$ the state-dependent part reaches the
ratio $W_{\rm reg}/W_{\rm sing}$ only at order $r^2$: a thermal state at temperature $T$ gives
$\pi^2r^2T^2/3$, with the coefficient $3.2898681$ against $\pi^2/3$ and the exponent
$2.000000$ fitted over five decades, and a classical background gives $4\pi^2r^2\phi_{\rm cl}^2$
at $1.999972$. Since the balance is already first order in the patch, an error of relative order
$(rT)^2$ in the temperature falls one order below the equation returned.

Alter the singular
coefficient, which is what leaving the Hadamard class does, and the ratio goes to the alteration
rather than to zero, at exponent $10^{-4}$ or smaller. Two limits belong with that. It is a
statement about a shrinking patch and not about a whole horizon, a stellar-mass hole in the present
microwave background having $rT$ near $3.57\times10^6$; and it quantifies the deviation rather than removing it, so a state with no KMS property at the horizon remains a state the fold has
nothing to say about. A third qualifier belongs with those two and is a restriction on *which* horizons the
result describes. The half-period shift is A.10's statement at a bifurcate Killing horizon, and a
hole formed by collapse has none: no past singularity, no second exterior, no bifurcation surface.
So the squeeze computed here is the squeeze at the cosmological horizon and at a two-sided hole,
and Section 6 counts that class at one member. What the result does not do is assign a two-sheet
correlation to the horizon of an ordinary astrophysical black hole, where the fold still pairs our
sheet with the mirror but pairs our hole with a mirror hole rather than with a second exterior of
its own.

![The two ends of the fold, and the one frequency where they agree. Left: what the crossing at the bang selects. Fold-invariance forces half-and-half occupancy, so the pair-block floor is $n_*(P)=(1-\sqrt{1-P})/2$, and since a squeeze carries $\langle N\rangle=\sinh^2 r$ the selected occupation is a selected squeeze $r=\mathrm{arcsinh}\sqrt{n_*}$, topping out at $\mathrm{arcsinh}(1/\sqrt2)=0.6585$ when the crossing is complete. Right: what equilibrium at a bifurcate horizon fixes, the half-period shift making the state a thermofield double with $\tanh r=e^{-\beta\omega/2}$. The dashed line is the same in both panels, and the horizon curve crosses it at $\beta\omega=\ln3$ exactly, to machine zero. The reason is that both ends are statements about one half: $\Theta$-invariance caps the crossing at half occupancy, a horizon mode is half occupied where the Bose factor is one half, and that is $e^{\beta\omega}=3$. The curves meet because they say the same thing.](fig_companion_two_ends.pdf)

One number joins this to the other end of the fold. The cosmology paper's crossing bounds
the squeeze at the bang by $\mathrm{arcsinh}(1/\sqrt2)=0.65848$, and that is exactly the horizon's
own value at $\beta\omega=\ln3$, since $\sinh r=1/\sqrt2$ gives $\cosh r=\sqrt{3/2}$ and therefore
$\tanh r=1/\sqrt3=e^{-\ln3/2}$; the two agree to $8\times10^{-22}$. That agreement is no coincidence, and it is better for not being one: the ceiling is half occupancy, the Bose factor
reaches half at $e^{\beta\omega}=3$, and the two curves meet there because they are the same
statement. The ceiling is exactly one half, which is what
$\Theta$-invariance gives and nothing else chose. Modes softer than $T\ln3$ sit
above the bang's ceiling and harder ones below it. The two ends of the fold meet at one
frequency, and the meeting is arithmetic rather than arranged.

The Hadamard step is short-distance: the image term is regular as *Z* $\to$ 1; its only pole sits
at *Z* = $-1,$ which a pair of points reaches only on the horizon; so the whole coincidence
singularity of *W* is *A* times the cover's, and fixing it to the cover's geometric value *is* *A*
= 1, whence *B* = 0. Against that, the tested projected image prescription, (*A*,*B*) = (1,1) or
($\nu$,$\kappa$) = (0,$- \tfrac12$), misses positivity by exactly $\tfrac14$; it assigns zero
variance to one quadrature, $\sigma$_x = 0, minimum eigenvalue $\tfrac12-1/\sqrt2=-0.2071067812$
exactly, and by Reeh–Schlieder the offending test function can be taken supported inside one
static patch, so the failure is already local. The Mottola–Allen $\alpha$-vacua give the positive
comparison family, with *A* = (1+|*c*|$^2$)/(1$-$|*c*|$^2$) and *B* = 2|*c*|cos
$\beta$/(1$-$|*c*|$^2$). A nonzero image coefficient requires *A* > 1, contrary to the Hadamard
normalisation *A* = 1.

**A.9 A selected time-symmetric response has zero net dissipative work.** §3.3's vanishing,
derived. The antipode is an isometry that reverses time orientation, so it exchanges the past and
future light cones and therefore $G_{\mathrm{ret}}$ $\circ$ (*A*×*A*) = $G_{\mathrm{adv}}$. The
Wheeler–Feynman exchange [27] is also checked numerically: max |$G_{\mathrm{ret}}$(*Ax*,*Ay*) $-$
$G_{\mathrm{adv}}$(*x*,*y*)| = 0.000e+00 for four masses; hence **within the retarded/advanced
affine family** the unique invariant combination is the even one. This does not make the complete
quotient Green problem unique without boundary data. For two sources at separation *R* coupled
through a stationary reciprocal mutual kernel *K*,

> $$
> W=\int dt\,\bigl[\dot q_2(t)\Phi_1(t)+\dot q_1(t)\Phi_2(t)\bigr],
> \qquad \Phi_i=\frac{K\ast q_i}{4\pi R}.
> $$
>
> For an even kernel, $K(t-t')=K(t'-t)$, and the integrand is
> $(\partial_t+\partial_{t'})[q_2(t)q_1(t')]K(t-t')$.
> Because $(\partial_t+\partial_{t'})K(t-t')=0$, that integrand is the total
> derivative $(\partial_t+\partial_{t'})\{q_2(t)q_1(t')K(t-t')\}$; with matching
> steady-state periods or vanishing all-history boundary terms it integrates to
> $W=0$. What vanishes is the symmetric derivative acting on the kernel. The
> derivative acting on the product does not.
> In frequency space, $\widetilde G_{\mathrm{sym}}=\cos(\omega R)$ is real and
> $\max|\operatorname{Im}\widetilde G_{\mathrm{sym}}|=0$; meanwhile
> $\tfrac12\widetilde\Delta=i\sin(\omega R)$ carries the dissipative part.
>
> $$
> \begin{aligned}
> W(G_{\mathrm{ret}})&=-w,&
> W(G_{\mathrm{adv}})&=+w,\\
> W(G_{\mathrm{sym}})&=0,&
> W(\tfrac12\Delta)&=-w=W(G_{\mathrm{ret}}),
> \end{aligned}
> $$

for a single positive $w$ fixed by the source parameters. The value of $w$ carries no meaning on
its own. What is exact is the zero, and that the two nonzero entries differ only in sign. Both are
reproduced here for Gaussian sources at seven parameter choices, with $|W(G_{\rm sym})|$ and
$|W(G_{\rm ret})+W(G_{\rm adv})|$ at $10^{-16}$, and a kernel skewed away from even gives a
nonzero answer, so the check is not vacuous. The retarded value alone is quoted to no digits
here, because a number for it means nothing without the source width, separation and $R$ it was
computed at; the structural statement is
what the argument uses and it is exact. The exact zero for the selected symmetric kernel comes
from the integration-by-parts argument. Nothing was rounded to reach it.

For a complete steady-state response that is this reciprocal even combination, the absorptive
operator vanishes and so does **total cycle-averaged secular work**. This does not set
instantaneous work, individual-source transfers or outgoing-wave amplitude to zero; nor does it
include an independent homogeneous or environmental response. The commutator $G_{\mathrm{ret}}$
$-$ $G_{\mathrm{adv}}$ identifies the part the selected classical response omits, but symmetrising
one kernel does not prove the full field theory classical. Weisberg and Huang report the
observed-to-GR orbital-decay ratio for PSR B1913+16 as 0.9983 $\pm$ 0.0016 [8]; the zero-damping
complete-response implementation is excluded by it.

**A.10 The law is the KMS condition off the surface, and how far the horizon ratio carries.** §3's
law, derived, and how far it carries. The objection to meet first is that the reciprocal law is
definitional: it holds for any involution and any positive kernel, so on $B$ it constrains
nothing. That objection is right, and it is right off $B$ as well. With $R(x,y)=W(x,JP_\perp
y)/W(x,Jy)$ and $P_\perp^2=\mathbb 1$, substitution alone gives $R(x,P_\perp y)=R(x,y)^{-1}$ at
every separation, so the identity carries no information anywhere and no temperature enters it.
What carries the argument off $B$ is a match and never the identity, and the boost separation that
has not yet dropped out is what leaves room for one. A.6 derives the image kernel $G_{\rm
img}\propto\operatorname{sech}^2(Ht/2)$ from the geometry of the antipodal map on the central
worldline, with no temperature anywhere in that derivation; the form is quoted here because what
follows uses only the form. Requiring that geometric object to be a thermal half-period shift
$W(t-i\beta/2)$ of the Bunch-Davies kernel then fixes $\beta$. The match holds to
$4\times10^{-17}$ at $\beta=2\pi/H$ and fails by $0.115$ ten per cent away on either side, a
factor $2.6\times10^{15}$ worse and symmetric in the two directions. Minimising the match itself
over $\beta$, with $2\pi/H$ nowhere in the search, returns $6.2831853072$; minimising the
$\alpha^2=1$ condition instead returns $6.2832000828$. Two conditions, two minimisations, one
temperature. But it holds equally at every odd multiple $\beta=(2j+1)2\pi/H$, because
$\sinh(z-i(2j+1)\pi/2)=(-1)^{j+1}i\cosh z$ and squaring removes the sign, so the match alone
determines $\beta$ only modulo odd multiples; checked to $3\times10^{-16}$ at $j=0,1,2,3$ and
failing at $25.6$ at every even multiple. What picks the fundamental is primitivity, which the KMS
condition already contains: a function of period $2\pi/H$ has period $3\times2\pi/H$ as well, and
the KMS temperature is the primitive period. No match occurs below the fundamental, which is what
primitivity requires and is checked. The temperature is returned by that match and is not
inserted. Only then does $\alpha^2=1$ read $W(t-i\beta)=W(t)$, which is the KMS condition; and we
note plainly that this last step is a consistency check rather than a derivation, since
$\operatorname{csch}^2$ has period $2\pi i/H$ however it was obtained. In the frequency domain the
same statement is that $e^{\beta E/2}$ squares to the KMS factor $e^{\beta E}$: the fold's map is
the square root of the KMS transformation, and the horizon temperature is returned rather than
assumed.

None of that is new physics and it is not offered as any. $\alpha$ contains the wedge reflection,
which is the boost through imaginary angle $\pi$, so $\alpha^2=1$ says a $2\pi$ Euclidean rotation
is the identity, and obtaining a horizon temperature from smoothness is standard. The point is defensive and stands anyway: the fold's $\mathbb Z_2$ *is* that condition, so the law's
triviality on $B$ is a constraint degenerating rather than a constraint missing, and the
ninety-degree equality is the boundary value of something that fixes a temperature. What none of
it touches is the reduction to $W_B$ itself, which is the step this appendix rests on and does not
prove. A.12 carries the rest of what the law reaches.

**How far the horizon ratio carries.** A.6 obtains $G_\alpha/G_J\to\tan^2(\gamma/2)$ at the de
Sitter horizon for the conformally coupled scalar in the Bunch-Davies state. This section
separates the part of that result which belongs to horizons from the part which belongs to that
spacetime and that state, because the two have been conflated, including by us.

Let $B$ be the bifurcation surface of a bifurcate Killing horizon. The wedge reflection $J$ fixes
$B$ pointwise; $P_\perp$ is a free involutive isometry of $B$ and moves it; $\alpha=J\circ
P_\perp$. The status of $P_\perp$ differs between the two settings and the difference matters. On
de Sitter nothing is chosen: $\alpha$ is the antipodal involution of the cover, $J$ is fixed by
the wedge-reflection theorems, and $P_\perp$ is whatever their composition makes it, the
$(-1)^\ell$ parity of the cosmology paper's §2.1. At a black hole the geometry selects it as well.

A free involutive isometry of a round $B$ is an element of $O(3)$ with no $+1$ eigenvalue, so it
is $-\mathrm{Id}$ and nothing else. Kerr's $B$ is not round, but it depends on $\theta$ only
through $\cos^2\theta$, and its latitudes are separated by the pair of Gaussian curvature and
circumference at every spin up to $a/M=0.998$, which fixes its isometry group at
$O(2)\times\mathbb Z_2$; of that group's eight involutions exactly one is free. Those two cases
exhaust the stationary vacuum holes in four dimensions, and the count stays one beyond them:
charge moves $r_+$ and nothing the argument uses, and on the round $S^{D-2}$ the eigenvalue
argument runs unchanged at every dimension, which is the range these contact results are stated
over. A $B$ with no equatorial symmetry admits
no free involutive isometry at all, so the failure mode is absence and never ambiguity, and
$P_\perp$ is an input nowhere the fold is applied. With both
arguments at $B$ the boost separation drops out of both kernels, and

$$
\frac{G_\alpha}{G_J}\longrightarrow\frac{W_B(x,P_\perp y)}{W_B(x,y)},
$$

the state's own correlator restricted to $B$, at the reflected separation over the direct one.
This uses no metric on $B$, no choice of state and nothing about the ambient spacetime beyond the
near-horizon form. It is why each kernel is singular exactly where its own map brings the
arguments together, and that much is general.

This reduction rests on three properties and nothing else, and the rest of this appendix leans on it. Expand both kernels in transverse modes on $B$, carrying two
labels rather than one, since assuming the modes diagonalise the state's kernel would be a
symmetry assumption about the state and we have just said there is none:

$$
G_J=\sum_{I,K} R_{IK}(\Delta\tau)\,Y_I(x)Y_K(y)^*,\qquad
G_\alpha=\sum_{I,K} R_{IK}(\Delta\tau)\,Y_I(x)Y_K(P_\perp y)^*.
$$

Since $P_\perp$ is an isometry of $B$ it acts on the transverse labels alone, and since it
commutes with the boost, $R_{IK}(\Delta\tau)$ is one common matrix for both kernels, whatever it
is. At $\Delta\tau=0$, which is both points on $B$, $R_{IK}(0)$ is exactly what defines the
restriction $W_B$, so the ratio is $W_B(x,P_\perp y)/W_B(x,y)$ and no diagonality was used to get
there. Checked on a dense random kernel with more than half its weight off the diagonal: the
reciprocal law holds to $2\times10^{-16}$ on it, while the diagonal truncation of the same kernel
gives a visibly different ratio, so the two expansions are not the same object and the narrower
one was never needed. Specialise to a round $B$ and the statement becomes visible by hand. There
the antipodal insertion is $(-1)^\ell$, and the Legendre polynomials obey $P_\ell(-x)=(-1)^\ell
P_\ell(x)$, so the two signs are the same sign and cancel term by term: the reflected sum at
$\gamma$ is the direct sum at $\pi-\gamma$ for every boost profile whatever, and three well-chosen
ones prove less than that. Four profiles, one of them random, confirm it to $9\times10^{-15}$,
which is roundoff. The round case is the easy illustration and not the content: the argument above
used no maximal symmetry, no round $B$, no particular state and no closed form.

The same check makes plain how little the identity itself demands. It holds on modes of the wrong
parity and on involutions of $B$ that are not free, because it follows from $P_\perp^2=\mathbb 1$
and nothing else. Freeness and parity carry the geometry downstream. The law itself needs neither.
Of the three properties the third carries the content, namely that the $\Delta\tau\to0$
restriction exists at all; for a Hadamard state it does away from transverse coincidence, which is
where the ratio is evaluated, but a restriction to a codimension-two surface is not automatic and
we do not prove it. The first two are geometry.

It is a limit and not an identity, and the boost dependence does not cancel away from $B$. At
$\gamma=110^\circ$ and $rH=0.9999$ the $B$-form is already off by $3.1\times10^{-4}$ at zero
boost, and the error grows as $\cosh\Delta t$ from there, reaching a per cent by $\Delta
t\simeq4.8$.

That reduction has a rate, which matters because any horizon calculation is done at small but nonzero lapse. The boost separation enters both kernels only through the coefficient
$\sqrt{(1-r_1^2)(1-r_2^2)}=N_1N_2$, so it does not cancel *between* them; it vanishes in each
separately as $B$ is approached. What is left is then first order in $N^2$, and its constant has a
closed form, which the measured table was sampling,

$$\frac{G_\alpha/G_J}{\tan^2(\gamma/2)}-1\;=\;\frac{2\cos\gamma\,(\cosh\Delta t+1)}{\sin^2\gamma}\,N^2+O(N^4),$$

with $\Delta t$ the static-patch time separation. The measured constants at $rH=0.9999$ are what
that formula says they should be: $12.1$ at $60^\circ$ and the same $12.1$ at $120^\circ$, because
$|\cos\gamma|/\sin^2\gamma$ is even about a right angle; $61.8$ at $150^\circ$; $517$ at
$170^\circ$, where $\sin^2\gamma$ is running out; and exactly zero at $90^\circ$. Fixing the one
free boost from the $60^\circ$ entry alone reproduces the other three to better than three per
cent, with nothing left to adjust. With $N^2\simeq d^2/16M^2$ for a proper distance $d$ above a
Schwarzschild horizon, a per cent needs $d=0.4M/\sqrt c$, which is $0.058$ of a Schwarzschild
radius at the moderate-angle coefficient, $0.025$ at $150^\circ$ and $0.009$ at $170^\circ$ where
the fold kernel's own pole is being approached. Moderate angles are the forgiving case and the
near-antipodal ones the tight case, by about a factor of two and then again by three. So the
reduction is a controlled limit with a calculable correction, and the ninety-degree equality is
exact at every radius and at every boost rather than first order small. A right angle sends
$\cos\gamma$ to zero, which removes the transverse term from $Z_J$ and $Z_\alpha$ at once, so the
two kernels are the same function and their ratio is one identically. That angle alone carries no
truncation error, which is a second reason to lean on it.

One more statement is general, and it was missed because the earlier work asked for a value rather
than a law. Write $R(x,y)=W_B(x,P_\perp y)/W_B(x,y)$. Replacing $y$ by $P_\perp y$ and using
$P_\perp^2=1$,

$$
R(x,y)\,R(x,P_\perp y)=1,
$$

identically. The proof is that displayed line and nothing else enters it, so the law holds for
every state, every correlator, every metric on $B$ and every free involution, since it uses only
that $P_\perp$ is one. On a round $B$ with an isotropic state $P_\perp$ carries $\gamma$ to
$\pi-\gamma$ and the law reads $R(\pi-\gamma)R(\gamma)=1$, so $\log R$ is odd about $\gamma=\pi/2$
whenever $R>0$. The law is exact by the displayed line, so the numerics that accompany it confirm
an implementation and not a mathematical fact; they are reported at better than $2\times10^{-14}$
across ten positive decreasing profiles at four radii and four hundred angles, and four thousand
random configurations at unequal radii and nonzero time separation, in that spirit. The oddness
needs $\log R$ to be a function it makes sense to expand: $R$ has a zero at one coincidence and a
pole at the other, so $\log R$ diverges at both ends, and it lies in $L^2(\sin\gamma\,d\gamma)$
exactly when those divergences are logarithmic. Hadamard states give power-law singularities and
therefore $\log R\sim\log\gamma$, which is square-integrable; a state with an essential
singularity there would not be, and the multipole statement below would not apply to it.

Three consequences follow that the pointwise statement did not supply. The unit value at
$\gamma=\pi/2$ is the fixed point of the law, so it needs no isotropy argument and no state.
$\tan^2(\gamma/2)$ satisfies the law, which is why the withdrawn universality looked right for as
long as it did: what is universal is the functional equation, and the conformal scalar's closed
form is one solution of it. And the law fixes a sign across the whole surface rather than at one
angle. For any correlator decreasing in separation, $R<1$ wherever $x$ lies nearer $y$ than
$P_\perp y$ and $R>1$ where it does not, so the fold suppresses correlations on the near side of
the involution's equidistant locus and enhances them on the far side, with the crossing exactly on
that locus. Magnitude belongs to the state and the spacetime; the sign and the locus do not.

How far the law reaches, what it measures when a state falls short of its own fold image, and what
it says about the closed-de Sitter overlap's branch weights are taken up separately in A.12, so
that this appendix can return to the question it set: how much of that value survives.

The value at a given angle is not. On a round $B$ with an isotropic state the ratio is
$G(\pi-\gamma)/G(\gamma)$, and three features of it hold for every such state: it vanishes like
$\gamma^2$, it equals exactly one at $\gamma=\pi/2$, and it has a $(\pi-\gamma)^{-2}$ pole. The
first and third are the Hadamard singularity at each coincidence; the second needs only that a
point at $\pi/2$ from $x$ has its antipode at $\pi/2$ as well. Nothing beyond those three is
universal. Three profiles with the same short-distance behaviour at both coincidences give 3.00,
4.00 and 2.75 at $\gamma=2\pi/3$: the chordal form $1/(1-\cos\gamma)$, the arclength form
$1/\gamma^2$, and a chordal form carrying an asymmetric finite-separation factor (log-slopes
$+2.000$ and $-2.000$ at the two ends for all three, and unity at $\pi/2$ to twelve digits).
$\tan^2(\gamma/2)$ is the first of them, exact on de Sitter because that state's two-point
function is exactly $1/(1-Z)$ at every separation, which is a consequence of maximal symmetry and
not of the horizon.

It is therefore not established for the Hartle-Hawking state on Schwarzschild. The near-horizon
route obtains it there by expanding about $r=2M$ to ${\rm Rindler}\times
S^2$ and using a flat transverse chord; the chord approximates the separation on a curved sphere
only at small angle, so that route does not reach the angles the law is read at, and it is not a
derivation. What survives on Schwarzschild is the general ratio above and the three
universal features, all of which $\tan^2(\gamma/2)$ happens to satisfy.

Kerr keeps the structure and loses the closed form. Its bifurcation surface carries

$$
ds^2=\rho_+^2\,d\theta^2+\frac{(r_+^2+a^2)^2\sin^2\theta}{\rho_+^2}\,d\phi^2,
\qquad \rho_+^2=r_+^2+a^2\cos^2\theta,
$$

which is axisymmetric and even in $\cos\theta$, so
$P_\perp:(\theta,\phi)\mapsto(\pi-\theta,\phi+\pi)$ is an exact isometry, free and involutive,
checked to $1.8\times10^{-15}$. The general ratio therefore applies. But $B$ is squashed, with
$g_{\theta\theta}({\rm pole})/g_{\theta\theta}({\rm equator})=1+a^2/r_+^2$, equal to 1.024, 1.072,
1.167, 1.393 and 1.753 at $a/M=0.3$, 0.5, 0.7, 0.9 and 0.99 and to unity at $a=0$. Isotropy fails,
and with it any closed form and the unit value *at that angle*. The reciprocal law survives,
because it needs only the involution and Kerr keeps that, and it still holds to $6\times10^{-15}$
at $a/M=0.3$, $0.7$ and $0.95$. What the law then locates is the set where $W_B(x,P_\perp
y)=W_B(x,y)$, and identifying that set with the curve equidistant from $y$ and $P_\perp y$
requires the state's restriction to $B$ to depend on those points only through geodesic distance
there. Given that, the locus is a deformed curve in place of a great circle, and six unrelated
distance-only profiles return $R=1$ to twelve digits at the same point on it. That check confirms
the geometry and not the state.

State dependence, though, turns out to be cheaper than we first allowed. $P_\perp$ factors as a
rotation by $\pi$ about the axis composed with the equatorial reflection
$\theta\mapsto\pi-\theta$, and Kerr has both symmetries, so an axisymmetric equatorially symmetric
restriction is $P_\perp$-invariant without that having to be assumed; kernels built from the
invariants such a state may depend on are invariant to $10^{-15}$ over $2\times10^4$ random pairs.
Then $W_B(x,P_\perp y)=W_B(P_\perp x,y)$ and the locus where $R=1$ exists and is well defined: it
is the set on which $P_\perp x$ and $x$ correlate equally with $y$.

What does fail is its identification with the *geodesic* equidistant curve, and the reason is a
count rather than a computation. On a round sphere $SO(3)$ acts transitively on pairs at a fixed
separation, so the space of pairs modulo isometry is one-dimensional and every invariant is
necessarily a function of distance. Spin breaks that group to the rotations about the axis and the
equatorial reflection, the quotient becomes three-dimensional in $(\theta_1,\theta_2,\Delta\phi)$,
and distance is one function on it whose level sets are surfaces. An invariant kernel is another
such function, and nothing makes it constant on those surfaces. So spin moves the locus off
the equidistant curve, and computing where it goes needs the state and not only the metric. That
is a smaller gap than the one we recorded before, and it is the one that remains. The departure is
switched on by spin alone and vanishes identically for a non-rotating hole. A.13 shows it is not a
separate fact.

**A.11 Transparent matching is adopted, and at a codimension-one interface the corner term is the
only seam carrying no scale.** §4's boundary condition, and why it is adopted. The result first,
and what it is not. One thing holds and it is narrow. Among quadratic boundary terms at the
specified codimension-one interface, the corner term is the one that can carry a dimensionless
coefficient and still act. That is not a classification of actions on $B$ itself, which is
codimension two, where $\int_B d^2x\,\phi^2$ already has a dimensionless coefficient for a
four-dimensional scalar; the concession below says so, and the opening may not claim more than the
concession allows. Within that action $r$ cannot depend on frequency, and unitarity fixes the
matching to $r(\kappa)=(1-\kappa^2)/(1+\kappa^2)$, $t(\kappa)=2\kappa/(1+\kappa^2)$.

What does *not* hold is a derivation of $\kappa=1$ from regularity. Three routes to one are set out
and closed below, two of them ours: an argument from finite relative entropy, which is circular; an
argument from an image singularity at spacelike separation, which fails in both accountings of the
mode sum and fails the second time by picking out the transparent seam as the worst case rather than
the only clean one; and the fold's own sheet symmetry, which the whole family satisfies. What does
select the transparent point is variational and soft: it maximises the entanglement across the seam,
and a five per cent reflection retains $99.7$ per cent of that. So $\kappa=1$ is adopted, with the
corner term's uniqueness among scale-free seams behind it and a maximum rather than an exclusion.

There is a third route, and it is the one tried first, so it is closed here. Demand that the fold be a symmetry of the seam, on the ground that
the seam is the fold locus and cannot be less symmetric than the map defining it. That demand
holds at every $\kappa$ and therefore selects nothing, and here is why in one line. The fold does
two things: it exchanges the sheets, and it reverses time orientation. The corner term is
antisymmetric in the sheet labels, so the exchange flips its sign; it is first order in a time
derivative, so the reversal flips it back. Those two flips cancel for any coefficient, which is
checked directly on profiles with no parity of their own, with each half of the fold applied alone
as the control and each half flipping the sign as it must.

A two-by-two scattering ansatz gives the opposite answer, that fold covariance forces zero
reflection, and the ansatz is what is wrong: it follows the exchange of sheets and drops the
exchange of incoming and outgoing channels that comes with time reversal. The family itself is
suggestive enough to make the mistake tempting, since $r=(1-\kappa^2)/(1+\kappa^2)$ and
$t=2\kappa/(1+\kappa^2)$ are the tangent half-angle substitution, so with $\kappa=\tan(\theta/2)$
the seam family is the circle $(r,t)=(\cos\theta,\sin\theta)$ and the transparent member sits at
$\theta=\pi/2$, the fixed point of $r\mapsto-r$. A distinguished member that is the fixed point of
an involution of its own family looks like a member a symmetry ought to pick, and no symmetry of
the fold does.

Symmetry is not the only thing the same action has to say, and what it says next does select. Vary
the two bulk actions and the corner term together at the join, with $\phi_L$ on $x<0$ and $\phi_R$
on $x>0$ and opposite outward normals. The matching conditions are $\partial_x\phi_L(0)=\kappa\dot
q_2$ and $\partial_x\phi_R(0)=\kappa\dot q_1$, and the scattering matrix that follows is

$$S=\begin{pmatrix} r & t\\ -t & r\end{pmatrix},\qquad r=\frac{1-\kappa^2}{1+\kappa^2},\qquad
t=\frac{2\kappa}{1+\kappa^2},$$

a rotation with $\det S=1$, and not the symmetric matrix the two-by-two argument above assumes.
Under the fold law $E\overline{S}E=S^{\dagger}$, with $E$ the exchange of sheets and the dagger
carrying the exchange of incoming and outgoing channels, that matrix is invariant at every
$\kappa$, which is the same no-selection result arrived at from the matrix side.

Now take the two fluxes across the join, from the same matching conditions. The energy defect
$T_{tx}(0^-)-T_{tx}(0^+)$ is identically zero at every $\kappa$: the seam conserves energy for
free. The momentum defect is not,

$$T_{xx}(0^-)-T_{xx}(0^+)=\tfrac12\left(1-\kappa^2\right)\left(\dot q_1^2-\dot q_2^2\right),$$

so for general data the seam pushes, and the push vanishes for arbitrary data at exactly
$|\kappa|=1$. That is the transparent member. The coefficient is $3/8$ at $\kappa=\tfrac12$ and
$-3/2$ at $\kappa=2$, and a self-coupled matching in place of the cross-coupled one gives
$\tfrac12(1+\kappa^2)(\dot q_1^2-\dot q_2^2)$, which never vanishes, so the selection belongs to
the cross coupling rather than to the bookkeeping.

What that momentum is, written in the variables the cosmology paper's §2.1 splits fields into, takes a line, because it is not what the raw expression looks like. Putting $q_1=\Phi_c+\Phi_q/2$
and $q_2=\Phi_c-\Phi_q/2$ into the defect gives

$$T_{xx}(0^-)-T_{xx}(0^+)=\left(1-\kappa^2\right)\dot\Phi_c\,\dot\Phi_q,$$

the classical velocity times the quantum one. So the seam's force is a classical-quantum cross
term. It vanishes when the two sheets agree, because then $\Phi_q=0$; it vanishes when the
classical field is static; it vanishes identically at the transparent point; and it has no
long-range tail, since it exists only where the sheets meet. Whatever else the fold does, this is
the one interaction in the fold that is not gravity and does not reach.

An interface that absorbs no momentum is one whose stress tensor is continuous across it, which is
what distinguishes a topological interface from a material one. So the family has exactly one
topological member and the paper adopts it. That is not a derivation of $\kappa=1$ from the fold,
and it is worth being exact about the difference: the fold does not imply that its seam is
topological. What is established is that if the seam is topological then $\kappa=1$ uniquely, so
the adoption rests on a stated physical premise with a name rather than on a choice of number. Reject the premise and one sentence goes; the rest stands.

What that gives is a better statement of the gap than the one this appendix had. The adoption of
$\kappa=1$ is not a hole in the symmetry analysis waiting to be filled. No symmetry of the fold can fix the coefficient, so it has to come from dynamics or from a completion, and
the corner term being ultraviolet-incomplete is then the whole of the difficulty rather than one
obstacle among several.

So transparency is adopted here, with A.11 saying what deriving it would take. The null Kerr
implementation the rest of the paper uses is unaffected, since it only ever needed a
frequency-independent reflectivity and a stated choice of it. Recovering a derivation would need:
the covariance of the actual seam action, with both incident channels and its actual domain,
computed rather than modelled by a one-dimensional image ansatz.

What follows is how that was reached and what remains open, because the corner term is
ultraviolet-incomplete and four earlier routes to the coefficient failed. Those failures say
something about the seam; they are not verdicts on the attempts. §4 records that three routes to
fixing the seam's one free coefficient are closed: symmetry and constraint closure admit the whole
family, the cover's vanishing $H^1$ leaves no period to quantise against, and the target's trivial
$\pi_1$ supplies no field content. We attempted a fourth, an external measurement, and it fails
for a reason that says something about the seam, and nothing about the measurement.

Parametrise the seam by $\kappa$ in the corner term $(\kappa/2)q^TJ\dot q$, where $q=(q_1,q_2)$
are the two sheets' field values at the join and $J$ is the antisymmetric unit. The reflection and
transmission that term produces are not quoted from anywhere; they follow from it. Varying
$S_c=(\kappa/2)\int dt\,(q_1\dot q_2-q_2\dot q_1)$ and integrating by parts in $t$ leaves
$\kappa(\dot q_2\,\delta q_1-\dot q_1\,\delta q_2)$, which against the bulk variation
$-\phi'\delta\phi$ gives the matching conditions $\phi_L'(0)=\pm\kappa\dot q_2$ and
$\phi_R'(0)=-\kappa\dot q_1$. With $\phi=e^{ikx}+re^{-ikx}$ on the left and $te^{ikx}$ on the
right at frequency $\omega=k$, these read $1-r=\kappa t$ and $t=\kappa(1+r)$, whose unique
solution is

$$
r(\kappa)=\frac{1-\kappa^2}{1+\kappa^2},\qquad
t(\kappa)=\frac{2\kappa}{1+\kappa^2},\qquad r^2+t^2=1.
$$

The other sign gives $r=(1+\kappa^2)/(1-\kappa^2)$, which is a different branch altogether and
fails unitarity: at $\kappa=\tfrac12$ it returns $r=5/3$ and $t=4/3$, so $r^2+t^2=41/9$, and the
violation grows with $\kappa$. Unitarity therefore selects the sign, where a convention would have
left it free. A full two-channel scattering matrix with both bulk actions, normals and incoming
channels specified is not constructed here. Both expressions and the unitarity are verified
against the matching condition at $\kappa=0,\tfrac14,\tfrac12,1,2,4$ to machine precision. So
$\kappa=1$ is the transparent point, $r=0$ and $t=1$, and $\kappa=0$ and $\kappa\to\infty$ are the
two perfect mirrors.

Because $\kappa$ is dimensionless and the corner term carries no other scale, $r$ and $t$ cannot
depend on frequency. That is the whole problem. A seam reflecting with the same amplitude at every
frequency, including arbitrarily short wavelengths, is not a physical interface: real interfaces
become transparent in the ultraviolet. The corner term as written therefore needs a scale it does
not have.

Any quantity built from it shows the symptom. Summing the two scattering channels at two ports of
separation $2a$ in the one-dimensional model of gives a ratio of image to local kernel of the form
$t(C+r)/(1+rC)$, where

$$
C(a)=\frac{\int dk\,\cos 2ka/2\omega_k}{\int dk\,1/2\omega_k}.
$$

Its numerator converges while its denominator diverges logarithmically, so $C$ is not a property
of the port geometry at all: it falls steadily as the cutoff is lifted, running $0.958$, $0.603$,
$0.497$, $0.441$, $0.371$, $0.315$ at $a=0.02$ for $k_{\max}$ from $20$ to $2\times10^4$. In the
limit the cutoff is removed $C\to0$ and the ratio tends to $tr$, which vanishes at $\kappa=1$
where A.6's established central-worldline result has magnitude one. The local kernel in that model
is itself logarithmically divergent with no seam at all, while A.6's $A_0=1/12\pi^2$ is finite,
which shows the one-dimensional model was never a model of A.6's object: a force-noise kernel is
not a field two-point function.

No measurement protocol is available in which two even/odd measurements at different port
separations were said to fix $\kappa$. They cannot, because the quantity being varied was set by
the regulator rather than by the separation.

The exercise establishes a condition on the seam action. It does not fix a value for the
coefficient, and no regularity condition does. That is computed here, because the route is the obvious one and has to be closed where it is looked for.

The route argues that a constant reflectivity puts an image term in the two-point function which
diverges between mirror-placed points, that no Hadamard state is singular at spacelike separation,
and so that $r$ must vanish. Two accountings of the mode sum are available and neither supports it.
Sum both incident channels of the two-port, as completeness of the mode basis requires, and the
cross-seam image coefficient is $rt+tr'$; unitarity of $S=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}r&t\\
t&r'\end{smallmatrix}\right)$ forces $r'=-r$ whenever $t$ is nonzero, so that coefficient is
identically zero at every $\kappa$, checked by reading it off the mode functions at
$\kappa=\tfrac14,\tfrac12,1,2,4$ and returning $10^{-17}$ each time. Taking $r'=+r$ instead
leaves $2rt$ behind, which is the plant that makes the zero mean something. Project the mode space
instead, as a $\mathbb Z_2$ quotient does, and the coefficient becomes $(t^2-r^2)/2$: nonzero, and
*largest at the transparent point*, where it is exactly $\tfrac12$ against $-0.2785$ at
$\kappa=\tfrac14$ and zero at $|r|=|t|$. So in the first accounting nothing is singular at any
$\kappa$, and in the second the transparent seam is the worst offender, where the argument needs it
to be the only clean one. The conclusion does not depend on which accounting is right.

The reflection term's own locus is out of reach in any case. It is singular at $x+y=0$ and has
same-side support, and two points on the same side give a sum of one sign, which never reaches
zero. Where the term exists it cannot bite, and where it could bite it is absent. That is the
ordinary structure of a point defect on a line, reflection terms having same-side support and
transmission terms opposite-side support.

One more version of the route looks different and is not, so it is recorded here too. Asking for
*finite relative entropy against the transparent case* also gives $r(k)\to0$ and so $\kappa=1$,
and it is circular: finiteness is asked of a comparison whose reference is the seam the argument
selects, and since two constant reflectivities differ at arbitrarily short distance every pair in
the family has infinite relative entropy against every other. "Finite relative entropy against
$X$" selects $X$ for any $X$ here, and therefore selects nothing.

One criterion does select the transparent point, and it is variational rather than a regularity
condition, which is what keeps it in play once the regularity routes are closed. A free-fermion chain with a defect
bond is the same two-port scattering problem, with transmission $T=4t_d^2/(1+t_d^2)^2$, in a
setting where entanglement is computable exactly. Total entropy across the defect is *not* a
function of $T$: a strong bond builds a dimer across the cut whose local contribution swamps the
transmission-dependent part, and $t_d=0.5$ and $t_d=2$ give entropies differing by more than a
factor of two at identical $T$.

The coefficient of $\log L$ is a function of $T$ and of nothing else, established below.
A bond defect and a site defect tuned to the same transmission perturb the chain differently and
return the same coefficient to within $0.63$ per cent, at $T=0.95$, $0.88$, $0.64$ and $0.30$; the
$T=0.88$ pair is $0.15363$ against $0.15460$. Defects at *different* transmissions differ by tens
of per cent, so the agreement is not an artefact of the fit. The absolute coefficient depends on
the chain's boundary conditions and on how many cut points the entropy counts; what is comparable
between two defects is the percentage, and that is what the claim uses. These are finite-size fits
and not a proof of universality. That coefficient is maximal at $T=1$, with both neighbours below
it. Its symmetry under $t_d\to1/t_d$ is inherited from the symmetry of $T$ under that map, so it
is the seam's rapidity inversion seen again and not a second fact. The transparent seam is
therefore the one that maximises the entanglement between the two sides, which for a framework
claiming the sheets are joined is the natural way to say how strongly.

Selection here is soft and should not be oversold: a five per cent reflection retains $99.7$ per
cent of the coefficient, so this identifies the maximum without excluding its neighbourhood, and
nothing above excludes it either. And the lattice shares only the
scattering problem; the dynamics differ, and the entropy computed is the chain's.

The classification reaches further than the constant case, which needs showing because the obvious
objection is that it does not. A seam might be non-linear, with a reflectivity that vanishes at
small field amplitude and switches on above some $A_0$; such a seam is transparent wherever the
field is weak, and it looks like a way of having a reflecting seam without paying for a scale. It is
not, and the reason is that short distance is not small amplitude. Vacuum fluctuations diverge as
the separation closes, the amplitude a probe at separation $s$ samples running as $1/2\pi s$, so for
any finite $A_0$ the seam is switched on below $s_*=1/2\pi A_0$ and its reflectivity tends to one
as the separation shrinks further: $0.99$ at $s_*/10$ and $0.9999$ at $s_*/100$, independently of
$A_0$. So $A_0$ is a scale and the seam is a scaled seam whatever the field-dependence looks like at
weak field, which is the case already covered: it falls outside the corner term and owes its own
matching law.

Scale dependence is real and runs opposite to the intuition that suggests it. A seam reflecting at
large amplitude is transparent in the infrared and reflecting in the ultraviolet. It is not
transparent where we can look while active near a singularity. That is the expected sign for a
boundary coupling carrying a dimensionful scale, which is irrelevant in the infrared and grows
toward the ultraviolet, and it is why an ultraviolet condition catches it. It is also why no such
seam shows in a waveform. Ringdown sits at $0.374\,c^3/2\pi GM$, which is $41$ orders of magnitude
below a Planck seam scale, $37$ below a grand-unified one, $30$ below the $492$ PeV mass of the
cosmology paper's §3.1 and $24$ below a TeV. That gap does not show that a ringdown cannot see
whatever a seam does at short distance; the inference runs the wrong way.

Write the response as $r(\omega/\Lambda_P)$, with $\Lambda_P$ the Planck scale or any other
microscopic one in its place; the letter is subscripted throughout this appendix to keep it clear
of the cosmological constant, which is $\Lambda$ everywhere else in this paper. Astrophysical
ringdown sits at $\omega/\Lambda_P\simeq4\times10^{-40}$ for $10M_\odot$ and $4\times10^{-48}$ for
$10^9M_\odot$, which is the deep *infrared* of that function and not its ultraviolet. A condition
forcing $r\to0$ at large argument constrains nothing there: $r(x)=e^{-x}$ obeys it and equals one
to forty significant figures at these arguments. A microscopic scale therefore does not make a
seam invisible at ringdown, and no statement about astrophysical waveforms follows from an
ultraviolet condition alone.

The implementation adopted here is transparent at every frequency by choice, which is a different
matter and is unaffected.

What must still be granted is that the seam state has to be Hadamard at all. That is the standard
admissibility condition, required for the stress tensor to renormalise, but it is an assumption here; declining it recovers a free $\kappa$, and everything else above is geometry.

It is not the case that no choice of $\kappa$ satisfies the ultraviolet condition. That is false:
$\kappa=1$ satisfies it, trivially and uniquely, and $10^5$ members sampled between
$\kappa=e^{-5}$ and $e^{5}$ return exactly one with $|r|<10^{-14}$.

*The corner term is not one choice among many.* The three steps need a constant reflectivity,
which the action supplies by carrying no scale of its own, so the question is which boundary terms
on $B$ can carry a dimensionless coefficient at all. At quadratic order there is one. A scalar has
dimension $(d-2)/2$ and a boundary density must have dimension $d-1$, so at $d=4$ only terms with
exactly one derivative qualify, namely $q\,\dot q$ and $q\,\nabla q$. The second is
$\nabla(q^2)/2$ and integrates to zero on a closed bifurcation surface. The symmetric part of the
first is $\tfrac12\,d(q^2)/dt$ and drops out of the matching condition, which we check directly;
the antisymmetric pairing does not and is the corner term. So on a codimension-one matching
surface "a seam carrying no scale of its own" and "the corner term" name the same object at this
order. On $B$ itself, which is codimension two, the count runs the other way: $g\int_B
d^2x\,\phi^2$ has a dimensionless $g$, and the prior objection to the counting above is correct
and is not answered here. Computing the reflectivity of a one-dimensional delta potential does not
answer it: a codimension-two defect does not scatter like a delta in one normal coordinate, needs
its own regularisation, and can generate logarithmic scales of its own. The rescue is withdrawn.
Relatedly, $r(\omega)\to0$ is not by itself Hadamard regularity, since removing a pointwise
divergence does not remove a wavefront singularity, and a proper microlocal treatment is not
attempted here.

So the statement to make is narrow and is stated narrowly: the dimensional counting above concerns
a specified codimension-one interface and does not classify every action that could live on the
bifurcation surface. What the three steps of this appendix need, and use, is a reflectivity that
does not depend on frequency; what they do not supply is a classification of seams.

So the seam weight is not a free parameter of *this* action. Ordinary absorbing Kerr dynamics is
what the corner term gives rather than what was chosen for it. The argument has three limits. Its
steps use a constant reflectivity, the image term that reflectivity puts in the two-point
function, and the spacelike separation of mirror points, and none of the three mentions the
action. The action enters only by making $r$ frequency-independent, since $\kappa$ is
dimensionless and the corner term carries no other scale. So the argument reaches every scale-free
seam and not only the one written here, and what it says nothing about is a seam carrying a scale
of its own, which §4 already separates out. Hadamard regularity is the standard admissibility
condition for a state in curved spacetime and is assumed here rather than derived. And the
image-term structure is generic to a reflecting interface, which is why it survives A.11's own
warning that the one-dimensional model is not a model of A.6's object: what is used is the
scattering parametrisation and the existence of an image term. The model's kernel plays no part.

**A symmetry route, and why it fails.** It is natural to ask whether the fold's own identification
of the sheets fixes $\kappa$ without any ultraviolet input. Writing $\kappa=e^{u}$, the family's
transfer matrix is $\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\cosh u&\sinh u\\\sinh u&\cosh
u\end{smallmatrix}\right)$ to $1.8\times10^{-15}$, so seams in series add rapidities,
$\kappa\to1/\kappa$ is the group inverse, and reversing the crossing sends $u\to-u$. Requiring the
seam to look the same from either sheet would then give $u=0$ uniquely. It does not, and the
reason is computable. The two channels are the sheets, so the exchange is
$E=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1\\1&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)$, which *anticommutes* with the
corner term's complex structure $J=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1\\-1&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)$.
Under the exchange together with the time reversal the fold's map carries, $q\to Eq$ and $\dot
q\to-E\dot q$, so $(\kappa/2)q^TJ\dot q$ returns to itself for every $\kappa$, verified on $2000$
random $(q,\dot q,\kappa)$. The fold's sheet symmetry is therefore satisfied by the whole family
and selects nothing. Three routes closed, then, and what stands behind the transparent point is the
corner term's uniqueness among scale-free seams together with the entanglement maximum, which
locates it without excluding its neighbourhood.

**The group has an identity, and it is the transparent seam.** The composition law above carries a
consequence the symmetry route passed over. A one-parameter group has an identity element, and
here it is $u=0$, which is $\kappa=1$. The transparent seam is the member that composes with any
other seam and leaves it where it was, and it is the only member that does. That is what having no
seam means as an operation on the theory. So adopting $\kappa=1$ adds no boundary term to the
action, which is the choice the minimal implementation makes wherever else the fold leaves
room, in taking a linear fold map and a Gaussian free state. A reflecting seam is an addition
owing a matching law of its own, and the question to press is what
would require one. None of this derives $\kappa=1$. A theory may add a boundary term, and an
identity element is privileged by minimality and by nothing else. What it settles is where the
burden sits, which the three closed routes above leave unclear on their own. Adopting the identity
is adopting nothing, and that is a lighter thing to have to defend than a choice among a
continuum.

**If the seam carries the horizon's own scale.** The one escape from the above is the one the
argument itself points at: $r$ cannot depend on frequency *because the corner term supplies no
scale*, and a horizon supplies several. A seam matched at the horizon may carry $T_H$, and then
$r=r(\omega/T_H)$ with $r(\infty)=0$ satisfies Hadamard regularity without being transparent. That
is a different action owing its own matching law, but it is not free, because two predictions
follow before any amplitude is chosen. Both $M\omega$ and $MT_H$ are dimensionless functions of
$a/M$ alone, so $\omega/T_H$ depends on spin and not on mass: such a seam reflects identically for
a $10\,M_\odot$ and a $10^{9}\,M_\odot$ hole at the same spin, which is a null test across eight
orders of magnitude available from ringdowns in matching spin bins. And with

$$
\frac{\omega}{T_H}=4\pi\,(M\omega)\,\frac{1+\sqrt{1-a^2}}{\sqrt{1-a^2}},
$$

equal to $8\pi M\omega$ at $a=0$ and diverging at extremality, spinning the hole up pushes the
ringdown to larger argument, where $r$ is smaller. Horizon reflectivity effects are therefore
largest at low spin and vanish as extremality is approached *for a response depending on
$\omega/T_H$ alone*, which §4's corrected co-rotating case is not: there the amplitude rises from
$9.8\times10^{-3}$ at zero spin to $0.444$ at $a=0.9$. An asymptotic falloff does not imply global
monotonicity, and a rotating response can depend on $(\omega-m\Omega_H)/T_H$. What follows is
therefore a property of the assumed response law, for every fall-off; three unrelated ones give
the same monotone ordering. Echo models with a reflecting wall at fixed proper distance predict
the opposite trend, since the light-crossing delay grows as the horizon is approached. The two are
separated by the sign of the trend with spin, with no amplitude required from either. The
quasinormal frequencies themselves are not computed here; the ratio above is carried by $T_H$,
which varies by a factor $8.4$ between $a=0$ and $a=0.998$ while $M\omega$ varies by a factor of a
few, so the trend does not depend on values we have not computed.

Those two predictions are not equally robust, and the difference matters. Mass independence
survives any choice of borrowed scale, because every scale a horizon can lend, the temperature,
the horizon radius, the square root of the area, the angular velocity, the curvature, is the mass
times a function of spin, so the mass cancels identically whichever is taken. The vanishing toward
extremality does not: it belongs to the temperature, which diverges at extremality and changes by
a factor of twelve across the spins sampled, where the radius and the area change by less than two
and give a nearly flat trend instead. Attaching the extremality behaviour
to any horizon scale is therefore wrong. So the spin dependence discriminates among reflecting
implementations built from horizon scales, while the mass independence is common to all of them.

We do not establish that the scale must be the horizon's, and the obvious reply is one word long: Planck. A seam could carry a
scale of external origin, and such a seam is neither transparent nor built from the horizon. It
does not escape the statement, but it changes it from a prohibition into a classification. An
external scale far above the horizon's puts the ringdown at $\omega/\Lambda_P$ between
$4\times10^{-40}$ and $4\times10^{-48}$ across astrophysical masses. The reciprocal quantity
$M\Lambda_P$ runs from $10^{38}$ to $10^{47}$ and is not the argument of the response. These
arguments are the deep infrared, an ultraviolet falloff condition does not constrain a response
there, and the invisibility claim is withdrawn. An external scale *comparable* to the horizon's
does show up, as a dependence on mass at fixed spin, and it would then be measuring that scale
rather than refuting anything; for that to be visible the scale must sit between about $10^{-11}$
and $10^{-20}$ eV, where nothing in particle physics currently sits.

So the statement is a sorting rather than a no-go. Reflectivity mapped across mass and spin
separates three cases. No variation at any mass or spin means a scale-free seam, or any scale far
above the horizon's, and does not distinguish between them. Variation with spin alone means a
horizon scale, which is the fold's own branch, with the trend fixed by which scale it is.
Variation with mass at fixed spin means a scale of external origin near the horizon's. That is a
measurement programme, it requires no amplitude, and unlike the dichotomy it does not rest on a
lemma we cannot prove.

**A.12 How far the reciprocal law reaches.** A.10 establishes the law and its immediate
consequences. This appendix collects what it reaches beyond the bifurcation surface: its algebraic
form, the measure it supplies for a state falling short of its own fold image, and what that
measure says about the branch weights of §2.1.

The law does not need a manifold, and saying so settles how much of this belongs to horizons and
how much to the fold. For a state $\omega$ on an algebra carrying an involutive automorphism
$\sigma$, write $R(A,B)=\omega(A\sigma(B))/\omega(AB)$; the same substitution gives $R(A,\sigma
B)=1/R(A,B)$, needing only $\sigma^2={\rm id}$. Checked on random density matrices with random
involutive automorphisms in eight dimensions over four thousand operator pairs, to
$4\times10^{-14}$, and the check is validated by substituting a generic unitary conjugation for
the involution, which breaks it at order unity. The geometric statement above is one instance.

Once the law is algebraic, the natural question is how far a state falls short of being its own
fold image, and the measure is the relative entropy $S(\omega\,\|\,\omega\circ\sigma)$. Two
properties of it belong to the involution rather than to relative entropy generally. It is
symmetric: relative entropy is invariant under a common automorphism, so
$S(\omega\circ\sigma\,\|\,\omega)=S(\omega\circ\sigma\,\|\,(\omega\circ\sigma)\circ\sigma)=S(\omega\,\|\,\omega\circ\sigma)$
once $\sigma^2={\rm id}$, and four hundred random state-and-involution pairs return an asymmetry
below $3\times10^{-12}$ against a median divergence of $1.07$. And it is second order in the
misalignment: mixing a fold-symmetric state toward a generic one by a fraction $\epsilon$, the
divergence grows as $\epsilon^2$, the log-log slope running $1.913$, $1.953$, $1.980$, $1.993$ and
$1.998$ as $\epsilon$ falls from $10^{-1}$ to $3\times10^{-4}$. It vanishes only at exact fold
symmetry. Fold-symmetric states are therefore a stationary point of this divergence and not merely
its zero set.

Second-order behaviour is the part with consequences, because it fixes how misalignment
accumulates. Independent departures of size $\epsilon$ over $N$ events sum to order $N\epsilon^2$
rather than $(N\epsilon)^2$, so the accumulation is diffusive, and a universe-wide tally comes out
far smaller than a naive addition of amplitudes suggests. We do not compute $\epsilon$ for any
process and nothing in the fold supplies it, so this is a scaling and not a number; turning it
into one means computing the misalignment a definite process produces, for which the branch
decoherence of the closed-de Sitter overlap below is the natural first case and is not attempted
here.

Applying it to a definite process closes that gap. The two branches of §2.1 carry environment
states of overlap $s$; writing the branch-label state as $a\lvert+\rangle\lvert
E_+\rangle+b\lvert-\rangle\lvert E_-\rangle$ and tracing out the environment gives a two-by-two
density matrix with diagonal $(\lvert a\rvert^2,\lvert b\rvert^2)$ and off-diagonal carrying $s$.
Because $\Theta$ is antilinear *and* exchanges the branches, $\Theta\rho\Theta$ is $\rho$ with the
two weights swapped. The misalignment therefore turns on the branch weight and not on the
coherence, and vanishes identically at equal weight, at every overlap.

Decoherence regulates the divergence without causing it: at unequal weight a coherent
superposition is pure, and a pure state has infinite relative entropy against anything off its
support, while the fully decohered value is finite and closed form, $S=(2p-1)\log[p/(1-p)]$, which
is $8(p-\tfrac12)^2$ to leading order and reproduces the coefficient the general argument gave.
And the timing is early: using the closed-de Sitter overlap below's own overlap with $aH=\cosh
Ht$, which returns its quoted $Ht=1.28984$ at $aH=1.95376$, the misalignment has settled onto its
decohered value by the threshold.

That closes a possibility; it opens none. A picture in which many small unobserved events
accumulate a misalignment across cosmic history does not apply here: the measure turns on the
branch weight alone, the weight stops evolving once the branches decohere, and the fold sets it to
one half by CPT. We compute no departure from one half and nothing in the paper supplies one, so
what the measure describes is a property of the initial weights, frozen inside the first e-fold,
and not a quantity that grows.

That last claim was too quick, and checking it reversed it. Equal weight at the fold point does
not propagate. $\Theta$ is antiunitary, and CPT covariance gives $\Theta U\Theta^{-1}=U^{-1}$,
which is not $[\Theta,U]=0$, so a state that is $\Theta$-invariant at the fold point is not
$\Theta$-invariant afterwards: $\Theta\lvert\psi(t)\rangle=\lvert\psi(-t)\rangle$. The branch
imbalance $\langle N\rangle$ is therefore an odd function of time, zero at the fold point and
nonzero away from it, checked to $2\times10^{-15}$ on a Hamiltonian built to satisfy $\Theta
H\Theta^{-1}=H$.
