What the oddness means is more useful than what it removes. $\langle N\rangle$ is neither
conserved nor zero on either sheet; what vanishes exactly is the sum over the pair, since the
mirror sheet's value at its time $t$ is ours at $-t$. The two sheets carry equal and opposite
branch imbalance at every time and the pair carries none. That is a joining of a kind not
otherwise in this paper: not a force between the sheets and not a shared boundary condition, but a
conserved quantity that exists only when both are counted.

It also corrects how the divergence should be read. $S(\omega\,\|\,\omega\circ\sigma)$ is not a
defect accumulating with cosmic time; the two sheets are meant to differ. What is constrained is
the antisymmetry, and that is exact. No rate is delivered: the growth depends on the Hamiltonian,
and the one used here is a random CPT-covariant matrix. The closed-de Sitter overlap's is a
different Hamiltonian. The shape is established; the number is not.

That last point needs a qualification, and it is the one that applies to the closed-de Sitter
overlap below. The calculation assumed a state evolving under a CPT-covariant Hamiltonian.
Wheeler-DeWitt supplies a constraint solution rather than an evolving state, and the no-boundary
wavefunction is real, $\psi\sim\cos S=\tfrac12(e^{iS}+e^{-iS})$, so the two branch amplitudes are
complex conjugates, their moduli are equal identically at every $aH$, and there is no time
dependence to track. In the setting of §2.1 the imbalance vanishes for that reason and not because
of CPT.

Those two conditions differ, and the stronger one is imported. CPT covariance gives an imbalance
odd in time and generally nonzero; reality of the wavefunction gives zero identically. Reality is
not, however, only a choice of cosmological boundary condition: the paragraphs below obtain it
from $\Theta$-invariance instead, and the cosmology paper's A.4 states the commitment that makes
that available. Preferring a tunnelling wavefunction therefore gives up the no-boundary route to reality. Reality itself survives, provided they keep $\Theta$-invariance of
physical states. What survives either way is the cross-sheet statement, since $\langle
N\rangle(t)+\langle N\rangle(-t)=0$ holds trivially when both vanish. The conservation law is
therefore the more robust of the two claims, and the equal weight on a single sheet is the weaker.

That weaker claim can be improved, though not to a theorem. Reality of the wavefunction need not
be borrowed from the no-boundary proposal, because $\Theta$ is antilinear and imposing
$\Theta\lvert\psi\rangle=\lvert\psi\rangle$ *is* a reality condition. Writing $\Theta=K\circ S$
with $K$ complex conjugation and $S$ linear, $\Theta^2=1$ requires $S\bar S=1$, and in the branch
basis with $S$ the branch swap the condition reads $\psi_-=\bar\psi_+$, which is the no-boundary
form and forces $\lvert\psi_-\rvert=\lvert\psi_+\rvert$. Equal branch weight and
$\Theta$-invariance are the same condition, not two. The restriction is well defined: the
Wheeler-DeWitt operator is real, so its solution space is closed under conjugation, checked
directly, and the invariant part carries exactly half the real dimension, measured as the rank of
$(1+\Theta)/2$ applied to a complex basis at $d=1,2,3,5$. That is the same halving A.16 obtains at
the singularity through $(1+JU)/2$: two loci, one mechanism, an involution keeping its even
sector.

What this changes is where the assumption comes from. Its status is unaffected. It is no longer
borrowed from Hartle and Hawking but stated in the fold's own terms. Two things it does not do. It
still needs the physical state to solve the constraint instead of evolving, since
$\Theta$-invariance is not preserved under evolution, and timelessness is a feature of the
formulation, which no theorem supplies. And the cosmology paper's C.2 gauges the *linear*
involution $P_\perp$, following Harlow and Numasawa; gauging an antilinear one is a different
operation which we do not perform. So $\Theta$-invariance is a condition the fold may adopt
naturally, and not one the fold already carries.

That reach matters for the horizons A.10 cannot otherwise touch, and it also marks where a natural
repair fails. One might hope to move from $B$ to a late-time cross-section, since the reduction
needed only $\Delta\tau=0$ and a cross-section supplies that by construction. It does not work:
$J$ fixes $B$ pointwise and fixes nothing else on the horizon, carrying every cross-section of
$H^+$ to a cross-section of the mirror sheet's $H^-$, so the two arguments no longer share a
surface and the ratio is a correlator between two horizon branches rather than one restricted to a
single surface. The reciprocal law itself is untouched by that, since both kernels send their
second argument to the same place and differ only by the transverse reflection. The obstruction
was never the absence of $B$; it is the absence of the mirror sheet. So the algebraic law
transfers to a collapse hole, whose settled exterior is stationary and axisymmetric and whose
transverse antipodal map is an automorphism of the exterior algebra, while $\alpha=J\circ P_\perp$
as the second Keldysh leg does not, because no algebra supplies a mirror sheet.

An obvious objection is that the law is definitional: it holds for any involution and any positive
kernel, so it constrains nothing. That is correct on $B$ and, as A.10 now records, off it too, the
identity being algebraic at every separation; and the two are one equation in different limits.
Off $B$ the boost separation has not dropped out, and A.6's identification makes $\alpha$ act as
the half-period continuation $W(t-i\beta/2)$, which reproduces the image kernel there to
$4\times10^{-17}$. Then $\alpha^2=1$ reads $W(t-i\beta)=W(t)$, and that is not free: sweeping the
shift period, the condition fails by $O(25)$ at every value tried and holds only at $\beta=2\pi/H$
and its multiples, while minimising the residual returns $6.2831853072$ against
$2\pi/H=6.2831853072$ from three of four sample times, the fourth a boundary artefact of the
search window carrying a residual of $45$. In the frequency domain the same statement is that
$e^{\beta E/2}$ squares to the full KMS factor $e^{\beta E}$, so the fold's map is the square root
of the KMS transformation.

A.10 sets out why none of that is new physics, why the point stands anyway, and what it
leaves untouched: the reduction to $W_B$ itself, which is the step A.10 rests on and does not
prove. That limit carries to everything collected here.

---



**A.13 A latitude-dependent horizon response mixes $\ell$, by two for a scalar and by one for a
graviton.** §4's second reflecting signature. The squashing of A.10 is one function, and it has a
second consequence that is easier to look for. Write the local anisotropy of the bifurcation
surface, azimuthal proper length per unit coordinate over meridional, as

$$
A(\theta)=\frac{\sqrt{g_{\phi\phi}}}{\sin\theta\,\sqrt{g_{\theta\theta}}}
=\frac{r_+^2+a^2}{r_+^2+a^2\cos^2\theta},
$$

verified against the induced metric to $4\times10^{-16}$ at four spins up to $a=0.9M$. It is unity
at every latitude only when $a=0$, and $A(\pi/2)/A(0)$ is exactly the squashing ratio
$1+a^2/r_+^2$ of A.10, at every spin tested. The two statements are one function seen twice.

Teukolsky's separation puts the horizon condition on the radial function alone, which works
because that condition is the same at every latitude. A response weighted by $A$ is not, so it
cannot be imposed mode by mode: it couples different $\ell$ at fixed $m$. Because $A$ is even in
$\cos\theta$, its Legendre expansion carries even terms only, so on a *scalar* field the coupling
is $\ell\to\ell\pm2$, $\ell\pm4$ and never $\ell\pm1$. Computed on a 200-node Gauss-Legendre rule,
every odd coefficient of $\langle P_\ell|A|P_{\ell'}\rangle$ vanishes: the largest such entry sits
at $4\times10^{-15}$ and does not move whether the cut-off is $\ell=6$ or $\ell=12$, which is the
rule's own floor and not a residual, since its Legendre orthogonality holds only to the same
figure. Validated by planting an anisotropy odd in $\cos\theta$, which lifts the odd entries to
$0.21$.

That rule does not survive the move to gravitational perturbations. Teukolsky modes are
spin-weighted, $s=-2$, and spin-weighted harmonics do not carry the parity the scalar argument
uses. The decisive matrix element is non-zero:
$\langle{}_{-2}Y_{22}|\cos^2\theta|{}_{-2}Y_{32}\rangle=\sqrt{35}/21\simeq0.281718$, reproduced to
$2\times10^{-16}$ on the same rule with the harmonics checked orthonormal first, against the
quadrature floor for the same element built from Legendre functions. An even response can
therefore mix $\ell=2$ with $\ell=3$ at fixed $m=2$. The scalar coefficients below are correct;
what fails is carrying their selection rule into the gravitational case.

The strength is the quadrupole of the anisotropy relative to its mean, $c_2/c_0$, where
$c_\ell=\tfrac{2\ell+1}{2}\int_{-1}^{1} A(x)P_\ell(x)\,dx$ with $x=\cos\theta$. It is $-0.00167$,
$-0.01552$, $-0.04614$, $-0.21875$ and $-0.36711$ at $a/M=0.1$, 0.3, 0.5, 0.9 and 0.99, and
identically zero at $a=0$, with every odd coefficient vanishing by the same parity.

What is useful here is the selection rule, and it is worth being exact about what it can and
cannot do. It supplies no amplitude: the law that would fix how strongly a horizon response is
weighted by $A$ is the matching law for a general rotating-horizon seam, which §4 still records as
unconstructed. For the corner term the weight is adopted as zero rather than derived, A.11 having
withdrawn both arguments that would have derived it. So the rule is not a falsifier of this paper.
Read as a falsifier it is empty, because the implementation defended here predicts no such
response to begin with.

It is not a discriminator on somebody's positive detection either, and that is the claim being
withdrawn. The multipole structure of a ringdown anomaly does not decide whether the fold could be
its cause: a parity-even transverse map can produce $\ell\pm1$ mixing for spin-weighted modes, by
the element above, so an $\ell\pm1$ admixture rules nothing out and its absence confirms nothing.
What remains is the scalar statement and the general one: a response that depends on latitude
couples different $\ell$ at fixed $m$, and the size of that coupling still has no law to fix it.

A selection rule without a detectability budget is not yet a prediction, so here is one, and the
useful feature is that it is asymmetric. Take the seam weight $w$ to be bounded by the echo
searches, since a reflecting seam is what those searches look for, and write the fractional power
going to $\ell=4$ as $w^2|c_2/c_0|$. A three-sigma detection of that contamination then needs a
fundamental-mode ringdown signal-to-noise of roughly $3/(w\sqrt{|c_2/c_0|})$, which at $a=0.9$ and
$w=10^{-2}$ is about 600 and at $w=10^{-3}$ about 6000. Current instruments reach a few tens on
loud events, so confirmation waits on next-generation detectors and, at the lower weight, on
nothing yet planned. These are order-of-magnitude figures and the detector numbers are nominal.

No asymmetry survives the selection rule. The fold does not forbid $\ell\pm1$ identically for
gravitational modes, so there is no free exclusion and a target amplitude does have to be reached,
on both sides. The measurement side is not free in the same way, and we do not have a matching
formula for it. Telling a real odd-parity admixture from noise, from waveform systematics, or from
an $\ell=2$ overtone is itself amplitude-dependent, and the smaller nature's true leakage the more
signal-to-noise it takes to resolve, exactly as on the confirming side. So which way that balance
tips is established and by how much is not: quoting a figure of order tens for the refuting side
would borrow generic loud-event capability and compute nothing, and we do not quote one. A claim
that is easier to refute than to confirm is the right way round, and it is the sense in which this
rule belongs here despite supplying no amplitude.

**A.14 The antilinear lift acts on the quasinormal spectrum as $\omega\to\omega^*$, and the outgoing branch quantifies a reflecting horizon.** §4's third reflecting signature. This follows from
the definitions in the cosmology paper's §2.1 with no further input. Acting on a mode $e^{-i\omega
t}$, the pullback by $\alpha$ reverses $t$, giving $e^{+i\omega t}$, and antilinearity then
conjugates the coefficient, giving $e^{-i\omega^*t}$. A mode damped in our time therefore pairs
with one growing in our time, which is the content of the CPT relation and nothing is wrong with
it: the mirror sheet carries the opposite time orientation, so each sheet sees ordinary damping
on its own clock, and the growth exists only when one sheet is described in the other's.

That action has a consequence for a reflecting horizon, and we state it before computing it. The
Schwarzschild spectrum has the mirror symmetry $\omega\to-\omega^*$: if $\omega$ is a quasinormal
frequency then so is $-\omega^*$. The fold sends $\omega\to\omega^*$, so composing the two gives
$\omega\to-\omega$, and whether the fold's action is compatible with the spectrum's own symmetry
is therefore a question about the boundary condition. It has a two-line answer. Leaver's
coefficients are real polynomials in $\rho=-i\omega$, so the continued fraction obeys
$\overline{F(\omega)}=F(-\omega^*)$, and the same argument gives it for $G$. Checked for $F$ at
six frequencies, real and complex, with a defect of zero rather than a small number, since the two
sides run the identical arithmetic; the check is validated by planting an imaginary part in
$\beta_n$, which breaks it. The $G$ half is the argument and not a computation, because $G$ is not
built anywhere here, which is said again below. Conjugating the quasinormal condition
$F(\omega)+\mathcal R\,G(\omega)=0$ then gives $F(-\omega^*)+\overline{\mathcal
R}\,G(-\omega^*)=0$, so $-\omega^*$ solves the same equation exactly when $\mathcal
R(-\omega^*)=\overline{\mathcal R(\omega)}$. For a *constant* reflectivity that condition is
$\overline{\mathcal R}=\mathcal R$, and everything below is that case. It is not the general
condition: a delayed response $\mathcal R(\omega)=r_0e^{i\omega\tau}$ with real $r_0$ and $\tau$
is complex at every real frequency and satisfies it exactly, as does any $\mathcal R$ with real
Taylor coefficients in $i\omega$. Reality is sufficient and not necessary.

To test this, write the horizon behaviour in powers of $x=r-1$, with $2M=1$ and $\rho=-i\omega$.
The two Frobenius solutions there go as $x^{\rho}$ and $x^{-\rho}$, the first carrying the wave
transmitted into the horizon and the second the wave reflected off it, so a reflecting horizon
imposes

$$\psi\sim x^{\rho}+\mathcal R_h\,x^{-\rho}$$

with unit leading coefficients on both. That normalisation is part of the statement. Measured
instead against unit-amplitude waves in $r_*=r+\log(r-1)$, the same physical reflectivity reads
$\mathcal R_{\rm wave}=e^{2\rho}\mathcal R_h$, because $x^{\rho}=e^{-\rho}e^{\rho r_*}$, a factor
of $0.1085+1.4233i$ at the fundamental, and shifting the additive origin of $r_*$ moves it again.
A response quoted without its convention is not a number, so the convention comes first here.

Leaver's continued fraction is built on the ingoing branch, so the outgoing one has to be
constructed, and its ansatz is fixed before any recursion is written. A trial $x^{A}r^{B}e^{-\rho
x}$ carries $r$-power $A+B$ at infinity, where outgoing means $e^{-\rho r_*}=r^{-\rho}e^{-\rho
r}$, so $A+B=-\rho$. Leaver's branch takes $A=+\rho$ and needs $B=-2\rho$; the outgoing branch
takes $A=-\rho$ and needs $B=0$, no power of $r$ at all. Over six decades of $r$ the ratio to
$e^{-\rho r_*}$ drifts by $10^{-10}$ for that choice and by $1.9$ for either $B=\pm2\rho$, and at
the horizon its ratio to $x^{-\rho}$ is one to eight figures while Leaver's winds through $3.7$
radians. Under that prefactor the three-term recursion reads

$$\alpha_n=(n+1)(n+1-2\rho),\qquad
\beta_n=-\left(2n^2+2n+\ell(\ell+1)-s^2+1\right),\qquad
\gamma_n=n^2-s^2,$$

which is Leaver's confluent reduction redone rather than his coefficients with signs moved. The
series it generates satisfies the original Regge-Wheeler equation to $5\times10^{-41}$ at forty
digits, and a part-per-million nudge to any one of the three families raises it to between $2
imes10^{-7}$ and $7 imes10^{-6}$, while Leaver's own coefficients under this prefactor fail at
$0.8$.

With both branches in hand the reflecting condition is $\mathcal S(\omega)=\mathcal R_h$, where
$\mathcal S=-F/G$ is the ratio of the two horizon amplitudes carried by the solution that is
outgoing at infinity, so the linear response is $d\omega/d\mathcal R_h=1/\mathcal S'(\omega_0)$.
Normalising the two Frobenius solutions is what makes that a number: $F$ and $G$ can be rescaled
independently without moving their zeros, and $-G/F'$ moves with them, but a ratio of two
solutions each carrying unit leading coefficient cannot be rescaled at all. Continuing the
equation in from large $r$ along a ray rotated off the real axis, so that the wanted solution is
the dominant one going inward, and projecting onto the two Frobenius solutions at the match point,
gives

$$M\,\frac{d\omega}{d\mathcal R_h}=0.00735845-0.0646771\,i,$$

or $0.0928510+0.0034534\,i$ in the tortoise normalisation. The same machinery returns
$M\omega_0=0.3736716844-0.0889623157i$ as the root of $\mathcal S$, which is Leaver's fundamental
with no continued fraction anywhere in the calculation, and the independent Leaver solver used to
seed that root returns the standard values for five other modes it was not built around. Neither
number moves by more than $5\times10^{-41}$ when the contour is changed from a ray at $45^{\circ}$
reaching $r=300$ to one at $60^{\circ}$ reaching $r=120$. Planting a scalar potential in place of
the gravitational one, or $\ell=3$ in place of $\ell=2$, or a flipped sign in the $1/r^3$ term,
moves $|\mathcal S(\omega_0)|$ from $5\times10^{-48}$ to of order one.

What that response says is that a reflecting horizon shows up in the damping long before it shows
up in the pitch. A reflectivity of one per cent moves the real frequency by two parts in ten
thousand and the damping rate by seven parts in a thousand, a factor of $37$ between them, so a
ringdown search for a reflecting horizon should be pushed on the decay time.

The mirror symmetry then behaves as the algebra said it would. Solving $\mathcal
S(\omega)=\mathcal R$ for the mode near $\omega_0$ and for the one near $-\omega_0^*$, the defect
$|\omega_+ +\overline{\omega_-}|$ is exactly zero at $\mathcal R=0.001$ and at $\mathcal R=0.01$,
zero rather than small because the mirrored contour runs the identical arithmetic, and it is
$2.604\times10^{-4}$ at $\mathcal R=0.001i$ and $2.604\times10^{-3}$ at $\mathcal R=0.01i$, linear
in the imaginary part and equal to twice it times the response above. Within the
constant-reflectivity case, then, reality preserves the mirror symmetry and an imaginary part
breaks it. Nothing here extends that to a frequency-dependent seam.

Two limits bound what this shows. It is a check of internal consistency: the prediction and the
test are both ours, and no measurement enters. And it does not favour a reflecting horizon, which
the minimal implementation of §4 does not adopt; it says what a reflecting implementation would
have to look like, namely that a complex reflectivity carries a spectral signature a real one
cannot, so the two are distinguishable in principle by the spectrum alone.

**A.15 Where the two sheets can meet, and what that requires.** §5's condition and §6's count,
both derived. *One hypothesis is assumed throughout, and it is stated first.* **(H)** *The fold maps the hole to itself as a set.*

That is the whole of it. It does not name a map, and it does not ask the hole to have a second
exterior. Both follow, and the counting that delivers them is short.

Near an isolated hole the geometry is Kruskal. Both $r$ and the conformal factor there depend on
$U$ and $V$ only through the product $UV$, so a linear map of the $(U,V)$ plane is an isometry
exactly when it preserves $UV$ and the form $dU\,dV$. That leaves the diagonal maps
$(U,V)\mapsto(\lambda U,V/\lambda)$ and the swaps $(U,V)\mapsto(\lambda V,U/\lambda)$, and nothing
else. Squaring picks out the involutions: among the diagonals only $\lambda=-1$, which is
$N:(U,V)\mapsto(-U,-V)$; among the swaps every $\lambda$, a one-parameter family that conjugation
by a boost collapses to two representatives, $S:(U,V)\mapsto(V,U)$ for $\lambda>0$ and
$SN:(U,V)\mapsto(-V,-U)$ for $\lambda<0$. Up to a boost there are three and there are no others.

![The classification, and what separates the three. In Kruskal coordinates $T=(U+V)/2$ and
$X=(V-U)/2$ the survivors of the squaring are the point reflection through the origin, the mirror
in the vertical axis and the mirror in the horizontal one. Shaded, the singularities; thin
diagonals, the horizons; green, each map's fixed set, which is a point for $N$ and a line for the
other two. The blue dot is a point of our exterior $R$ and the orange one is its image. $S$ carries
$R$ to $L$ but preserves time orientation, so a CPT map cannot be it; it is the $\mathbb{RP}^3$
geon. $SN$ reverses time orientation but sends each exterior to itself, so it offers no mirror
sheet at all, and the contact it would describe would sit where we can look. $N$ does
both things the fold needs and, unlike the swaps, admits no boost freedom to fix afterwards. The
script checks that all three preserve $UV$ and square to the identity, and that a fourth map does
neither.](fig_companion_involutions.pdf)

Two of the three go, for different reasons. The fold is a CPT map and has to exchange past and
future, which removes $S$: it fixes $T$, preserves time orientation and carries the future
interior to itself. $S$ is the $\mathbb{RP}^3$ geon map of Louko, Mann and Marolf [28], whose
quotient is globally hyperbolic and well behaved, and substituting it gives a different
question with a different answer. $SN$ passes the CPT test, since it reverses time orientation and
carries $F$ to $P$, and fails an observational one. It sends each exterior to itself, so it
identifies $(t,r,n)$ with $(-t,r,-n)$ with both events in our own exterior, and those two are
causally connected once $|t|$ exceeds about a light-crossing time. The minimum photon time from a
point to its own antipode at the same radius is $16.6M$ at $r=3.5M$, $28.7M$ at $r=10M$, and
approaches the flat-space chord $2r$ far out. Under $SN$ the fold's contact would sit in the open,
at every radius and around every hole older than a few crossing times. It does not.

What is left is $N$, and it is rigid. Composing it with a boost destroys the involution, so no
parameter survives to be chosen. The transverse factor is fixed as well: it has to act freely on
the bifurcation sphere, and every free involution of $S^2$ is conjugate to the antipodal map,
since the quotient is a closed surface of Euler characteristic one and $\mathbb{RP}^2$ is the only
one.

The same count answers a question one step earlier, which is why an involution rather than
something of higher order: why two sheets and not four. A free action of a finite group $G$ on a
compact manifold $M$ has $\chi(M)=|G|\,\chi(M/G)$, so $|G|$ divides $\chi(M)$. The transverse
sphere in $D$ dimensions is $S^{D-2}$ and $\chi(S^n)=1+(-1)^n$, which is two for even $n$ and zero
for odd. In four dimensions that is $\chi(S^2)=2$, so $|G|$ divides two and the fold is
two-sheeted with nothing else available. A free $\mathbb Z_3$ would need a quotient of Euler
characteristic $2/3$. The restriction is even-dimensional: $S^3$ carries free $\mathbb Z_n$
actions for every $n$, which is what a lens space is, so a four-sheeted fold is a five-dimensional
possibility and not a four-dimensional one. Two sheets is not an assumption of the fold.
It is what the sphere admits. So the fold near a hole it fixes is $\Theta=N\circ P_\perp$, with
nothing left over.

The second exterior then comes free. $N$ carries $R$ to $L$, so a fold-invariant hole has $L$, the
past interior $P$, and a bifurcation surface, whatever its formation history. That is not what Section 6 used to have to argue. A white-hole region is a
consequence of the hole being fold-invariant, not a separate demand on how the hole was made, and
no particular formation channel has to supply one.

It also says where such a hole would have to sit, and that is where the trouble is. If $\Theta$
carries our sheet to the mirror sheet and fixes the hole, the hole lies in both: its right
exterior in ours, its left in the mirror, and its bifurcation surface on the locus joining them,
which in this cosmology is the bang. The two routes to the mirror sheet, down a throat and back
through the bang, would then have to be one route and one map.

Schwarzschild-de Sitter is where to test that, since it carries a black-hole horizon and a
cosmological one at once, and the answer is a single point.

Below the Nariai limit it fails. Each horizon has its own wedge reflection, and in the conformal
diagram those are point reflections about two different bifurcation surfaces, so their composition
is a translation by twice the separation. Both neighbours of a static region lie to its future,
because $f$ is positive strictly between the horizons and negative on both sides, so $r$ is
timelike in both, falling to the singularity inside $r_b$ and growing without bound outside $r_c$.
The translation is therefore timelike, and identifying the two reflections means quotienting by
it, which closes timelike curves. Nothing milder is available either, since the surface gravities
differ, by a factor $6.5$ at $\Lambda M^2=0.005$ and $1.29$ at $0.10$.

At Nariai it works, and that is the result. There the geometry is the product $\mathrm{dS}_2\times
S^2$, and the embedding settles it with no appeal to diagrams at all. Write
$\mathrm{dS}_2=\{X\in\mathbb{R}^{1,2}:-X_0^2+X_1^2+X_2^2=\ell^2\}$, take the static patch to be
$X_1>|X_0|$ and the horizons to be $X_1=\pm X_0$. Both bifurcation surfaces sit at $X_0=X_1=0$, so
at $X_2=\pm\ell$, and the linear map $(X_0,X_1,X_2)\mapsto(-X_0,-X_1,X_2)$ fixes both. It is an
isometry and an involution, it reverses time orientation, it carries our static patch to the other
one and $F$ to $P$, and composing it with the sphere's antipodal map makes it free. It is the
wedge reflection at both horizons at once, which is what no sub-Nariai member of the family has.
The reason is that $\mathrm{dS}_2$ has circles for spatial sections, so its maximal extension
closes into four regions, with no chain and no translation to obstruct.

So the family contains exactly one fold-invariant hole and it is the largest one the family holds.
At the observed $\Lambda$ that is $M=1/(3\sqrt\Lambda)$, some $2.2\times10^{22}$ solar masses,
with a horizon ten billion light years in radius: not an object inside a universe but a
configuration of one. Two calculations run for unrelated reasons agree on the point. The contact
radius of Section 5, swept against $\Lambda$, runs from half the horizon radius at $\Lambda=0$ to
$0.778$, $0.919$ and $0.991$ of it as $9\Lambda M^2$ reaches $0.9$, $0.99$ and $0.9999$. The one
hole the fold can fix is also the one whose interior is entirely in contact, with no forbidden
outer shell left over.

Read from the other end the family says something tidier than a lone exception. The fold does not
switch on and off along it. It is present throughout, because the cosmological horizon always has
a wedge reflection, and composing that with $P_\perp$ gives a free, time-reversing map carrying
our static region across that horizon to a mirror region. That is the map Section 2 uses at our
own horizon. At $M=0$ there is one horizon and the fold fixes it. For $0<9\Lambda M^2<1$ there are
two with distinct bifurcation surfaces, the fold fixes the cosmological one, and it cannot also
fix the hole for the reason above. At $9\Lambda M^2=1$ the two horizons are the same object and
fixing one fixes both.

So the second class is not a separate population. A hole is two-sided exactly when its horizon is
the cosmological horizon, and the only mass at which that happens is the maximum. Section 2 and
Section 6 are describing one structure at the two ends of one parameter, and the contact radius
climbing from half the horizon radius to all of it is that parameter made visible.

One objection to a maximal configuration is that it does not last, and it needs answering, because
a hole that decays is not an object. Reduce on the sphere. With $ds^2=\hat
g_{ab}dx^adx^b+R^2d\Omega^2$ the vacuum equations with $\Lambda$ read $\hat
R_{ab}-(2/R)\nabla_a\nabla_bR=\Lambda\hat g_{ab}$ and $1-(\nabla R)^2-R\nabla^2R=\Lambda R^2$. Put
$R=\ell(1+\varphi)$. The second linearises to $\nabla^2\varphi=-2\Lambda\varphi$, and since $\hat
R_{ab}=(\hat R/2)\hat g_{ab}$ identically in two dimensions the first forces the traceless part of
$\nabla_a\nabla_b\varphi$ to vanish, leaving $\nabla_a\nabla_b\varphi=-\Lambda\hat g_{ab}\varphi$.
That is three solutions, where a wave equation alone would leave a function's worth, namely
$\varphi=c\cdot X$ for constant $c$ in $\mathbb{R}^{1,2}$.

Two of the three run away. In the static patch $X_2^2=\ell^2+X_0^2-X_1^2<\ell^2$, so $X_2/\ell$ is
bounded by one while $X_0/\ell$ and $X_1/\ell$ pass a thousand within eight static times. That is
the classical Nariai instability, the sphere swelling on one side and shrinking on the other as
the solution runs toward Schwarzschild-de Sitter. The third mode is the static near-Nariai
deformation and does nothing.

The fold removes both runaways. A perturbation descends to the quotient only if it agrees at
identified points, and the sphere radius is a function on the $\mathrm{dS}_2$ factor alone, so the
condition is $\varphi(N_\ell x)=\varphi(x)$. Under $(X_0,X_1,X_2)\mapsto(-X_0,-X_1,X_2)$ the two
growing modes are odd and the bounded one is even, so neither runaway is a perturbation of the
folded solution at all. The one hole the fold can fix is the one the fold makes stable, in the
sector where the instability lives. That is linear stability in the spherically symmetric sector
and no more: non-spherical modes and the semiclassical decay are different questions and are not
treated here.

Charge turns that point into a curve. In Reissner-Nordström-de Sitter a degenerate horizon needs
$f(r_0)=f'(r_0)=0$, and eliminating $M$ leaves $Q^2=r_0^2(1-\Lambda r_0^2)$ with
$M=Q^2/r_0+\Lambda r_0^3/3$. That curve has two halves. Above $r_0=1/\sqrt{2\Lambda}$ the merged
pair is $r_+=r_c$ and $f''(r_0)<0$, so $f\leq0$ on both sides of the double root, $r$ is timelike
there, and the near-horizon factor is $\mathrm{dS}_2$. The argument above used nothing but that
factor, so it applies verbatim. Below it the merged pair is $r_-=r_+$ and $f''(r_0)>0$: the factor
is $\mathrm{AdS}_2$, the horizon is extremal with zero surface gravity, and there is no bifurcate
Killing horizon for a wedge reflection to act on at all. The halves meet at the ultracold point
where all three horizons coincide, at the largest charge the family admits, $Q=1/(2\sqrt\Lambda)$.

So the fold-invariant set is a one-parameter family rather than a point, running from uncharged
Nariai to ultracold, every member with its black-hole horizon coincident with its cosmological
one. The stability result travels with it, since what selects the three modes is the traceless
constraint and not any coefficient, and their parity under $N_\ell$ is a property of the embedding
functions.

Rotation widens it again, and the branch there has an exact end. The transverse map is an isometry
of Kerr-de Sitter at every spin and every $\Lambda$ for the reason it works in Kerr, the metric
functions depending on $\theta$ through $\cos^2\theta$ alone, and the merger of the outer pair
runs from $a=0$ with $\Delta_r''<0$ throughout, so the radial factor is $\mathrm{dS}_2$ and never
$\mathrm{AdS}_2$. It stops where $\Delta_r$ acquires a triple root. There the quartic factorises
as $-(\Lambda/3)(r-r_0)^3(r+3r_0)$, matching coefficients gives $\Lambda r_0^2=2\sqrt3-3$,
$a^2=\Lambda r_0^4$ and $M=(4\Lambda/3)r_0^3$, and the spin at the endpoint is a pure number,

$$
\frac{a}{M}=\frac{3}{4\sqrt{2\sqrt3-3}}=1.1009173688,
$$

the same in every de Sitter background and past the Kerr extremal bound, which a positive
$\Lambda$ permits. What is not done there is the embedding argument inside the warped rotating
near-horizon geometry: what is established is that its two ingredients survive rotation.

One check came free there, and neither side of it was arranged. Reducing
with a radial field, the angular equation reads $1-(\nabla R)^2-R\nabla^2R=\Lambda R^2+Q^2/R^2$,
and linearising gives $\nabla^2\varphi=-(2/r_0^2)(2\Lambda r_0^2-1)\varphi$. The modes still have
to be $\varphi=c\cdot X$, so consistency forces $\ell_2^2=r_0^2/(2\Lambda r_0^2-1)$, while
$\ell_2$ was already fixed by the metric as $\ell_2^2=2/|f''(r_0)|$. The two agree to
$6\times10^{-15}$ along the branch, and the perturbation expression changes sign exactly at
$r_0=1/\sqrt{2\Lambda}$, which is where the metric says Nariai stops.

What stays open is therefore narrow. For every hole below it
the alternative reading is still live: the mirror sheet contains its own image of every hole in
ours, the fold carries our hole's exterior to the mirror hole's exterior, and $L$ is a second
asymptotic region on our own sheet related to us by no fold map at all.

A second thing the comparison makes clear is where the content sits. Drop $P_\perp$ and keep only
the $(U,V)\mapsto(-U,-V)$ part, and a point and its image share an angular position, so the
required path falls from $\pi$ to zero and the line $X\equiv0$ connects them for every interior
point: the answer becomes the whole interior. The sphere's antipodal map is therefore the entire
content of the question. Without it the sheets reach each other throughout the interior; with it,
only inside $r=M$.

A.11 uses the fact that the two wedges are spacelike separated at every pair of points. That is a
statement about exteriors, and taking it for the whole answer would be a mistake, because the
question of where an inter-sheet interaction is possible has a different answer and a sharp one.
Kruskal coordinates are conformally flat in the $(T,X)$ plane, so light cones lie at forty-five
degrees and the causal structure is Minkowski's. Writing $R=\{X>|T|\}$, $L=\{X<-|T|\}$,
$F=\{T>|X|\}$ and $P=\{T<-|X|\}$,

$$
J^{+}(R)\cap J^{+}(L)=F,\qquad J^{-}(R)\cap J^{-}(L)=P,
$$

so the black hole interior is exactly the set of events both exteriors can influence, and the
white hole interior is exactly the set that can influence both. Sampling $2\times10^4$
configurations per region, no event outside $F$ admits influence from both, and the shortfall from
unity inside $F$ comes from how the source points were sampled. The geometry contributes none of
it.

The fold's own map exchanges them. $J$ is the boost through imaginary angle $\pi$, which in
Kruskal coordinates is $(T,X)\mapsto(-T,-X)$, so $R\leftrightarrow L$ and $F\leftrightarrow P$,
checked on $2\times10^4$ points with no exception. Pairing a black hole interior with a white hole
interior is therefore not a further postulate; it is the same map that pairs the exteriors, read
in the other two regions.

Three consequences follow, one of them unwelcome. An inter-sheet interaction is causally
impossible outside a horizon and causally possible inside one, which is sharper than the seam
argument and independent of the seam coefficient. Anything occurring in $F$ lies in the causal
future of both exteriors and therefore reaches neither, so even a strong inter-sheet force is
unobservable from outside: §4's null has a second reason behind it that does not use A.11 at all.
And the contact window is finite. The longest proper time from horizon to singularity is $\pi M$,
and the part of it lying inside $r=M$, which is where contact is possible at all, is $(\pi/2-1)M$,
eighteen per cent of the whole. That is $8.4\,\mu$s at $3M_\odot$, twelve seconds at Sgr A* and
five hours at $6.5\times10^{9}M_\odot$, scaling linearly with mass.

The commutator makes the same statement sharper, and gives the depth a number. Under the wedge
reflection $J:(U,V)\mapsto(-U,-V)$ the Kruskal null coordinates scale as $dU=2U$ and $dV=2V$, so
$dU\,dV=4UV$ and the radial part of the separation between a point and its image turns timelike
exactly when $UV>0$. That set is the interior. The cross-sheet commutator therefore vanishes
identically outside a horizon and is nonzero inside one, switching on wherever $UV$ changes sign,
with nothing tuned to put it there.

*The four-dimensional condition.* It does not switch on at the horizon itself, and the
four-dimensional statement is sharper than the two-dimensional one above. Reaching the mirror sheet's causal region requires covering the transverse angle as well, since the fold is
$\alpha=J\circ P_\perp$ and $P_\perp$ sends the unit vector $\hat n$ to $-\hat n$. Explicitly: $P_\perp$ is the antipodal map of the sphere, so the angular
separation between a point and its image is exactly $\pi$ at every point, and nothing is lost by
working in the equatorial plane.

Causality then bounds the angle directly. From $ds^2\leq0$ in Kruskal,

$$
r^2\,d\Omega^2\;\leq\;F(r)\,(dT^2-dX^2),\qquad F(r)=\frac{32M^3}{r}e^{-r/2M},
$$

so the angular path along any causal curve is at most $\int g\,d\tau_2$ with $g=\sqrt F/r$ and
$d\tau_2$ the proper time of the $(T,X)$ part. Throughout, $x$ is taken in the black-hole interior
$F$ and $\Theta x$ in the white-hole interior $P$; the roles swap if $x$ is taken in $P$, and the
implication then runs the other way. The maximum angular sweep between two events is therefore the
length of the longest timelike curve in the conformally rescaled two-dimensional metric
$g^2(-dT^2+dX^2)$, and the sheets touch exactly when that maximum reaches $\pi$.

A boost sends $(U,V)\mapsto(U/\lambda,\lambda V)$ and fixes the angles, and it carries the pair
$(x,\Theta x)$ to $(x',\Theta x')$, so the answer depends only on the boost invariant $UV$, which
is to say only on $r$. Fixing that freedom by putting $x$ at $X=0$ leaves $x$ at $(T_0,0)$ and
$\Theta x$ at $(-T_0,0)$ with $T_0=\sqrt{(1-r/2M)e^{r/2M}}$. Along a causal curve $dT>0$, so $T$
parametrises, and

$$
\Delta\phi=\int_{-T_0}^{T_0} g\,\sqrt{1-X'^2}\;dT.
$$

Both factors are largest at $X\equiv0$: $g$ falls with $r$, since $d\log g^2/dr=-1/2M-3/r<0$,
while $r$ rises as $T^2-X^2$ falls. So the maximiser is $X\equiv0$, uniquely, and it is also the
fixed set of $X\mapsto-X$ and hence a geodesic. On it every exponential cancels,
$g\,|dT/dr|=1/\sqrt{r(2M-r)}$, and the integral is elementary:

$$
\Delta\phi_{\max}(r)=2\int_r^{2M}\frac{dr'}{\sqrt{r'(2M-r')}}
=2\pi-4\arcsin\sqrt{\frac{r}{2M}}.
$$

Setting this equal to $\pi$ gives $r=2M\sin^2(\pi/4)$, so the condition is

$$
\boxed{\;r\;\leq\;M\;}
$$

exactly half the horizon radius, with the mass cancelling because the answer is a fraction of
$r_h$ and not a length.

This question is older than the calculation. The map is Gibbons' elliptic interpretation of the
Kruskal manifold [29], written in the form used here by Sanchez and Whiting [30]. Harlow and
Numasawa [6], whose gauging argument A.11 already leans on, pose it again for the same quotient
and call the objects self-intersecting timelike curves, conjecturing that quantum gravity permits
them only where the self-intersections sit behind horizons. The result above is a sharp instance
of that conjecture and says more than it does: behind the horizon, and then inside $r=M$ as well.

We have not found a radius stated in any of them, and the search went this far and no further: not far. Harlow and Numasawa is available and was read at
source. Gibbons 1986 and Sanchez and Whiting 1987 are pre-arXiv, and neither was read at source
here; both were reached through abstracts and secondary citation, and repeated attempts at a
full-text route found the publisher copies gated and no repository holding either. So the claim
that $r=M$ is new rests on a sweep that did not open two of its doors, and we make no statement at
all about what the text of either paper says. It is plausible that the radius is new. It is not
established, and nothing else in this appendix depends on which way it goes.

Put in words the result stops looking like a coincidence of arcsines. The antipodal map bills half
a revolution. The journey has two interior legs and the fold's own symmetry splits the bill evenly
between them, so each leg must supply a quarter revolution. A leg starting at $r$ supplies
$\pi-2\arcsin\sqrt{r/2M}$, which is a quarter revolution exactly at $r=M$. Half the horizon radius
is the depth from which a causal curve can just turn a quarter of the way round before the horizon
reaches it. The whole journey, past singularity through the bifurcation surface to future
singularity, is worth one full revolution and no more. The antipodal map asks for half of one.
Half a revolution takes half the interior, and a fold through a general angle $\alpha$ would take $\cos^2(\alpha/4)$ of it.

Three things follow immediately. The commutator is nonzero only for $UV\geq\sqrt e/2=0.8244$,
against the singularity at $UV=1$, so the two-dimensional claim that it is nonzero throughout
$UV>0$ is too generous by half. The bound is attained rather than approached, since the curve
$X\equiv0$ with $d\phi=g\,dT$ is null, and it is the one that does it.

That the maximiser is a geodesic and not merely a null curve is what makes the statement an
equivalence rather than one direction of one. If the maximiser were null and not geodesic, Hawking
and Ellis' 4.5.10 would put its endpoint in the chronological future, which is open, and contact
would leak past $r=M$. It is the $E=0$ branch, whose $d\phi/dr=1/(r\sqrt{|f|})$ saturates the
bound exactly; structurally, $\{U=V\}$ is the fixed-point set of $t\mapsto-t$ and so totally
geodesic, a great circle inside it is totally geodesic, and in the remaining two dimensions every
null curve is a null pregeodesic. So at $r=M$ the image sits in $J^+(\Theta x)$ and not in
$I^+(\Theta x)$, and $\leq$ is the right sign. And the bounding sphere carries exactly a quarter
of the horizon area, which is an identity and not a coincidence: the threshold is
$\arcsin\sqrt{r/2M}=\pi/4$, so $\sqrt{r/2M}=1/\sqrt2$, and the quarter in the area is the square
of the half in the radius. Whether the echo of $S=A/4G$ is anything is a question we raise and do
not answer. What weighs against it is that the quarter here is a bare area ratio with no $G$ and
no $\hbar$ in it.

*One candidate for that number, ruled out.* It is the first optimisation anyone tries, so it gets a paragraph. Let the connecting curve sweep its angle where the sweep is
cheapest, at the photon sphere: $d\phi/dt=\sqrt{1-2M/r}/r$ is maximised at exactly $r=3M$,
giving a minimum coordinate time $3\sqrt3\,\pi M$ and a Kruskal ratio $e^{3\sqrt3\pi/4}=59.21$.
The optimisation is right and the photon sphere is out of reach. Along a future-directed causal
curve $dU\,dV\geq0$ with $dT>0$, so both $U$ and $V$ increase, and a curve joining $\Theta x$
to $x$ is confined to $|UV|\leq UV(r)\leq1$, while at $r=3M$, $|UV|=2.241$. No curve that does
the job gets there. What the candidate has right is the shape: contact needs the angle covered
as well as the fall, it does not open at the horizon, and how deep it opens is a pure number
free of the mass. Once the constraint is imposed that number is one half, not $59.21$.

*Charge.* Charge decides which form of that statement is the robust one, and it is not the one
just written. For Reissner-Nordstrom, $r^2f=(r-r_+)(r-r_-)$, so $-r^2f$ is a monic quadratic whose
roots are the two horizons, and $\int_a^b dr/\sqrt{(b-r)(r-a)}=\pi$ for any pair of roots. The
interior band therefore supplies exactly $\pi$ of turning at every charge, the leg integral is
$2\arcsin\sqrt{(r_+-r)/(r_+-r_-)}$, and the condition becomes

$$
r\;\leq\;\frac{r_++r_-}{2}\;=\;M,
$$

the same $M$, at every charge, and always exactly half way across the band. So the robust
statement is that contact fills the inner half of the interior; "half the horizon radius" is the
$r_-=0$ special case of it. That also settles the quarter of the area, which does not survive
charge: $A_c/A_+$ runs from $0.250$ at $Q=0$ to $0.768$ at $Q/M=0.99$ while the physical statement
does not move at all. Bekenstein-Hawking is $A/4$ for charged holes too, so anything thermodynamic
would have had to survive charge. It is arithmetic.

All of that has a sharper reading, and it should be said once. $P_\perp$ is antipodal and
therefore free, and $J$ has no fixed point off $B$, so the fold is a free involution and the
quotient is a manifold in which $x$ and $\Theta x$ are the same point. On the quotient, then,
$x\in J^+(\Theta x)$ says there is a closed causal curve through that point. What $r\leq M$
locates is the causality-violating core of the folded hole: closed timelike curves strictly
inside, the closed null geodesic exactly on the boundary, nothing outside it, and the whole of it
hidden behind a horizon and then half the interior.

One consequence decides whether the fold polices itself. The
quantity an image sum sees is the world function $\sigma(x,\Theta x)$, and it has a simple zero at
the boundary. Along $X\equiv0$ the Schwarzschild $t$ is constant, so the connecting geodesics are
the $E=0$ family,

$$
\left(\frac{dr}{d\tau}\right)^{2}=\left(\frac{2M}{r}-1\right)\left(1+\frac{L^2}{r^2}\right),
\qquad \frac{d\phi}{d\tau}=\frac{L}{r^2},
$$

whose large-$L$ limit is the null curve above. Fixing $L$ so the two interior legs deliver exactly
$\pi$ and reading off the proper time gives $\tau^2/(M-r)\to34.85$, a finite nonzero constant, so
$\tau\sim\sqrt{M-r}$ and $\sigma=-\tau^2/2$ vanishes *linearly* at $r=M$. A double zero and a
linear vanishing of $\tau$ are both excluded by the same numbers. The deepest loop, at $r\to0$,
has proper length $5.8839M$, against $\sqrt3\,\pi M=5.4414M$ for the constant-winding ansatz,
which is only a lower bound.

A simple zero of $\sigma$ is what a power-law divergence needs: the image term of a free field's
two-point function goes as $1/\sigma$, so it blows up as $(M-r)^{-1}$ on approach to the contact
sphere, and the stress tensor, built from two derivatives of it, as a higher inverse power. That
is how a chronology horizon in ordinary general relativity censors its own formation. The power
written that way is not the one that holds, because the image point is a caustic and $1/\sigma$
assumes it is not; A.18 does the sum that needs no such assumption, in a model geometry carrying
the same degeneracy, and returns a steeper $s^{-3/2}$ with the stress tensor at $s^{-7/2}$. The
conjecture needed the sign as well as the power, and A.18's components supply it. In
the point-split energy density the fold's pullbacks reproduce the wave operator, so every term
cancels except the part of the radial potential the transverse sphere does not account for.

That zero is the constant transverse radius's doing, and where the radius varies there is no
cancellation: in flat space, whose fold is the time reflection composed with the spatial point
reflection, the image energy density is exactly $(8r^2+6\xi^2)/4\pi^2D^3$, positive everywhere and
diverging positively at contact. So the sign the conjecture wanted is the sign it gets, in the one
setting where the whole thing is closed form. Neither model is a black hole: one has the caustic
and no sign, the other the sign and no caustic. On Schwarzschild the remainder is radial and nothing else.
A.19 carries the fold through for any static spherically symmetric metric and the transverse
sphere pays for the angular part of the potential exactly, at every one of them, so no $\ell$
survives in the density: what is left is the radial part of the wave operator set against the
mixed radial derivative, a two-dimensional object on the contact orbit.

Two caveats travel with that reading. The fold reverses time
orientation, so the quotient is not time-orientable and the loop closes with the transported arrow
flipped, which makes "closed timelike curve" a convention that has to be argued rather than
assumed. And surfaces of constant $r$ are spacelike inside, so this chronology horizon is
spacelike. Hawking's theorems produce the null kind and assume the time orientability this
quotient lacks.

The negative null-null component has a second consequence and it is larger than the first. For a
null $k$ the trace term and $\Lambda$ leave the field equations, so $R_{ab}k^ak^b=8\pi
G\,T_{ab}k^ak^b$ with no residue, which locks the two signs together. A negative
$R_{ab}k^ak^b$ is a failure of the null convergence condition, and that condition is the hypothesis
Penrose's argument uses to carry a trapped surface to an incomplete geodesic. So if that sign holds
along Penrose's own congruence it does two things at once: it delays the conjugate point, which is
what shrinks the contact region and polices the causality violation, and it removes the hypothesis
of the singularity theorem in the one region where the theorem's conclusion lives. The antecedent is
not free: the paragraphs below make it the whole question, and the evidence there points against it. How much would be needed, were it to hold, is definite. An ingoing radial null congruence in the interior is shear-free and enters the contact
region with $\theta=-4/r_h$, so $\theta'=-\theta^2/2-R_{ab}k^ak^b$ turns the expansion around
where $-R_{ab}k^ak^b$ exceeds $8/r_h^2$. Because the region is half the interior and
$\theta'=-\theta^2/2$ resumes on the far side, the expansion has to reach zero before the
congruence leaves, which is $\mathrm{artanh}(1/u)=u$ at $u=1.1997$ and raises the requirement to
$11.5138/r_h^2$, a factor $1.439$ above the pointwise one. In the vacuum the bound is saturated:
$\theta=-2/r$ with $dr/d\lambda=-1$ diverges at exactly $\lambda=2/|\theta_0|$.

Both of those are requirements on a constant stress and the stress is not constant. A.19's world
function vanishes linearly on the contact sphere, so the image term diverges there, and the contact
sphere is the region's outer edge, which is where an ingoing congruence enters. The largest stress
arrives at entry and falls inward, the integrated effect at the edge is divergent, and the vacuum's
focusing is not the leading behaviour there, so no finite level on a constant is the question. What takes a number is the thickness of the shell in which the fold's term is the larger of the two,
measured against $11.5138/r_h^2$ as the level to reach. Dimensions leave one combination of the two
lengths available, since $T_{ab}k^ak^b$ carries four inverse lengths and $m^2$ carries two:

$$
T_{ab}k^ak^b=\kappa\,m^2D^{-p}r_h^{\,p-2},\qquad
\frac{D_*}{r_h}=\left(\frac{8\pi\kappa\,(m/m_P)^2}{11.5138}\right)^{1/p},
$$

with $D$ the distance to the contact sphere and $\kappa$ the pure number A.18's sum on the contact
geodesic would supply. $D_*$ is proportional to $r_h$ and to no other length.

The power decides the answer and $\kappa$ barely enters it, and the power is $5/2$. Three links fix
it and each is measured on this geometry. A.19's rule puts the image Green function at
$\delta^{-(D-2+n)/2}$ in the distance to the caustic, over four members of a family, so a stress
built from two derivatives of it sits at $\delta^{-(D+2+n)/2}$: here $\delta^{-4}$, measured at
$\delta^{-3.95}$, which is the first test that rule has had against a stress rather than a
correlator. The mass-dependent part of every quantity is one power softer than its massless
counterpart, measured at $0.956$, $0.976$ and $0.968$ of a power for $G$, $P$ and $Q$, and that one
follows from the proper-time representation rather than from the mode sum. A mass is a phase in the
proper time, so to first order the mass part is
$\int ds\,s^{-(D+n)/2}e^{-\sigma/2s}$ with one extra power of $s$, and that raises the
world-function exponent from $(D+n)/2-1$ to $(D+n)/2-2$: one power of $\sigma$, checked against
quadrature at six pairs $(D,n)$ to $10^{-8}$ and against the exact massive integral, which returns
the first-order exponent to $2\times10^{-4}$ at $m^2=10^{-4}$. On this geometry the mass part is also
the first derivative times $m^2$ to $2.5\times10^{-4}$ at $m=0.05$, so nothing beyond first order is
in play.

One power of $\sigma$ is one power of the distance here and two in flat space, and that is the whole
of the apparent conflict with the textbook massive correction. At the antipodal caustic
$\sigma=(\pi^2a^2-\tau^2)/2$, which is $\pi a\delta$ to eight figures at $\delta=10^{-5}$; at a hole
A.19 finds $\tau^2/(M-r)$ tending to $34.85$, finite and nonzero, so $\sigma$ is proportional to
$M-r$ there as well. Away from a caustic $\sigma=r^2/2$ and one power of it is two powers of $r$,
which is the log-carrying flat-space answer, the same statement read in a different variable. The
sphere family cannot settle this on its own, because it ties the caustic order to the dimension,
$n=N-1$ and $D=N+1$, so order one arrives only in three dimensions where the powers collide and a
logarithm interferes with the fit.

The further power the mass slot appeared to lose is the coupling's doing. Where the Ricci tensor
vanishes the null stress is $(1-4\xi)P-Q$, so the leading mass parts cancel exactly when $1-4\xi$
equals $dQ/dP$ at the caustic, and here that ratio is $0.33018$, putting the cancelling coupling at
$\xi=0.16746$, which is the conformal one to three figures. That is a property of a conformally flat
geometry and not of the mass: at $\xi=0$, $1/12$, $0.2$ and $0.25$ the mass slot measures $-2.97$,
$-2.97$, $-3.00$ and $-2.99$, and $\xi=1/6+0.02$ already gives $-3.01$, so the cancellation is a
point and not a neighbourhood. At a hole the caustic is order one, so the leading stress is
$\delta^{-7/2}$ and the mass part is $\delta^{-5/2}$, and the lower reading survives only as a
fine-tuning of $\xi$ to a hole's own $dQ/dP$, a number nobody has computed.

At $p=5/2$, $\kappa$ is no longer open either, because every ingredient of it is already here. The
caustic amplitude is $\Delta^{1/2}\to3.9004\,M\,s^{-1/2}$, on A.19's projection arc length
$M(\pi+2)$ and not on the affine total, which the same appendix's measurement excludes; the massless and mass parts are the
$k=5/2$ and $k=3/2$ proper-time integrals, reproduced against quadrature to $10^{-8}$; the world
function vanishes linearly with $|\sigma|=17.425\,(M-r)$ from A.19's $\tau^2/(M-r)\to34.85$; and the
contraction factor, the square of the null derivative of the distance, is exactly one on the ingoing
radial congruence because $dr/d\lambda=-1$ on it. Collecting those,
$G_{\rm mass}=-A\,m^2\sigma^{-1/2}$ with $A=0.0525\,M$, and two derivatives give
$\tfrac34A\,m^2\sigma^{-5/2}(\partial_k\sigma)^2$, whose $D$ dependence is
$|\sigma|^{-1/2}D^{-5/2}$. In units of $r_h^{1/2}$ that is $\kappa=0.0039329$.

At one solar mass the shell is then $2.1\times10^{-6}$ m for the $491.6$ PeV fermion,
$1.5\times10^{-11}$ m for a top quark, $2.3\times10^{-13}$ m for a proton and
$5.5\times10^{-16}$ m for an electron, a factor $0.1091$ below the unit-$\kappa$ values because the
thickness goes as $\kappa^{2/5}$, and it drops below a Planck length only for masses under
$2.0\times10^{-19}$ eV. So the shell is a real length for every field there is. It also widens with
the hole rather than narrowing, since $D_*$ is proportional to $r_h$, which puts the effect at its
largest in the largest holes. One thing the coefficient does not settle and one it now does. The
radial sign, which A.18's components settle below, rests on conservation rather than on a sum done
in the interior. The order-unity factor that used to stand beside $\kappa$, from keeping only the
leading derivative pairing, is measured at one: with $G=F(\sigma)$ the null-null second derivative
is $F''(k\cdot\nabla\sigma)^2+F'k^ak^b\nabla_a\nabla_b\sigma$ and the second piece carries two
fewer powers of $\sigma$, so it cannot reach the leading coefficient. Against A.18's exact tower,
in the geometry where A.19's rule leaves nothing to choose, the leading pairing agrees to twelve
digits for the massless part and to $0.2$ per cent for the mass part.

The reduction that sign goes through is pure algebra. A stress invariant under the interior's isometries has only $T^t{}_t$,
$T^r{}_r$ and $T^\theta{}_\theta=T^\varphi{}_\varphi$, and contracting with a null vector without
and with a transverse part gives
$T_{kk}=|f|(k^t)^2(T^t{}_t-T^r{}_r)$ and that same expression plus
$r^2(k^\theta)^2(T^\theta{}_\theta-T^r{}_r)$: the radial answer is the contact answer less the
anisotropy. Conservation supplies the anisotropy outright,
$T^\theta{}_\theta-T^r{}_r=(r/2)[\partial_rT^r{}_r-(f'/2f)(T^t{}_t-T^r{}_r)]$, verified against the
Reissner-Nordström electromagnetic stress to $10^{-11}$ and broken at $1.1$ when the transverse
pressure is scaled by $1.4$. With the trace as the third relation the $2\times2$ system inverts
exactly and its determinant is nowhere zero in the interior. The trace carries no conformal anomaly,
because an anomaly is state-independent and the image part is a difference of states, so it is
$(6\xi-1)\tfrac12\Box\langle\phi^2\rangle-m^2\langle\phi^2\rangle$ with
$\langle\phi^2\rangle_{\rm img}=0.0014423\,M^{-1/2}D^{-3/2}$ from the same assembly as the
coefficient. At $\xi=1/6$ the Laplacian drops and the trace adds nothing further; away from it that
Laplacian is A.18's object, since the image moves with the point. What the reduction leaves at
$\xi=1/6$ is one ratio, the contact geodesic's own $(k\cdot\nabla\sigma)^2$ against the radial
congruence's, which is a transport along a curve A.19 already has and not a tensor.

Following that through changes the reading of the sign, and it is the sharpest thing in this appendix. The fold joins a point to its image at fixed Schwarzschild $t$, so
the contact geodesic is the $E=0$ family and its tangent has no $t$ component: for a null geodesic
with $E=0$, $\dot r^2=|f|L^2/r^2$ and $d\varphi/dr=1/r\sqrt{|f|}$, and §5's leg, from $r$ out to the
horizon, delivers $\pi-2\arcsin\sqrt{r/2M}$, so the two-leg budget is $2\pi-4\arcsin\sqrt{r/2M}$ and
reaches $\pi$ at $r=M$ with the mass cancelling, matched to quadrature at $10^{-5}$. Integrating the
leg the other way, from the singularity out to $r$, gives $4\arcsin\sqrt{r/2M}$ instead, which
agrees at $r=M$ and at no other radius and carries the contact condition the opposite way round, so
a check made at $r=M$ alone cannot tell the two apart. With $k^t=0$ the first term of the contraction drops, so the contact
direction measures the anisotropy alone while the radial congruence measures $X$, and the two are
orthogonal.

Conservation and the trace then tie the two together, and the coupling decides how. The trace is
$X+4Y+2A$ and conservation is $Y'=(f'/2f)X+2A/r$, so $X=T-2A-4Y$, and the trace's two pieces sit on
either side of the anisotropy: $A$ carries $D^{-5/2}$, the $m^2$ piece of
$(6\xi-1)\tfrac12\Box\langle\phi^2\rangle-m^2\langle\phi^2\rangle$ carries $D^{-3/2}$ and the
$\Box$ piece $D^{-7/2}$. At $\xi=1/6$ the $\Box$ piece is absent and $X\to-2A$, the ratio running
$1.056$, $1.005$, $1.0005$, $1.00005$ as $D$ falls through four decades, so a negative anisotropy puts
$X$ on the other side of zero and the radial contraction with it. At any other coupling the $\Box$
piece leads, $X$ follows the trace to $1.00005$ of it over the same range, and the contact value does
not reach the leading behaviour at all.

Which combination the computed sign belongs to is settled by a symmetry, and the answer is that the
calibration geometry cannot distinguish them. On $\mathbb R\times S^3$ the rotations fixing a point
also fix its antipode, a rotation about an axis fixing both ends of it, so the isotropy group there is
the whole of $SO(3)$ and it fixes the image. A symmetric spatial tensor invariant under $SO(3)$ is a
multiple of the identity, which the twenty-four octahedral rotations enforce exactly: a test tensor
of trace $4$ averages to $1.3333333333$ on every diagonal entry with the off-diagonals at machine
zero, so the image stress there is isotropic and its anisotropy is identically zero. Inside a hole
the isotropy is only the $SO(2)$ about the radial direction, which fixes the contact image too but
keeps the radial and transverse pressures distinct: the same tensor averages to $0.50000$ on both
transverse entries and $3$ on the radial one, the closed form matching a twenty-thousand-point
quadrature of the group average to $2\times10^{-13}$.

The calibration therefore carries one combination where a hole carries two, and the one that
distinguishes them vanishes identically there. Its negative value says that some $\rho+p$ is negative
at a hole without saying which, and conservation says that at conformal coupling exactly one of them
is. What decides which is A.18's split into radial and transverse pressures, now computed. The contact
contraction there is negative, and it is the contraction built the same way in both geometries, a
timelike direction plus a transverse one, so the anisotropy inherits the sign, $X=-2A$ comes out
positive, and the null convergence condition holds along Penrose's ingoing radial congruence. It
fails along the contact direction instead, which is where the conjecture wanted it to fail. This
appendix therefore claims the magnitude and the sign, with the radial half of the sign resting on
conservation rather than on a sum done in the interior.

None of that is a claim about singularities, and the reason is better than a caveat. The
contraction along Penrose's congruence comes out positive, so his hypothesis holds where he uses it
and the theorem stands untouched; what the fold's term does is defocus the contact direction, which
is a statement about the two sheets touching. The arithmetic caveat is separate. A $D^{-1}$ growth
at a caustic is where a Hadamard expansion
stops being a guide to a value, so the crossing point $D_*=c\,m^2/B_*$ is written in terms of a
coefficient this release does not evaluate. A.19 closes the larger part of that coefficient: the Green function's amplitude at the caustic is a length and
nothing else, and the length is the projection's arc length $M(\pi+2)$, so
$c_1=\sqrt\pi\,M(\pi+2)$ and $\Delta^{1/2}\to3.9004\,M\,s^{-1/2}$ on the contact geodesic. The step from that to the stress tensor's coefficient is what remains.

One thing does not carry over, and it is stated here. The proof that the maximiser sits at
$X\equiv0$ needs $C(r)=r(2\kappa-f')+2f<0$, which holds for Schwarzschild and for every
Tangherlini $n$ and fails for every $Q\neq0$ in a shell outside the Cauchy horizon. The reason is
one line: $r_+r_-=Q^2$ and $r_++r_-=2M$ give $f'(r_-)=-2\sqrt{M^2-Q^2}/r_-^2$, finite but negative
because $f$ falls through zero going outward, so $C(r_-)=2\kappa r_-+2\sqrt{M^2-Q^2}/r_-$ is
positive at every nonzero charge, with no threshold anywhere. At the radius the result is about it
survives longer: $C(M)=2s/(1+s)^2-4s^2$ with $s=\sqrt{1-Q^2/M^2}$, which stays negative until the
root of $2s^3+4s^2+2s-1=0$ at $Q/M=0.9548$. What survives at charge is one direction: the $E=0$
null geodesic is an explicit curve reaching $\pi$ at $r=M$, so contact holds for $r\leq M$ at
every charge. Whether it also reaches somewhere in $(M,r_+)$ is open, and settling it means
maximising over $X(T)$ in the charged chart, where this argument assumes the fixed-point curve
wins.

A cosmological constant has the same gap at the other boundary, and there it is sharp. Redoing the
scan in the patch adapted to the cosmological horizon, the condition at $r_c$ reduces to
$r_cf''(r_c)<2f'(r_c)$; with $\Lambda r_c^3=3r_c-6M$ from $f(r_c)=0$ that fails exactly above
$9\Lambda M^2=1/2$, where $r_c=6M$. Inside the black-hole horizon the scan stays below $-2$ at
every $\Lambda$, so what goes unproved above the threshold is only the exclusion of curves
straying beyond $r_c$. Both gaps run the same way, and it is the forgiving way: a maximiser that
leaves $X\equiv0$ delivers more angle, so the contact radius becomes a lower bound and nothing
already claimed shrinks. Neither bites where it would matter. A ten solar mass hole at the
observed $\Lambda$ has $9\Lambda M^2=2\times10^{-43}$, and at the other end, where the contact
region has already grown to the whole interior, a lower bound that cannot be improved is the
answer.

One nearby radius should be kept apart from this one, because a quick reader will run them
together. The maximal-volume slice inside Schwarzschild sits at $r=3M/2$, where the sphere has
nine-sixteenths of the horizon area [31]. That is a different construction answering a different
question, and it is not $r=M$.

Two features of the maximiser are properties of it, where connecting
curves in general do reach outside, to between $1.07$ and $1.28$ horizon radii depending on the
dimension. The maximiser never leaves the closed interior; its outermost point is $r=2M$, reached
once, at the bifurcation surface. And at $r=M$ it is the only curve that reaches $\pi$, so contact
at the outer edge of the region passes through $B$ alone. That is a statement about the boundary;
the region behaves otherwise, since deeper in there is slack, and curves missing $B$ by a finite
margin work too. At $r=0.5M$ the maximum sweep is $4.19$ and a curve displaced by $0.15$ in $X$
still returns $3.73$, comfortably past $\pi$.

*The same calculation in $D$ dimensions.* Run it in $D$ dimensions and it selects the dimension,
which is the one place in this work where the fold says anything about how many there are.
Schwarzschild-Tangherlini has $f(r)=1-(r_h/r)^{\,n}$ with $n=D-3$, a simple zero at $r_h$ and the
same causal diagram. The interior bound is unchanged, $d(\text{angle})\leq dr/(r\sqrt{|f|})$, and
substituting $w=(r/r_h)^n$ turns the leg into a Beta integral:

$$
\Delta\phi_1(r)=\int_r^{r_h}\frac{dr'}{r'\sqrt{|f|}}
=\frac1n\int_{(r/r_h)^n}^{1}\frac{dw}{\sqrt{w}\sqrt{1-w}}
=\frac2n\left[\frac\pi2-\arcsin\left(\frac{r}{r_h}\right)^{\!n/2}\right].
$$

So the whole interior, horizon to singularity, supplies exactly $\pi/n$ of turning to any causal
curve crossing it, whatever the mass. In four dimensions that is $\pi$: half a revolution, and no
causal curve inside a Schwarzschild black hole can turn further than that.

That four-dimensional statement is not ours. Hamilton and McMaken [32] derive the same integral
for null geodesics of unbounded angular momentum and invert it to a cardioid that closes on the
horizon at the observer's antipode, which is the same $\pi$. We use it as a lemma and the
$D$-dependent form above is the only part of it we add. The proper-time analogue, $\tau\leq\pi M$
from horizon to singularity, is older and standard; the two integrands differ only by a factor of
$r$, which is worth noticing, since the familiar bound is the one with the $r$ in it.

The maximiser is still $X\equiv0$ in every dimension. Inside the horizon $d\log
g^2/dr=(n/r_h)[(r_h/r)^{n+1}-1]/f-2/r$ has a positive numerator over a negative denominator plus a
negative term. That is not by itself enough, because the curves being excluded are the ones that
stray outside, so the same quantity has to be checked there: for $r>r_h$ the numerator turns
negative and $f$ turns positive, and the ratio stays negative, with the maximum over
$r\in(1.001,50)r_h$ running from $-1.06$ at $D=4$ to $-6.04$ at $D=9$. So the excursions lose in
the exterior too and the two interior legs are the whole budget.

The leg count itself does not depend on the maximiser and is better argued directly. A
future-directed causal curve has $dU\geq0$ and $dV\geq0$, so once it leaves $P$ it cannot return,
and it cannot visit both exteriors because every point of $R$ is spacelike to every point of $L$.
The itinerary is $P$, then at most one exterior, then $F$: one leg in each interior and no more.

The same monotonicity settles the count for spacetimes whose maximal extension is an infinite
tower, which was the obvious worry about charge and about $\Lambda$: a curve that could cross an
inner horizon into a fresh universe could accumulate angle without limit. It cannot. Both null
coordinates increase, so the curve joining $\Theta x$ to $x$ is confined to $|UV|\leq UV(r)$, and
a second horizon is reachable only if it sits at finite $|UV|$ in the patch built on the horizon
the fold reflects. Since $UV=-\mathrm{sgn}(f)\,e^{2\kappa r_*}$, that asks whether $r_*=\int dr/f$
stays finite there, and at a non-degenerate horizon it does not: it diverges logarithmically, so
the next horizon sits at infinite $|UV|$ and the tower behind it is unreachable. The count is two
for the charged and the de Sitter cases as well, and the only thing it needs is that the reflected
horizon be non-degenerate, which fails only at extremality and at Nariai. That budget is $2\pi/n$.
The bill is $\pi$, fixed by the antipode being a free involution of a sphere and by nothing else.
They meet only for $n\leq2$:

| $D$ | $n=D-3$ | budget $2\pi/n$ | where the sheets can touch |
|---|---|---|---|
| 4 | 1 | $2\pi$ | $r\leq r_h/2$ |
| 5 | 2 | $\pi$, approached and never reached | nowhere |
| $\geq6$ | $\geq3$ | $\leq2\pi/3$ | nowhere |

The five-dimensional entry of 5.2's figure needs care and it goes the sharp way. At $n=2$ the
two-leg budget is $\pi-2\arcsin(r/r_h)$, which is strictly below $\pi$ for every $r>0$ and reaches
it only in the limit $r\to0$. That limit is not a point of the spacetime, so the contact set is
empty there too, and four dimensions is the only one in which the sheets touch anywhere at all.
Above five the budget is short outright. The bill is $\pi$ because the antipodal map is an
involution; the budget is $\pi/n$ per leg because $f$ has a simple zero and falls as $r^{-(D-3)}$;
and the two happen to match in four dimensions, with a factor of two to spare, which is why the
contact region here is a region rather than a point.

A.16 identifies two loci at which the fold joins the sheets structurally, the bifurcation surface
and the singularity, and those are there in every dimension. The causal contact region is not. In
four dimensions it is a neighbourhood of the singularity reaching out to the midpoint of the
interior; in five and above there is none, while A.16's two loci remain untouched. That is the
clearest statement of the difference between the two kinds of joining. The fold's map still
relates the sheets at the singularity whatever the dimension, and outside four nothing can use it.

*One algebraic fact underneath all of it.* The charged case and the dimensional one look unrelated
and are the same observation about $r^2|f|$, made once now that both are on the table.
Whenever it is a **quadratic whose two real roots are the ends of the $f<0$ region**, the explicit
$r$ cancels from $r\sqrt{|f|}=\sqrt{(b-r)(r-a)}$, the leg integral is an arcsine, the total is

$$
\int_a^b\frac{dr}{\sqrt{(b-r)(r-a)}}=\pi
$$

for any $a<b$ whatever, and the contact region is exactly the lower half of the interval,
$r\leq(a+b)/2$. Checked on random pairs belonging to no metric at all. The metrics with $r^2f$
quadratic are exactly $f=A+B/r+C/r^2$, which is the Reissner-Nordstrom family with Schwarzschild
at $C=0$.

That one fact does four jobs. Schwarzschild has roots $0$ and $r_h$, both ends of the region, so
the total is $\pi$ and the midpoint is $r_h/2=M$. Charging the hole replaces the root at the
singularity with the inner horizon and changes nothing else: the total is still $\pi$, at every
charge, and the midpoint is $(r_++r_-)/2=M$, the same $M$. In five dimensions $r^2|f|=r_h^2-r^2$
is still a quadratic, but its roots are $\pm r_h$ and the region runs from $0$ to $r_h$, which is
the upper half of the root interval, so the budget is exactly half the arcsine, $\pi/2$, and that
is where the marginality comes from. From six dimensions up it is not a quadratic at all and
$\pi/n$ is not an arcsine.

*A cosmological constant, which breaks the rule the other way.* Schwarzschild-de Sitter has
$r^2|f|=2Mr-r^2+(\Lambda/3)r^4$, a quartic, so the midpoint rule does not apply and the answer
moves. It moves outward, monotonically: at $9\Lambda M^2=0.1$ the contact radius is $1.047M$ and
$0.515$ of the horizon, at $0.5$ it is $1.311M$ and $0.597$, and at $0.9$ it is $1.990M$ and
$0.778$, with the total turn rising from $\pi$ to $4.21$ over the same range. So a positive
cosmological constant deepens the contact region rather than shrinking it, and at the Nariai
point, where the two horizons merge and the quartic acquires a double root, the budget diverges
and the whole interior becomes contact-capable. The ratio runs $0.919$ at $9\Lambda M^2=0.99$ and
$0.991$ at $0.9999$. Above $9\Lambda M^2=1/2$ those figures are lower bounds rather than suprema,
for the reason given with the charged case above, and they climb toward one either way. That last
statement was worked out here for its own sake and it turns out to name the same configuration the
involution count later picks: Nariai is both the only member of the Schwarzschild-de Sitter family
the fold can fix and the only one with no forbidden outer shell. Neither calculation was looking
for the other.

Set beside the charged case that makes a clean pair. Charge leaves the fraction at exactly one
half because it keeps $r^2f$ quadratic; $\Lambda$ moves it because it does not; and extra
dimensions break it a third way, by moving the region off the span of the roots. The dividing line
is algebraic and not physical, which is worth knowing before anyone reads significance into the
one half.

*A check where this overlaps the rest of the paper.* Pure de Sitter, outside the cosmological
horizon, has $r^2|f|=r^4/\ell^2-r^2$, and substituting $u=\ell/r$ gives a total turn of exactly
$\pi/2$ for every $\ell$, so the two-leg budget is exactly $\pi$. Elliptic de Sitter sits
precisely on the threshold, never over it. That is the same statement Section 1 makes on entirely
different grounds, that antipodal points of de Sitter are spacelike separated with the bound
saturated only in the limit, and the two calculations agree where they meet.

*Where the dimension argument sits.* Arguments that single out four dimensions are a small and old genre, and this one is placed in it here. Most entries assume something
about matter or observers: Ehrenfest on stable orbits, Tangherlini on atoms, Barrow and Tipler on
undistorted wave propagation, Tegmark on predictability. The closest relative assumes neither, and
it is a century old. Weyl [33] observes that the Maxwell action is the simplest integral invariant
that exists at all, that no such invariant of that structure exists in any other dimension, and
that the four-dimensionality of the world, previously taken as accidental, becomes intelligible
through it. His selection rests on the degree of a form. This one rests on a causal budget, and
the two are independent of each other and of everything in the genre that assumes an observer. We
claim no more than that.

Two limits sit on how far to carry that. It is Schwarzschild-Tangherlini, so it says nothing about
black rings or other horizon topologies, where the transverse space is not a sphere and the
antipodal map is not the relevant involution. And the fold is built here as a four-dimensional
construction, so the higher-dimensional cases test what the argument depends on. No claim about a
higher-dimensional world is made. What they show is that the four-dimensional result is not robust
to the thing one might expect it to be robust to. It sits one dimension away from being empty.

*Spin.* The fold map itself carries to Kerr exactly, as the end of this section shows. The contact
derivation does not carry as it stands. It uses spherical symmetry twice: once to say that
$P_\perp$ is antipodal and the required angular path is $\pi$ at every point, and once to reduce
the causal question to a two-dimensional conformal problem whose answer depends only on $UV$. A
rotating interior has neither, and it has an inner horizon and a ring singularity besides.

There is a route through it, though, and the obstruction above looks fatal and is not. In the ingoing angle the budget diverges: frame dragging turns the inverse
square root into a simple pole at each horizon, and a simple pole is not integrable. In the
co-rotating angle $\phi_K=\phi-\Omega_H t$, which is the one regular at the bifurcation surface
where the optimal curve turns round, the bound is $r\sqrt N/|\Delta|$ with $N$ the norm squared of
the horizon generator. $N$ has a simple zero at $r_+$, so the integrand goes as $(r_+-r)^{-1/2}$
and the budget is finite. A first pass on that footing gives a contact radius falling from $r_c=M$
at $a=0$ to about $0.89M$ at $a=0.9$, with the fraction of the interior band it occupies falling
monotonically from exactly one half to about a third. So spin shrinks the contact region where
charge left it alone, which is the sharpest contrast available between the two.

Which isometry the fold uses is settled, and the apparent ambiguity came from naming the angle
shift by a coordinate instead of by a flow. Take it to be the flow of the axial Killing field
$\psi=\partial_\phi$ through parameter $\pi$, composed with the equatorial reflection
$E:\theta\mapsto\pi-\theta$, and there is one map rather than two. It commutes with the wedge
reflection $N$ for a reason that needs no calculation: $N$ is built from the horizon generator
$\xi=\partial_t+\Omega_H\partial_\phi$, so any isometry preserving $\xi$ preserves the Kruskal
structure and commutes with $N$; $\psi$ preserves $\xi$ because $[\partial_t,\partial_\phi]=0$
with $\Omega_H$ constant, and $E$ preserves $\partial_t$ and $\partial_\phi$ separately. So
$\Theta=N\circ e^{\pi\psi}\circ E$ squares to $N^2\circ e^{2\pi\psi}\circ E^2=\mathbb{1}$, with
nothing left to choose.

It is free without needing $N$ at all, since $e^{\pi\psi}\circ E$ fixes a point only where
$\theta=\pi/2$ and $\phi=\phi+\pi$ together, and it is an exact isometry of Kerr at every spin
because the metric functions depend on $\theta$ through $\cos^2\theta$ alone and on $\phi$ not at
all. The bifurcation surface is genuinely not round, its polar area radius exceeding its
equatorial one by $5.41$ per cent at $a=0.6M$ and $37.15$ per cent at $a=0.998M$, and the map does
not care. On that surface it is the antipodal map, which is the same transverse structure the
non-rotating case uses.

The contact radius above stays a conjecture even so, because settling the map settles the fold and
not the angular budget, and the budget on a distorted sphere is a separate calculation. What the
Kerr case needs beyond that is stated in A.11.

What this does not do: The image of infalling matter sits in $P$, which is in
the *past* of both exteriors, so the fold returns information to the past and not to the future.
That is not a solution of the information problem as usually posed and we do not offer it as one.
Structurally it is a consistency condition between a future boundary and a past one, which is the
shape already used in §3.3 and A.9.

What this narrows is the domain. These results do not all lean on the same thing, and separating
them narrows it further. The reciprocal law needs $P_\perp^2=1$ and positivity, so it is
indifferent to the state: five states, thermal, flux-carrying, wildly non-thermal, oscillating,
and a power law with no temperature at all, each satisfy $R(x,y)R(x,P_\perp y)=1$ to machine
