precision. The ninety-degree equality and the odd-multipole statement follow from it and inherit
that indifference, as does the mass-independence in the cosmology paper's §3. A.10's reduction
needs $B$. Exactly one claim in these appendices needs the KMS property, that $\alpha^2=1$ fixes
the temperature, and four need the two-sided structure: that same claim, the transparent seam, the
meeting region above, and A.16's consistency condition.

Evaporation, which is what breaks KMS, therefore threatens one line of it. The appendix survives.
Its size is $(\kappa t_{\rm evap})^{-1}\sim M^{-2}$ in Planck units, between $10^{-77}$ at
$3M_\odot$ and $10^{-94}$ at $10^{9}M_\odot$, and the violation it produces is linear in the
departure rather than discontinuous in it, unlike A.16's condition, which empties at any breaking
whatever. We have not computed the coefficient, a physical non-equilibrium correction being
nothing like the Gaussian used to test continuity, but the conclusion tolerates more than fifty
orders of magnitude of it.

What collapse takes is different, and smallness does not repair it. A hole formed by collapse has
no $P$ and no $L$, the collapsing matter replacing them, and no bifurcation surface. For such a
hole the $F\leftrightarrow P$ statement is empty, "both sheets meet in $F$" has one sheet, and
A.10's reduction has no $B$ to sit on. Those results belong first to the cosmological horizon,
which is bifurcate, which we are inside, and on which the fold supplies $P_\perp$ with nothing
chosen, and second to eternal black holes. The count above says those two are not really two: the
fold fixes a black hole's horizon only when that horizon is the cosmological one. Everything that
needs neither $B$ nor a mirror sheet survives for a collapse hole, the ringdown statements among
them, since they are boundary conditions on an exterior a settled collapse hole has. The
observational programme of the cosmology paper's §3 is untouched. The domain of this appendix was
not stated before and is now.

**A.16 The singularity is the fold's second contact, and what it requires there.** §5.3's second
joining place. A.15's map does one more thing, and it locates the fold's other contact. In Kruskal
coordinates the curvature singularity is the hyperbola $T^2-X^2=1$, whose future branch bounds $F$
and whose past branch bounds $P$; since $J$ is $(T,X)\mapsto(-T,-X)$ it exchanges the two
branches, verified on $2\times10^4$ points of the locus. So under the fold the black hole's future
singularity and the white hole's past singularity are one locus, and the fold has exactly two loci
where it joins the two sheets structurally: the bifurcation surface and the singularity. Only the
first has been worked out here.

That is a different sense of joining from A.15's, and the two should not be run together. A.15
asks where a point can causally influence its own image and answers $r\leq M$; this asks where the
fold's map relates the sheets at all, and answers at two surfaces. The singularity lies inside
A.15's region and the bifurcation surface lies outside it, at $r=2M$, so a point of $B$ is not
causally connected to its own image even though the fold carries $B$ to itself. What $B$ does is
carry the curve: the one that attains A.15's bound passes through it. A.15's domain caveat applies
with full force: a hole formed by collapse has no past singularity to be identified with, so for
an astrophysical black hole this section says nothing.

That changes the character of the interior boundary condition without resolving anything. A
single-sheet theory must say what happens at the singularity as a terminal condition. The fold
instead requires the data on that locus to be compatible with its own image under $J$, which is a
self-consistency requirement of the kind §3.3 already uses, and which is the interior counterpart
of $\alpha^2=1$ being the smoothness condition at $B$. It does not resolve the singularity: the
curvature still diverges there in both sheets, and identifying two divergences does not remove
them.

That requirement does have solutions, and counting them is finite work. Data leaves the past
branch, crosses under the evolution $U$, and reaches the future branch, where $J$ returns it, so
consistency reads $Ux=Jx$, equivalently $(JU)x=x$ since $J^2=1$. If the evolution is covariant
under the fold's map, $JUJ=U^{-1}$, then $(JU)^2=1$ and the condition becomes an eigenvalue
problem for an involution. We build it explicitly on a $2n$-dimensional phase space with $J$ the
antisymplectic involution $(q,p)\mapsto(q,-p)$ and $U=\exp A$ with $A$ anticommuting with $J$
inside $\mathfrak{sp}(2n)$, which is an ordinary kinetic-plus-potential system: $(JU)^2=1$ to the
arithmetic floor, the spectrum is $\pm1$ with no imaginary part, and the $+1$ eigenspace has
dimension exactly $n$.

Nothing observed sets that dimension; the counting does. For an involution
$\operatorname{tr}M=\dim(+1)-\dim(-1)$, so the split is even exactly when
$\operatorname{tr}(JU)=0$. Since $A$ anticommutes with $J$, $A^2$ is block diagonal with blocks
$bc$ and $cb$; the diagonal blocks of $\exp A$ are the same series in each; and $bc$ and $cb$
share a spectrum, so the two traces cancel. It is the identity
$\operatorname{tr}f(XY)=\operatorname{tr}f(YX)$ and nothing more. Checked at $n=2,5,9,16$ over two
hundred draws apiece, with $\operatorname{tr}(JU)$ and $(JU)^2-1$ both at the floor of whichever
matrix exponential is used, and the dimension $n$ every time. It remains $n$ when $b$ and $c$ are
not symmetric, so the count comes from $A$ anticommuting with $J$ rather than from the mechanics
placed inside it, and letting $A$ commute with $J$ instead breaks both the involution and the
trace. The condition is therefore neither empty nor over-determined. It keeps exactly half the
data, through the projector $(1+JU)/2$.

That projector has the form of the Keldysh split the cosmology paper's §2.1 already uses,
$\Phi_c=(\Phi+\Theta\Phi)/2$, with the loop's involution standing where $\Theta$ stands; the two
differ by the evolution between the branches, and we record the parallel, claiming no
identification. What the interior wants is a projection, and the projection is the even sector of
a $\mathbb{Z}_2$, as at $B$.

Covariance is load-bearing here. Adding to $A$ a piece that commutes with $J$ instead of
anticommuting, with strength $\epsilon$, destroys the involution and the solution space with it:
the kernel has dimension $n$ at $\epsilon=0$ and is empty at $\epsilon=10^{-6}$ and above, with
the smallest singular value of $JU-1$ bounded away from zero rather than nearly degenerate. The
covariance at issue is not $T$ but $\Theta$, which is CPT, so it comes from a theorem and is
untouched by $T$ violation in the kaon system. The interior condition has solutions because CPT
holds and would have none otherwise, which puts weight on that assumption in a second place
independent of §2's.

One reading of that projector needs checking, because it is the natural one and it is wrong in
general. A projection that keeps half the data invites being read as a measurement, with the
singularity collapsing the state onto its even sector. That requires the projector to be
self-adjoint, and $(1+JU)/2$ is not: $U$ is symplectic rather than symmetric, $JU$ inherits that,
and the projector comes out idempotent but oblique, with range and kernel that are not orthogonal
complements. An oblique projection carries no Born rule and is not a measurement.

It becomes one in exactly one limit. The obliquity, measured as the departure of the principal
angles from orthogonality, is $0.9^\circ$ at evolution parameter $0.01$ and grows to $82^\circ$ at
$2$, vanishing only as the evolution between the branches does. So the collapse reading is exact
in the constraint formulation and nowhere else. That is the cosmology paper's A.4 commitment
again, arriving from a fourth direction and independently of the three recorded there.

All of this is linear, classical and finite-dimensional. An interacting field theory is not a
$12\times12$ matrix, and nothing here shows the count survives that.

One compatibility question belongs here, because it has a sharp answer. The condition above keeps
the $+1$ eigenspace of $JU$, and A.10 records that $\Theta$-invariance keeps the $+1$ eigenspace
of $\Theta$. Whether one state can satisfy both turns entirely on how the sheet swap inside
$\Theta$ sits against the time reversal in $J$. Measuring that rather than assuming it gives three
cases. If the two anticommute, the common sector is empty at every separation
including zero. If the swap is trivial, the sector is full. If the swap *is* the time reversal,
the sector is full at zero separation and empty otherwise.

A.15 settles which case applies. The fold's map is $(T,X)\mapsto(-T,-X)$, effecting
$R\leftrightarrow L$ and $F\leftrightarrow P$ together, so the swap is not an independent
involution standing alongside the time reversal but the same map acting on the wedges. That is the
third case, and the two conditions therefore share a sector exactly when there is no evolution
between the branches: the Wheeler-DeWitt situation, where the state solves a constraint and there
is no $t$ to evolve along.

This is the third calculation to reach the cosmology paper's A.4 requirement, and it is recorded
there, so the argument is not repeated. The conflict is not general. It was a feature of a model
in which the swap and the time reversal were independent and anticommuting, a choice made without
examining it, and one A.15 had already excluded.

**A.17 Whether the fold can dispense with the Hawking temperature.** §4's question about the
Hawking temperature. Identifying the two singular branches invites the question of whether matter
may transit the locus instead of being radiated away, making the Hawking temperature dispensable.
The answer here is partial, and the limit is the fold's own. A.10 shows that in the Bunch-Davies
state $\alpha^2=1$ can be *read as* $W(t-i\beta)=W(t)$, the KMS condition, at the primitive period
$\beta=2\pi/\kappa$ and, as A.10 now records, at its odd multiples. The reading is what a
non-thermal state gives up, and not the identity: $\alpha^2=1$ holds because $J$ and $P_\perp$ are
commuting involutions, which is a statement about the geometry and is true whatever state the
field is in. The involution itself does not break, which reopens the question it was meant to
settle. In frequency space that is detailed balance, $\widetilde
W(-\omega)=e^{-\beta\omega}\widetilde W(\omega)$, and a departure from the thermal occupation
violates it at first order with no tolerant window: a fractional departure $10^{-4}$ gives a
violation $8.55\times10^{-5}$, and $10^{-2}$ gives $8.55\times10^{-3}$. So a state that departs
from the thermal occupation loses the detailed-balance reading quickly. What it does not lose is
$\alpha^2=1$. The fold's involution property therefore does not by itself make the Hawking
temperature compulsory, and the argument that it did is withdrawn. Whether the temperature can
actually be dispensed with, with the locus absorbing what would have been radiated, is left open
here, and turns on the statement of domain below rather than on the involution property.

Flux is a separate matter and falls outside the domain, unsettled. An equilibrium state at $T_H$
is KMS and carries no net flux; an evaporating hole sits in a state that is not KMS in Killing
time, so $\alpha^2=1$ fails for it and A.10 to A.15 do not apply. That is the same boundary this
appendix records for collapse holes, reached from a different direction. And the transit cannot
substitute for evaporation in any case, since the image of infalling matter lies in $P$, in the
past of both exteriors, while evaporation is a statement about the future. The two answer
different questions.

What that leaves is a sharper statement of domain, and a narrower one than it may look. The
restriction bites on the KMS-dependent claim and on nothing else: A.10's audit of what each result
leans on finds exactly one that needs KMS, namely $\alpha^2=1$ fixing the temperature, which is
this appendix's subject. The cosmological horizon is in equilibrium and genuinely KMS, and so is
an eternal hole in the Hartle-Hawking state, so that claim holds without qualification on both.
What it does not cover is a hole that formed and will evaporate. Extending to astrophysical holes
needs the departure of their state from equilibrium, of order $(\kappa t_{\rm evap})^{-1}$, to
degrade the predictions smoothly rather than void them. That calculation would widen the domain
and is not done here.


**A.18 The image singularity at a caustic, computed without a parametrix.** §5.3's withdrawn
power, recovered. The reading under test is that the fold censors its own closed causal curves:
the world function between a point and its image vanishes linearly at the contact sphere, so the
image term of the two-point function blows up there and the stress tensor with it. Counting the
blow-up through the Hadamard parametrix gives an image term $\Delta^{1/2}/\sigma$ and a stress
tensor $s^{-3}$, and that count needs $\Delta$ finite at the image point, which it is not. The fold's transverse factor is the antipodal map on a round $S^2$, every great
circle through a point refocuses at its antipode, and the image point sits on the caustic.

What fails there is an approximation. A parametrix carries a Van Vleck factor and a
mode sum does not, so the exact object can be computed where the approximate one cannot, and the
two ingredients that computation needs are both now in hand. The state is the first, and A.8
settles it: at a bifurcate Killing horizon the propagation gives the thermofield double at $\tanh
r=e^{-\beta\omega/2}$, carrying the transverse parity that separates $J\circ P_\perp$ from $J$,
which on the cross-sheet term is the insertion of $(-1)^{\ell}$ per multipole. That insertion and
the antipodal map are one operation, by $P_\ell(-x)=(-1)^{\ell}P_\ell(x)$, which holds to
$4.6\times10^{-14}$ over the cases checked. That is the identity §3 already uses to empty the
even multipoles of the horizon law, doing a second job here. The second is a geometry in which the sum closes.

The geometry used is $-dt^2+dr^2+a^2d\Omega^2$, which is not Schwarzschild and is not offered as
it. It is the smallest geometry carrying the feature at issue: null geodesics leaving a point
spread over every great circle of the transverse sphere and refocus, all together, after exactly
$\pi$ of transverse angle, so the connecting family at the image point is one-parameter, the same
degeneracy the contact sphere carries. Reducing on the sphere leaves a tower of two-dimensional
fields of mass $m_\ell=\sqrt{\ell(\ell+1)}/a$ and

> $$
> W(x,x')=\sum_\ell \frac{2\ell+1}{4\pi}\,P_\ell(\cos\gamma)\,W_2(m_\ell;\Delta t,\Delta r).
> $$

The tower's terms grow as $\sqrt{\ell}$ at the image pair, because the antipodal parity cancels
the transverse phase and every multipole arrives in step. That coherence is the singularity, so a
taper in $\ell$ would not regulate the sum, it would set the answer. The Wightman function's own
$i\epsilon$ is the regulator that belongs to the object, and it damps the tower by
$e^{-m_\ell\epsilon}$; taken at a fortieth of the offset and varied over a factor of four it
moves the sum by $1.8\times10^{-3}$.

Both configurations are approached from outside the lightcone by an offset $s$, and the
comparison is like with like because the world function vanishes linearly in $s$ in each:
$\sigma\simeq s$ at the generic pair, where the separation is $\Delta r=1$ and $\Delta t=1-s$,
and $\sigma\simeq\pi s$ at the image pair, where it is the full $\pi$ of transverse angle
against $\Delta t=\pi-s$. So the two fitted powers are powers of the same kind of variable, which
is also the variable §5.3's linear zero of $\sigma$ is written in. A generic null
pair, no caustic, returns a fitted power $-1.0095$ against the known $-1$, which is the case that
could have failed and the reason the other number means anything. The image pair returns $-1.4920$
on the widest window and sharpens to $-1.49674$ and then $-1.49874$ as the window closes, with the
largest log-residual falling from $2.6\times10^{-3}$ to $4\times10^{-4}$: the power is $-3/2$.
Removing the antipodal parity by hand, leaving the configuration otherwise untouched, removes the
divergence altogether at $+0.0004$.

The coefficient carries information too. Summed at a fixed offset, the argument of the result
halves each time $\epsilon$ halves, tracked over four halvings with the product of phase and
regulator constant to $3\times10^{-3}$, so the phase belongs to the regulator and the limit sits
on the positive real axis while the magnitude holds at $502$. The divergence is real and positive
and does not oscillate.

So the caustic steepens the divergence by half a power: its image term
goes as $s^{-3/2}$ where the parametrix had $s^{-1}$, and a stress tensor quadratic in derivatives
goes as $s^{-7/2}$ where the parametrix count had $s^{-3}$. So the self-censoring reading holds with a steeper
exponent than the parametrix count gives it, on a calculation that carries no Van Vleck factor at
all and therefore does not care that the parametrix fails here.

The same sum gives the components, which is what the sign needs, since a positive coefficient in
the two-point function is not a sign for the stress tensor. Three scalars carry the whole tensor at
the image pair, the isotropy there being the $SO(2)$ of rotations about the axis through the point
and its antipode: the second time derivative $D$, the second transverse derivative $Q$ and the
second radial derivative $E$. The transverse Hessian is isotropic at the antipode because
$P_\ell(\cos\gamma)$ there is a function of the squared distance from it,
$\cos\gamma=-1+|u-v|^2/2$ with $u$ and $v$ the tangent displacements of the two ends, so
differentiating once at each end and twice at one end return the same number; parallel transport
along the connecting geodesic reverses a great circle's own tangent, which puts the two on opposite
signs. Measured against the exact Legendre function that relation holds to $10^{-4}$ at $\ell$ from
1 to 40, and the transport is checked in $\mathbb R^3$.

Two identities fix the fold and neither is imposed. Each mode obeys its two-dimensional field
equation and carries the sphere's eigenvalue, so $\Box W=-D+E-2Q$ must equal $(m^2+\xi R)W$
exactly. It does, to $3\times10^{-7}$ against a $D$ of $6\times10^{5}$, and scaling $Q$ by a
thousandth breaks it. That in turn forces the trace here to be $T^a{}_a=-m^2W$, so in this
geometry a massless field's image stress is traceless at every offset and not only at leading
order, and the computed trace sits at $4.8\times10^{-14}$ of the energy density. The restriction
to this geometry is not cosmetic: the step uses $g^{cd'}W_{;cd'}=-\Box W$, which holds because $W$
depends on the coordinate difference here, and A.19's general trace, carrying
$(6\xi-1)\tfrac12\Box\langle\phi^2\rangle$ with the image point moving alongside the point, is
the one that governs a hole. The image term itself reproduces this
appendix's own $501.9$ at an offset of $10^{-3}$ and a fitted power of $-1.4916$, and the
non-caustic controls return $-0.9934$ and $-2.999$ where the parametrix predicts $-1$ and $-3$.

What comes out is a traceless, zero-density, purely anisotropic divergence. $D$ and $Q$ both go as
$s^{-7/2}$ with $Q=-D/2$, $E$ goes as $s^{-5/2}$ and drops out, and the components are
$\rho=-15s^{-7/2}/32\pi\sqrt{2\pi}=-0.059525\,s^{-7/2}$, $T^r{}_r=0$ and
$T^\theta{}_\theta=-\rho/2$: a pattern
$T^t{}_t:T^r{}_r:T^\theta{}_\theta=2:0:-1$, returned as $1.9997:0.0003:-1$. Both of that
geometry's null contractions are negative, $-D$ along its radial direction and $-D+Q$ along the
contact one, in the ratio $1.5003$ against $3/2$. Neither sign moves with the coupling or the mass, checked at
six pairs. The flat calibration's sign flips as $\xi$ crosses $1/6$ because it is proportional to
$m^2+(\xi-\tfrac16)R$; a caustic's cannot, because $\xi$ and $m$ enter the tower only through a
constant shift of $\mu_\ell^2$ and through terms two powers softer than the divergence.

One contraction carries over to a hole and one does not, and what decides which is causal character.
Inside a horizon $f<0$, so $r$ is timelike and $t$ spacelike, and the same three components mean
different things. The contact geodesic there
has $E=f\dot t=0$ with $f$ nonzero, so its tangent is a timelike direction plus a transverse one,
which is what the contact direction is here; this geometry's radial direction is its spacelike one
and its transverse sphere has constant radius, so it carries no tidal field and its conservation law
is empty. The contact contraction therefore carries over, the anisotropy at a hole is negative, and
A.19's conservation chain puts $X=-2A$ above zero.

What does not carry over is the component pattern, and the reason also explains where the pattern comes from. Near a null caustic the leading term collapses to
$T_{ab}=-C\tfrac{15}{4}\sigma^{-7/2}\nabla_a\sigma\nabla_b\sigma$, the trace piece being two
powers softer since $\nabla\sigma\cdot\nabla\sigma=2\sigma$, so the leading stress is rank one
along the connecting geodesic and traceless. At a caustic the connecting geodesics are a family, so
what acts is the average of $k_ak_b$ over it, which is $\mathrm{diag}(1,0,\tfrac12,\tfrac12)$ in
the frame the contact tangent picks out, and that is $2:0:-1$ exactly, with no sum required. Read
in a hole's labels the timelike slot is $r$, so the pattern would put the $2$ on $T^r{}_r$; and
then conservation forbids it, the $\mathrm{d}T^r{}_r/\mathrm{d}r$ term carrying $D^{-9/2}$ against
$D^{-7/2}$ for the other two, so the ratio of what could cancel it to what must be cancelled falls
as $5.8\times10^{-3}$, $5.7\times10^{-4}$, $5.7\times10^{-5}$ over three decades. Here that check
is empty, because the coefficient does not depend on position and the transverse radius is
constant.

What that rules out is the rank-one form as a hole's leading term, and not the pattern, which
conservation returns by a route of its own. Three statements fix it and the rank-one form is none
of them: the leading divergence is traceless, since $T^a{}_a=-m^2\langle\phi^2\rangle$ is two
powers soft; the transverse pressures are equal, from the $SO(2)$ about the radial direction; and
$T^r{}_r$ vanishes at leading order, which is what $Y'=(f'/2f)X+2A/r$ gives, putting $Y$ one power
below $X$ and $A$ so that $Y/X$ falls as $D$. The trace then reads
$T^t{}_t+2T^\theta{}_\theta=0$ and the pattern is forced. So both geometries empty the same slot
and both are traceless, for reasons with nothing in common: here because the radial direction is
not in the connecting tangent at all, at a hole because conservation empties it while the tangent
runs straight through it. Two unrelated routes to one pattern is worth more than a transfer would
have been.

One more thing has to match for a sign to carry, and it is the caustic count, because a crossing
supplies a phase and the leading real part is what holds the sign. Every scalar above takes the
same phase, so the pattern and the ratio $3/2$ do not feel it at all: a uniform extra $\theta$
multiplies each real part by $\cos\theta$, which vanishes at $\pi/2$ and turns over beyond it.
This geometry has one crossing, the transverse one, and it is carried by $P_\ell(-1)=(-1)^\ell$
itself. A hole would have a second if a second direction refocused there, and it does not. The count
is the dimension of the family of null geodesics running from a point to its image, so geodesic
integration measures it.

In this geometry the sphere's share of a null direction has to be exactly
all of it for the image to be reached at $\Delta r=0$, which leaves one free parameter, the choice
of great circle, and two circles tilted by $\delta$ separate as $\delta\sin$ of the arc and close
again at $\pi$. In the interior the same is true for the same reason: spherical symmetry makes the
angular sweep a function of $r$ alone, so every tilted member reaches $\pi$ at $r=M$, while giving the geodesic an energy penalises it twice over: $\dot r^2$ at the horizon is $E^2$ rather than zero, so
the member does not turn there and leaves the interior instead of entering the other one, and the
angle it sweeps on the way out is smaller at every radius because $\dot r$ is larger. At $E=1$ the
sweep falls short of $\pi$ by $2.268$ and at $E=0.01$ by $0.0777$. One degenerate direction in each.

Why the second direction fails to focus differs between the two and does not need to agree. Here
it is flat, the $2d$ factor being Minkowski, so its Jacobi field is $\lambda$ and never returns.
There it defocuses, and it has to, because Schwarzschild is Ricci-flat: $R_{ab}k^ak^b=0$ leaves the
optical tidal matrix trace-free, so one screen direction focuses exactly as much as the other
defocuses. Both $R_{tt}=f(f''/2+f'/r)$ and $R_{\theta\theta}=1-f-rf'$ vanish identically for
$f=1-2M/r$, and adding charge gives $R_{\theta\theta}=Q^2/r^2$, which does not, so the test has
something to catch.

Equal counts, so no relative phase from a conjugate point. One difference between the two geometries
has to be named and disposed of before that is worth anything. On Schwarzschild the connecting curve
turns in $r$, its $\dot r$ vanishing at the horizon where $|f|$ does, and nothing here corresponds.
That turning belongs to the Killing slicing and not to the curve: in Kruskal coordinates the
bifurcation region is smooth, the connecting curve runs through it as one smooth null geodesic, and
$r$ merely happens to take a maximum along the way. A caustic is where a Jacobi field vanishes and
none does there. Reality caps what could still go wrong: a Wightman function at spacelike separation
is real, so any residual phase is a multiple of $\pi$ and the sign either carries exactly or turns
over exactly, with nothing between.

And it carries, because the quantity whose phase this is can be computed both ways and comes out
the same. A.19's rule replaces the divergent Van Vleck factor at a
caustic by $\Delta^{1/2}\to\Delta'^{1/2}\sqrt\pi\,Ls^{-1/2}$, with $L$ the projection's arc length
and $\Delta'^{1/2}$ the reduced factor of the one non-degenerate transverse direction. Applied to
A.18's geometry that direction is flat, so $\Delta'^{1/2}=1$ exactly, the projection is a great
semicircle, and the rule predicts $\pi^{3/2}$ with nothing left to choose. Feeding it through the
proper-time integral at $\sigma=\pi s$ returns $1/8\pi\sqrt{2\pi}$, which is A.18's mode-sum
coefficient, to sixteen digits and as an exact cancellation rather than a numerical match. The
affine total in place of $L$ misses by $2.773$, a Van Vleck factor of $0.428$ by that factor, and
dropping the $\sqrt\pi$ by $0.564$. So the image term is the same positive expression in both, for
the same reason: a reduced Van Vleck factor is a square root, $\sqrt\pi$ is positive and an arc
length is positive.

Were it to turn over, the two contractions would swap roles, since A.19's conservation chain makes
exactly one of them negative whichever way this goes. The
structure of the conclusion is fixed and only the assignment rests on the transfer. What is left is that the radial half rests on conservation
rather than on a sum done in the interior, and the same sum on Schwarzschild would remove that
step. Its hardest ingredient is now in hand. At zero frequency and in the eikonal limit the
interior's radial equation is $r(1-r)\psi''-\psi'+\ell(\ell+1)\psi=0$ with $2M=1$, which is
hypergeometric with exponents $\{0,2\}$ at the singularity and the double exponent $\{0,0\}$ at the
horizon, and its regular branch is the polynomial $\psi_\ell=r^2P^{(2,0)}_{\ell-1}(1-2r)$. Jacobi
asymptotics then give the phase in closed form, $2\nu\arcsin\sqrt r-5\pi/4$ with $\nu=\ell+1/2$,
whose variable part is exactly §5's leg from $r$ out to the horizon. Zeros fix a phase only modulo
$\pi$, so the constant is pinned by the amplitude as well: the full Darboux form matches to
$0.0040$ at $\ell=800$ against $1.9991$ for the candidate a half-turn away, which is as far apart
as two cosines reach. What the fold still needs beyond it is the measure and the saddle of the
frequency integral, whose stationary point for equal-time endpoints is $E=0$.


**A.19 The energy density of the image term, and what sets its size.** A.18 settles the sign in its
own geometry, which A.15 calls the half that decides whether the divergence closes the region or
merely marks it. Getting there turns up a larger question. Four settings are computed below, two returning zero and
two returning a divergence, and what separates them is whether the field is conformally invariant,
which is a statement about matter content. So
this appendix runs in three parts: what makes the density vanish, what exponent the divergence
carries where it does not, and what its magnitude is. Answers come for the first two. Magnitude
is settled wherever the caustic sits in a Riemannian factor, and at a hole it is shown to need a
treatment of a different kind.

The energy density a static observer measures for a massless minimally coupled scalar is
$\tfrac12[(\partial_t\phi)^2+(\partial_r\phi)^2+|\nabla_\perp\phi|^2]$, three terms that are
positive classically. Point-split against the image kernel, each becomes a mixed derivative
$\partial_\mu^x\partial_\nu^y G(x,\Theta y)$ at $y=x$, and the fold decides the sign of each
through its own pullback. Taking $\Theta(t,r,n)=(T-t,r,-n)$, which is an isometry, an involution,
free, and reverses time orientation the way the real fold does, the three pullbacks are
$\partial_t^y=-\partial_{t'}$, $\partial_r^y=+\partial_{r'}$, and $\partial_n^y=-\partial_{n'}$,
the last because the antipodal map's differential is minus the identity.

The angular contraction is the piece that decides everything. At the antipode the first term of
the mixed derivative of $P_\ell(\cos\gamma)$ drops, since the projection of $n'$ off $n$ vanishes
when $n'=-n$, and what survives is $2P_\ell'(-1)=-(-1)^{\ell}\ell(\ell+1)$: the transverse sphere
hands back exactly its own Laplacian eigenvalue. That is the step the whole cancellation turns on,
so it is checked by differencing on the sphere rather than by the projection argument alone, and
agrees for $\ell$ up to twelve to $3.9\times10^{-5}$. So the three pieces are $W_{\tau\tau}$,
$-W_{\rho\rho}$ and $+\ell(\ell+1)a^{-2}W$, and that combination is the wave operator, which
annihilates the two-point function. The image term carries no energy density at all, at every
separation including the caustic, so the divergence A.18 found is invisible in $\rho$ here.
The three pieces run to $1.9\times10^{9}$ at the smallest offset and their sum is $2\times10^{-12}$
of that. Bessel identities supply the derivatives rather than the wave equation, so the
cancellation is evidence rather than restatement of it; flipping each pullback sign in turn moves the
sum by exactly the piece flipped, to one part in $10^{6}$; and dropping the time reversal from
$\Theta$, which leaves a perfectly good isometry, leaves the density non-zero at
$6.7\times10^{6}$.

A zero reached that way is a statement about the geometry and not about the fold. The wave
operator appeared because the transverse sphere supplies the whole of the radial equation's
potential here, leaving no remainder, and the test is to give the tower a mass the sphere does not
supply. Exactly that much survives:

> $$
> \rho_{\rm img}=-\tfrac12\,\mu^2\sum_\ell \frac{2\ell+1}{4\pi}(-1)^{\ell}\,W(m_\ell;\xi),
> $$

which the assembled sum reproduces to $2.6\times10^{-6}$ at $\mu=0.5$ and to
$6.0\times10^{-8}$ at $\mu=2$, the agreement tightening as the residue grows.

So the sign question has a smaller shape than it had, in this model. Everything in the energy
density cancels against the wave operator except the part of the radial potential the transverse
sphere does not supply, and the sign is that remainder's sign times the A.18 sum, which is real
and positive.

The cancellation was assembled where the transverse radius is a constant, and that turns out to
be the whole of why it happens. Flat four-dimensional Minkowski is the same calculation with the
radius varying: written in spherical coordinates it is $-dt^2+dr^2+r^2d\Omega^2$, its fold is
$\Theta(t,\mathbf x)=(T-t,-\mathbf x)$, a time reflection composed with the spatial point
reflection, and its differential is minus the identity on every Cartesian component, which in
spherical terms is exactly the three pullbacks above. Nothing about the fold has changed except
the transverse radius, which is now $r$, and everything is in closed form, so the step
can be taken here. With all four pullbacks at $-1$ the fold is
$\rho_{\rm img}=\tfrac12[G_{\Delta t\Delta t}+\nabla^2 G]$, the wave equation in the separation
turns that into $\partial^2 G/\partial(\Delta t)^2$, and at the image pair, where
$\Delta\mathbf x=-2\mathbf x$ and $\Delta t=\xi$,

> $$
> \rho_{\rm img}=\frac{8r^2+6\xi^2}{4\pi^2 D^3},\qquad D=4r^2-\xi^2.
> $$

The symbolic assembly and the wave-equation route agree with that expression exactly, each
difference returning zero. The numerator is positive at every $r$ and
$\xi$ and $D$ is positive outside the contact surface, so the image energy density is positive
there and diverges positively on approach, at a fitted power of $-3.000052$ in $D$ over five
halvings, the shortfall being the numerator moving with $\xi$ at finite offset. Three is the
generic exponent rather than A.18's $-7/2$, and it should be: a straight line is the only geodesic
joining a point to its image here, so there is no refocusing and no caustic. That is also a check
across the two appendices, by methods with nothing in common. A.18's control fits a mode sum and
returns $-1.0095$ for the two-point function, so $-3.009$ for a stress tensor; this returns
$-3.000052$ from a closed form with no sum in it. The generic case is the one place the two
calculations overlap and they agree there.

So the sign has an answer in the one setting where everything is exact, and the answer is
positive. One thing it rests on should be named, because it is a statement about the state and not
about the geometry: the image term is taken to enter with a positive coefficient, and the
propagation of A.8 is what supplies it. The cross-sheet correlator there is the
direct one shifted by half a period, $W(t-i\beta/2)=\cos\omega t/2\omega\sinh(\beta\omega/2)$,
positive at coincidence. A negative one would flip the sign of
everything below. Two further limits travel with the result. This model has no caustic, so it supplies the sign where A.18 supplied the power,
while A.18 supplies the power and not the sign, and neither is the black hole. And its fold fixes
the single event $(T/2,\mathbf 0)$ where the real one is free, which changes nothing at the
contact surface but makes the model globally unfaithful. What both models agree on is that the
zero of the paragraph above is a property of a constant transverse radius and not of the fold.

Doing the fold in general settles that. Take any static spherically symmetric metric, $-h\,dt^2+dr^2/f+R^2d\Omega^2$, which covers
Schwarzschild, flat space and the constant-radius model at once, and keep the same three
pullbacks. Mode by mode the density is
$\tfrac1{2h}\partial_\tau^2g_\ell+\tfrac f2\partial_r\partial_{r'}g_\ell
+\tfrac{\ell(\ell+1)}{2R^2}g_\ell$, and the wave equation for the $\ell$-mode writes the time
term as the radial part of the wave operator minus $\ell(\ell+1)g_\ell/R^2$. The two angular
pieces then cancel against each other exactly, at every $h$, $f$ and $R$, leaving

> $$
> \rho_{\rm img}=\tfrac12\sum_\ell \frac{2\ell+1}{4\pi}(-1)^{\ell}
> \left[\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathcal G}}\partial_r\!\left(\sqrt{\mathcal G}\,f\,\partial_r
> g_\ell\right)+f\,\partial_r\partial_{r'}g_\ell\right],
> \qquad \sqrt{\mathcal G}=\sqrt{h/f}\,R^2 .
> $$

So the transverse sphere pays for the angular part of the potential in every geometry of this
class, and no $\ell$ survives in the density at all. The remainder is not the curvature term and
not any part of the potential: the wave equation was used to remove the time derivative, and what
it leaves behind is the radial operator, not $V_\ell$. What is left is the difference between that
operator and the mixed radial derivative, and nothing else.

Both earlier cases follow from it. With $R$ constant and $h=f=1$ the mode function depends on $r$
and $r'$ only through $r'-r$, so $\partial_r\partial_{r'}g=-\partial_r^2g$ and the radial pair
cancels as well: that is the identical zero above, needing the constant radius exactly where this
appendix said it did. Let $R$ vary and the measure contributes $R'/R$ terms with no counterpart in
the mixed derivative, the cancellation stops, and a density is left, which is the flat-space
answer. On Schwarzschild the radial operator is
$\partial_r^2g+\tfrac2r\partial_rg-\tfrac{2M}{r}\partial_r^2g-\tfrac{2M}{r^2}\partial_rg$.

That is a smaller problem than the one this appendix opened with, and the rest of it can be
settled by asking which of the two answers above a black hole is entitled to.

The flat case is not a toy, which was not noticed when it was written. Its fold
$\Theta(t,\mathbf x)=(-t,-\mathbf x)$ is $\mathrm{diag}(-1,-1,-1,-1)$, and the wedge reflection
for the $x_1$-wedge composed with the transverse antipode is
$\mathrm{diag}(-1,-1,+1,+1)\cdot\mathrm{diag}(+1,+1,-1,-1)$, the same matrix. **The flat-space
calculation above is the fold's own fold, at a Rindler horizon, in closed form.** Its
positive answer is therefore an exact statement about the fold at a horizon and not about a model
resembling one.

A third geometry is exactly solvable where the interior is not, and it decides between them. On
the Einstein static universe a conformally coupled massless scalar has
$\omega_n=n/a$ exactly with degeneracy $n^2$, and the $S^3$ addition theorem at the antipode
returns $(2\pi^2a^3)^{-1}n^2(-1)^{n+1}$, so the antipodal parity arrives on its own rather than
being inserted. The sum closes, and it closes at general angle:

> $$
> G=\frac{1}{8\pi^2a^2(\cos\eta-\cos\gamma)},\qquad
> G_{\rm img}=\frac{1}{16\pi^2a^2\cos^2(\eta/2)},\qquad \eta=\Delta t/a .
> $$

The Abel-summed series matches that to $1.2\times10^{-5}$ at five angles well away from the
antipode, and its coincidence limit returns the flat $1/4\pi^2s^2$ to eight figures. That part
holds, and the image term diverges where the null geodesics refocus.

The density needs every term of the point-split operator, and the transverse one is easy to
mislay. Homogeneity of $S^3$ says that the total derivative along the diagonal vanishes,
$G_{\alpha\alpha}+2G_{\alpha\beta}+G_{\beta\beta}=0$, which constrains the Laplacian of
$\phi^2$ and leaves the mixed derivative to be computed. It is $-\delta^{ij}/8\pi^2D^2$ where
the time term carries $+\delta^{ij}$, so it cancels the fourth inverse power exactly, and the
conformal improvement $-\xi\nabla_s^2(\phi^2)+\xi G_{tt}\phi^2$ cancels what is left. **The
image stress of a conformally coupled scalar on the Einstein static universe is zero, identically
in $\eta$.**

The operator returning that zero returns the right nonzero answers elsewhere, which is what makes the zero believable. On a single Dirichlet mirror in flat space it gives $-1/16\pi^2z^4$ at
minimal coupling and zero at conformal coupling, both of which are in the textbooks. On this
appendix's own flat inversion it gives $+1/32\pi^2R^4$ and $+1/96\pi^2R^4$, so the flat inversion
carries a density and the Einstein static universe does not. Moving $\xi$ off
$1/6$ moves the zero with it, in the density and in the null-null component alike, so the
cancellation is conformal invariance and not an operator that returns zero whatever it is handed.
Two implementations agree, the algebra above and finite differences taken on the exact $S^3$
embedding with nothing differentiated by hand.

The zero is the value at one coupling of something with an exact closed form. Since
$\nabla_s^2(\phi^2)$ vanishes identically, the whole $\xi$ dependence sits in one term and

> $$
> \rho_{\rm img}=\frac{6\xi-1}{16\pi^2a^2(1+\cos\eta)},\qquad
> T_{kk}=\frac{(1-6\xi)(1-\cos\eta)}{8\pi^2a^4(1+\cos\eta)^2},
> $$

both reproducing the assembled operator at six couplings including two outside $[0,1/6]$. **The
image stress is proportional to the departure from conformal coupling**, rather than merely
vanishing at it. At $\xi=0$ the density is $-1/32\pi^2a^2\cos^2(\eta/2)$, negative and going as
$\delta^{-2}$, while the null-null component keeps the fourth power and is positive, which is the
sign that focuses a null congruence.

The Hadamard expansion says where that factor comes from. Its log coefficient is
$V_0=\Delta^{1/2}[m^2+(\xi-\tfrac16)R]/2$, and a massless conformal field in a Ricci-flat
spacetime has nothing to put in it. Here $R=6/a^2$, so $(\xi-\tfrac16)R$ is the whole of the
breaking and $1-6\xi$ is its measure. The same slot carries $m^2$, which is why A.18 finds a
divergence where this finds none: its tower carries masses $m_\ell=\sqrt{\ell(\ell+1)}/a$. So the
constant-radius zero that opens this appendix is the same phenomenon rather than an artefact of a
product geometry.

That reading has a limit, and it is the parametrix's own. Everything above is the Hadamard log
coefficient, and at a caustic the divergence is not the log term but $\Delta^{1/2}/\sigma$, whose
coefficient is one whatever the field is. Two things then separate this appendix's geometry from a
hole's. This one is conformally flat, so a conformal field's image stress maps to the flat one and
vanishes; and its antipodal pair at equal time is spacelike separated, so there is no
$\sigma\to0$ to expand in. A hole's contact is a null caustic in a spacetime that is not
conformally flat, and there the coupling cancels outright, since $k\cdot k=0$ drops the metric
terms and the two surviving derivative terms are equal and opposite at leading order, leaving
$(1-2\xi)(-X)-2\xi X=-X$. A.18's tower agrees: five couplings and four masses return one
coefficient, $-0.0592$, with a spread of $10^{-5}$ and no zero at $\xi=1/6$. So a massless
conformal field does feel a contact divergence at a hole. Two derivatives on A.19's own amplitude
give $T_{kk}=-3.19\times10^{-3}M^{-1/2}(M-r)^{-7/2}$, and against the same $11.5138/r_h^2$ that
leaves a floor under the shell, $(9.85\times10^{-3})^{2/7}\ell_P^{4/7}r_h^{3/7}$, which is
$1.1\times10^{-19}$ m at a solar mass and $7.8\times10^{-16}$ m at a billion. Mass still decides every shell that matters, beating the floor's term by $3\times10^{46}$ at
the fold's own fermion, but it no longer decides whether there is one.

The transport along the contact curve matters in its own right, since it fixes how the fold acts there. A.15's contact curve is the $E=0$ null geodesic, and $E=f\,dt/d\lambda=0$ makes $t$ constant, which makes $V/U$
constant, so in Kruskal the curve is a straight ray through the origin, passing through the
bifurcation surface where the fold acts and carried to its image by $\lambda\to-\lambda$. The
contact boundary sits at $UV=\sqrt e/2=0.824361$ and the ray turns the angle by $\pi$ to
$1.3\times10^{-13}$, reproducing the contact condition rather than assuming it. The Kruskal
connection coefficients each carry a factor of $U$ or $V$, so they vanish at the crossing and are
odd along the ray; the transport from $x$ to $\Theta x$ is therefore the identity, while
transporting only as far as the bifurcation surface departs from it by $1.04$, which is what
distinguishes a cancellation from an integrator doing nothing. So $P=d\Theta$, with one $+1$
eigenvalue in the angular direction and $-1$ on both Kruskal directions and on the polar angle.

So the sign at a black hole is open. A.15's conjecture has one exact positive datum, the flat
inversion, which is the fold's own fold at a Rindler horizon and which carries a positive
density at both couplings, and it has no argument that carries that sign to a hole.

An exponent is a different question and it does transfer, because it depends on the geometry
alone. The image two-point function has a single curvature input, the Hadamard amplitude $\Delta^{1/2}(x,\Theta x)/4\pi^2\sigma$, whose Van Vleck
determinant is unity in flat space and infinite at a conjugate point. The exponent is therefore
fixed by how many transverse directions refocus at contact, and both that count and its reason are
available in closed form.

Inside the horizon $\dot r^2=E^2+L^2(2M/r-1)/r^2>0$ whenever $E\neq0$, so $r$ runs monotonically
unless $E$ vanishes. Since the fold preserves $UV$ it preserves $r$, which puts $x$ and $\Theta x$
at one radius, leaving the $E=0$ geodesic as the only curve that can return to the radius it left.
So the connecting geodesic is unique, and $dr/d\varphi=\pm\sqrt{r(2M-r)}$ integrates to

$$
r(\varphi)=M\,(1+\sin\varphi),\qquad \varphi\in[0,\pi],
$$

which leaves $r=M$, grazes the bifurcation surface at $\varphi=\pi/2$, returns to $r=M$ and turns
through exactly $\pi$. The contact boundary comes out of that: it is the radius at which the
geodesic returning to its own radius turns through the angle the antipode asks for.

Both transverse directions carry a Killing field, and a Killing field restricted to a geodesic is a
Jacobi field. The rotation about the axis through $x$ is $-\sin\varphi\,\partial_\theta$ on the
equator, so in the parallel frame $\partial_\theta/r$ the focusing Jacobi field is
$M(1+\sin\varphi)\sin\varphi$, which already carries the initial conditions the Van Vleck
determinant asks for, and which vanishes at $\varphi=\pi$. A rotation vanishes on its own axis,
and the axis through $x$ runs through the antipodal direction too, so in any spherically symmetric
spacetime a geodesic turning through exactly $\pi$ ends on a point conjugate to its start.
Every connecting geodesic here turns through $\pi$, since that is where the fold puts the image,
so **the fold's image sits at a conjugate point at every radius**. Solving the two-point problem in
Kruskal and continuing it in $r$ from the null solution shows what the boundary picks out instead.
The world function $\sigma(x,\Theta x)$ is negative inside, positive outside, and crosses zero at
$r=M$ with

> $$
> \sigma\;\longrightarrow\;(3\pi+8)\,M\,(r-M),
> $$

a linear zero whose slope is twice the affine length $3\pi/2+4$ of the contact geodesic itself,
reproduced to $1.1\times10^{-9}$ with a worst shooting residual of $2.1\times10^{-14}$ over the
continuation. So conjugacy holds everywhere on the family and contact is the surface where the
caustic reaches the light cone, which is also where the pair stops being unrelated and becomes
causally related. The fold puts its own image on a caustic wherever it acts, which is what entitled
A.18's model geometry to the caustic it was built around.

Nothing in that uses the metric, so it should survive charge, and it does. Substituting
$J=r\sin\varphi$ into the Jacobi equation fixes the tidal in closed form for any static
spherically symmetric $f$,

> $$
> T_\theta=\frac{L^2}{r^4}\Big(1-f+\tfrac12 rf'\Big),
> $$

which is $3ML^2/r^5$ at $f=1-2M/r$ and $L^2(3Mr-2Q^2)/r^6$ with charge, and which matches a
numerically built Riemann tensor to $3\times10^{-8}$ at three charges. Integrating the geodesic and
reading the tidal off that tensor rather than off the formula, $r\sin\varphi$ solves the equation
along the whole curve. The contact radius stays at $(r_++r_-)/2=M$ at every charge while the area
ratio $A_c/A_+$ runs from $0.25$ to $0.77$, which is why the turning statement is the invariant one
and the quarter of an area is not. Dimension generalises the same way and fixes the caustic's
order: in $D$ dimensions the transverse space has $D-2$ directions, one of them $\partial_t$ and
$D-3$ of them sphere directions each carrying a rotation that vanishes at both ends, so **the
caustic order is $D-3$**, which is one in four.

The order of that caustic separates the two exponents, and emptiness fixes it. Expanding the
identity in a null frame leaves $R_{ab}k^ak^b$ equal to the trace of the transverse tidal matrix,
the $k$ and $n$ terms dropping by Riemann's antisymmetry. Schwarzschild is vacuum, so that matrix
is traceless, $\mathrm{diag}(+\Psi,-\Psi)$ with $\Psi=3ML^2/r^5$, one direction focusing and one
spreading, and the Jacobi determinant can vanish through a single factor. A vacuum black hole has
no point caustic available to it. The Einstein static universe has one, carrying
$R_{ab}k^ak^b=(2/a^2)(k^t)^2>0$, both directions focusing and an antipode conjugate at order two.
That is the whole difference between the two answers. Order two gives this appendix's
$\delta^{-2}$ in the two-point function and $\delta^{-4}$ in the stress; order one gives A.18's
$\delta^{-3/2}$ and $\delta^{-7/2}$.

So A.18's exponent is the one a black hole carries, and it now has a reason behind it. The obstacle
A.18 named was that its geometry is not Schwarzschild. The caustic order is the only feature of the
geometry the exponent depends on, and that order agrees in the two, forced on the black hole by the
Ricci term in Raychaudhuri. A.18's fields are massive, which by the paragraphs above is the
condition for feeling the image term at all, so the two halves fit.

An amplitude comes out of the same two Jacobi fields:
$dJ/d\lambda=-L/M$ at contact exactly, the spreading partner integrates to $47.561945$ against an
affine length $3\pi/2+4=8.712389$, and $\Delta\to0.183180\,\lambda_{\rm
tot}/(\lambda_{\rm tot}-\lambda)$, a ratio left alone by the rescaling of the affine parameter
that $L$ performs. The tidal strength behind all of it was read off a Riemann tensor built
numerically and checked first against $R_{ab}=0$ and the Kretschmann scalar $48M^2/r^6$, and the
two Jacobi fields satisfy their branch equations to $9\times10^{-16}$.

The exponent itself follows from the caustic's order. A Green function's generic light-cone
singularity is $\delta^{-(D-2)/2}$, and each direction that refocuses on the connecting family
steepens it by half a power, giving an exponent $-(D-2+n)/2$ for the image term and two powers
further for a stress quadratic in derivatives. In four dimensions that reads $-(1+n/2)$.

Five points fix that without being fitted to it, three of them exact. A whole family,
$\mathbb{R}\times S^N$, turns out to be solvable, because conformal coupling in $D=N+1$ makes
$\omega_n=[n+(N-1)/2]/a$ equally spaced and the degeneracy cancels the frequency, leaving a
binomial series that closes on

> $$
> G_{\rm img}=\frac{a^{1-N}}{2\lambda\,2^{2\lambda}\,\mathrm{Vol}(S^N)\,\cos^{2\lambda}(\eta/2)},
> \qquad \lambda=\tfrac{N-1}{2},
> $$

whose $N=3$ member is the Einstein static universe above, which is how the family was found, and
whose exponent is $-(N-1)$ at every $N$. The Abel-summed modes match that to $8\times10^{-4}$
relative over twelve points, and the exponents land on $-(D-2+n)/2$ at $(D,n)=(3,1)$, $(4,2)$,
$(5,3)$, $(6,4)$ and $(7,5)$. The two measured points are A.18's: its control pair with no caustic
returns $-1.0095$ against $-1$, and its image pair returns $-1.49874$ against $-3/2$. With $n=D-3$
for a spherically symmetric hole the four-dimensional answer is $-3/2$ and $-7/2$, which is A.18's
measurement, so the transfer to Schwarzschild rests on the two configurations sharing a degeneracy.

The amplitude is the same statement seen in the proper-time representation, where
$G=\int_0^\infty ds\,(4\pi s)^{-D/2}\Delta^{1/2}e^{-\sigma/2s}$ and a degenerate family replaces
the infinite $\Delta^{1/2}$ by a finite $c_n s^{-n/2}$, which is precisely what shifts the
exponent. On the sphere family $c_n=L^n\pi^{n/2}4\pi/(n!\,\mathrm{Vol}(S^{n+1}))$, and a
Lorentzian Green function and a Riemannian heat kernel at the antipode agree on it to
$3\times10^{-6}$ at $n=1,2,3,4$. Since the heat kernel of a metric product factorises and a flat
factor carries $\Delta=1$, the amplitude belongs to the degenerate family alone, so A.18's
$\mathbb{R}^2\times S^2$ has $S^2$'s value, $c_1=\pi^{3/2}a$, in the $D=4$, $n=1$ case a hole is
also in.

That expression references the volume of a sphere one dimension above the family, which a local
caustic cannot know about, so it wants rearranging. Rearranged it is $c_n=L^n\,2\Gamma(n/2+1)/n!$, exact at all four, or equivalently

> $$
> c_n=V_{\rm fam}\left(\frac{L}{2\sqrt\pi}\right)^{\!n},
> $$

with $V_{\rm fam}=\mathrm{Vol}(S^n)$ running over $2\pi$, $4\pi$, $2\pi^2$ and $8\pi^2/3$, so the
linear dependence on the family's volume is tested rather than assumed: any other power of it
misses by $80\%$ or more.

Whether the length there is the geodesic's, or some other scale of the Jacobi profile that happens
to agree with it on a sphere, cannot be settled on a symmetric space. The curvature operator is
parallel along a geodesic in one, so every Jacobi field is a pure sine and every candidate scale
coincides; complex projective space gives $\sin(2u)/2$ at its cut locus, which is still a sine. A
surface of revolution $du^2+f(u)^2d\varphi^2$ does settle it. Its meridians are a one-parameter
family of volume $2\pi$ whose Jacobi field is $f$ itself, and $f=a[\sin\theta+\epsilon h(\theta)]$
with $h$ odd at both poles moves the profile while $L$ and $V_{\rm fam}$ stay where they are.
Taking the ratio of the perturbed pole-to-pole heat kernel to the round one at fixed proper time,
which cancels the solver's bias, that ratio extrapolates to $1$ for six perturbations pushing in
both directions, where a profile-dependent amplitude would need between $0.73$ and $1.50$. **The
amplitude is the geodesic length and the family's volume and nothing else.**

On the contact geodesic $V_{\rm fam}=2\pi$ as well, a circle of planes through the axis, and the
tidal matrix is exactly $\mathrm{diag}(+\Psi,-\Psi)$ in a parallel-propagated frame, so the
Gaussian integral factorises and the single non-degenerate transverse direction contributes the
$0.183180$ above multiplicatively rather than by assumption.

The affine parameter is not that length, and a plane wave shows why while confirming the exponent
on a genuinely null caustic. Take $ds^2=2\,du\,dv+A_{ij}x^ix^jdu^2+dx^2+dy^2$ with $A$ traceless,
which is vacuum and so carries one focusing transverse direction and one spreading, exactly the
contact geodesic's structure. Fourier in $v$ turns the wave equation into a Schr\"odinger equation
in $u$ with an oscillator of frequency $\omega$ in one transverse direction and an inverted one in
the other, so the propagator is Mehler's, the caustic sits at $\omega U=\pi$, and the amplitude
goes as $(\pi-\omega U)^{-1/2}$: the same half power, exactly, on a null vacuum caustic. What it
supplies no candidate for is a length, since under $u\to cu$, $v\to v/c$ the metric keeps its form
with $\omega\to\omega/c$ and $U\to cU$, so $1/\omega$ and $U$ carry the dimension of $u$.

Two measurements fix which length the rule uses. It is not a functional of the curvature along the
geodesic: across the surfaces of revolution above the Gaussian curvature $-f''/f$ moves by up to
$254$ per cent and changes sign, while $L$ and $V_{\rm fam}$ stay put and the amplitude does not
move. And it is not the geodesic's own length: putting the caustic in an $S^2$ and letting the
geodesic also run along a flat direction holds the projection at $\pi a$ while the geodesic's own
length grows as $\sqrt{\Delta u^2+(\pi a)^2}$, and over a $38$ per cent change in that, in one, two
and three flat dimensions, the amplitude holds to the fifth decimal while tracking the sphere's
radius exactly when that is changed instead. In the Lorentzian version the pair sits on the light
cone, where the geodesic's own length is zero and the amplitude is unchanged.

What it is, then, is the arc length of the geodesic's projection onto the factor the caustic lives
in. On $\mathbb{R}^2\times S^2$, which is A.18's own geometry, the curve covers $\pi$ of the sphere,
the projection is $\pi a$, and $\sqrt\pi\cdot\pi a=\pi^{3/2}a$ exactly, giving that model a
closed-form amplitude. A black hole carries the same transverse sphere with a varying
radius, so the same quantity reads $\int r\,d\varphi$, which along $r=M(1+\sin\varphi)$ is
$M(\pi+2)$.

It has a second reading, and the two turn out to be one integral.
Inside the horizon $r$ is timelike, so the geodesic sweeps a proper extent
$\int d\tau=\int dr/\sqrt{2M/r-1}$ climbing to the bifurcation surface and returning, and
$dr/d\varphi=\sqrt{r(2M-r)}$ makes $r\,d\varphi=\sqrt{r/(2M-r)}\,dr=d\tau$ term by term. Both come
to $M(\pi+2)=5.141593M$, and neither mentions the affine parameter. With the reduced Van Vleck
$0.183180$ from the spreading direction,

> $$
> c_1=\sqrt\pi\,M(\pi+2)=9.1132\,M,\qquad
> \Delta^{1/2}\;\longrightarrow\;3.90\,M\,s^{-1/2}.
> $$

The sign follows from the one exactly solvable caustic. On the Einstein static universe the
divergent stress is $T_{kk}=(1-6\xi)(1-\cos\eta)/8\pi^2a^4(1+\cos\eta)^2$ while the Hadamard log
coefficient is $V_0=\Delta^{1/2}[m^2+(\xi-\tfrac16)R]/2$, and the two carry the same factor with
opposite signs, so the caustic-divergent stress goes as $-V_0$. Schwarzschild is Ricci-flat, which
shuts the $(\xi-\tfrac16)R$ channel whatever the coupling and leaves $V_0=m^2\Delta^{1/2}/2>0$ for
any field that is not massless. So $T_{kk}<0$ at the contact surface. Integrating Raychaudhuri, a
negative $R_{kk}$ pushes the conjugate point away and away entirely: at $R_{kk}=0.5$, $0.2$ and
$0.05$ it sits at $6.28$, $9.94$ and $19.87$, and at zero or below there is none. Contact requires
a conjugate point at the antipodal angle, so delaying it makes contact harder. **A.15's divergence
closes the region rather than marking it**, and it does so for the fields that exist rather than
for a chosen coupling. The sign is calibrated on that one exactly solvable caustic.

The shape either side of the boundary follows from the sign of $\sigma$ and needs nothing further.
Outside the horizon the pair is spacelike too, so the term does not stop there: it is suppressed
rather than absent, exponentially in the mass for anything heavy and quadratically for anything
light, with the worst case across the whole field content thirty-four orders below the dark
energy. Section 3's silence is about the commutator and does not reach the stress tensor, which
§6.1 says in its own words. Between the horizon and $r=M$ the pair is spacelike and the term is finite and real.
At $r=M$ it diverges as $\delta^{-3/2}$ with the coefficient above. Inside, $\sigma$ is negative
and $\sigma^{-3/2}$ continues to $i|\sigma|^{-3/2}$, so the image term is imaginary there: **the
contact region carries a decay rate**, which is pair creation, and it arrives from the causal
structure rather than from any added assumption.

One thing about that treatment: the obvious tool is the wrong one. At a
generic fold caustic the action has a cubic stationary point in the family parameter and the
uniform replacement for the failed parametrix is an Airy function. Here the degenerate direction is
generated by a rotation Killing field, so every member of the family is an isometric image of every
other and the action is exactly flat along it: the affine length is the same at all nine rotations
sampled, to $10^{-12}$. A symmetry degeneracy is not resolved by a cubic term, and integrating the
orbit exactly is what replaces it. The power that follows can be checked against the exponent rule
without doing the integral, since the Riemannian $\Delta^{1/2}\to c_ns^{-n/2}$ reads in the
Lorentzian propagator as one extra half power of the null momentum per degenerate direction, and
that reproduces $-(D-2+n)/2$ at $n=0,1,2,3$, in agreement with the amplitude above reached the
other way. The sign of the resulting stress still needs A.18's sum redone on the Schwarzschild
contact geodesic, which is now possible in a way it was not when A.18 was written, since that
geodesic is the closed-form curve above rather than an unknown.


## Acknowledgements

The cosmology paper this accompanies carries the thanks; they hold here too. The author is
responsible for every claim and decision in this paper. Literature search, grammar, proof
reading, and automated tests, cross-checks, code revisions and sanity checks used contemporary
research tools, large language models among them. No such tool is an author. Every number here
is produced by a script in the release, and each check over those scripts was validated by
planting an error it had to catch, since a check that matches nothing reports success.

## Code and data availability

Every quantitative claim in this paper is produced by a script in the release, and the release
carries a table, `CLAIMS.tsv`, mapping each claim to the file that produces it. The code is at
<https://github.com/BenWiseman/separate-ways>, and this paper's Zenodo record is
<https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.23030633>, the concept DOI, which always resolves to the most
recent version. The cosmology paper it accompanies is at
<https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.22888119>. The scripts specific to this paper are
the 169 calculation files and the 21 figure generators, each printing its own results and its
own limits when run with no arguments. Of those, 147 calculations and all 21 generators are R and
load no package, so base R is enough; the other 22 calculations are Python, three using the
standard library alone and the rest importing numpy, scipy, sympy or mpmath at the versions the
release records. 15 of those generators draw the figures here, and each re-derives and
asserts the numbers its own caption states. Several plant deliberate failures and stop if the
check does not catch them, because a check that cannot fail is worse than none. Two further files
are shared with the cosmology paper and predate this one.

Ten of those files stand behind no claim above, and deliberately. Three are the checkers that
guard the manuscript rather than results in it. Four are superseded, and each prints why and what
replaced it, first and last, when run: a header comment is not enough when the output is what a
reader sees. One adjudicates an external model's claims rather than making any. One supports a
result in the cosmology paper. And one assesses outlier detection as the instrument for a rare
contaminating minority, which is the right instrument for the falsifier Section 6.2
withdraws, and it is kept as the record of a quantified test.

Two files in the same release carry what is not settled. `REFEREE_OPEN.md` lists the open
objections and what was done to each, including the ones that went against the paper.
`PROVENANCE_GAPS.md` records every number that was once stated without a script behind it, what
closing it found, and the four figures that had to be replaced because they sat below the
arithmetic floor of the method that produced them.
